Chapter 8 Does Psychotherapy Work? Research, Common Factors and the Therapeutic Relationship

In this chapter
  1. 8.1 Eysenck's Challenge: Does Psychotherapy Work at All?: explain why improvement after therapy is not proof that therapy works, describe Eysenck's 1952 review and its claim of spontaneous remission, and list the methodological replies to it.
  2. 8.2 Meta-Analysis: Measuring How Much Therapy Helps: explain how a meta-analysis and an effect size work, and state the findings of Smith and Glass (1977), Smith, Glass and Miller (1980) and Lambert's later estimates.
  3. 8.3 The Dodo Bird Verdict and the Specific-Effects Debate: trace the dodo bird verdict from Rosenzweig (1936) to Luborsky and Wampold, and weigh it against the evidence for specific effects.
  4. 8.4 Common Factors: Frank, Lambert's Pie and Wampold's Contextual Model: describe Jerome Frank's four shared features of healing, Lambert's estimates of what produces change, and the contextual model.
  5. 8.5 The Therapeutic Alliance and the Core Conditions: define Bordin's working alliance, explain how it is measured and what it predicts, describe ruptures and repair, and evaluate Rogers' core conditions as evidence.
  6. 8.6 Therapist Effects, Allegiance Effects and Client Variables: explain therapist and allegiance effects, describe which client characteristics predict benefit (including the YAVIS client), and explain the hello-goodbye effect.
  7. 8.7 Testing a Treatment: Efficacy, Effectiveness and the Randomised Controlled Trial: distinguish efficacy from effectiveness and analogue research, and describe the design of a randomised controlled trial, its control conditions and its safeguards.
  8. 8.8 Landmark Trials, Empirically Supported Treatments and Evidence-Based Practice: describe the NIMH depression trial and Project MATCH, state the Chambless criteria for empirically supported treatments, and define evidence-based practice in psychology.
  9. 8.9 How Much Therapy, How Much Change: Dose, Clinical Significance and Deterioration: describe the dose-effect relationship, calculate and interpret the reliable change index, and explain how and why some clients get worse.
  10. 8.10 Inside the Therapy Room: Process Research, the Course of Therapy and Its Ending: describe what process research studies, the stages of therapy, termination and premature dropout, therapist self-disclosure and homework.
  11. 8.11 Combining and Adapting Therapies: Integration, Culture and Therapy in India: distinguish the four routes to psychotherapy integration, explain cultural adaptation, and describe Indian contributions such as the guru-chela paradigm.
  12. 8.12 Becoming a Therapist: Training, Supervision, the Wounded Healer and Self-Care: describe the main models of clinical supervision, the idea of the wounded healer, and the causes, signs and prevention of burnout.

Weightage. 20 of the 346 questions mapped in this book (5.8%) test this chapter, 15 of them from real past papers. 2 of its 12 sections are High yield.

In 1952 a psychologist in his mid-thirties, working at the Maudsley Hospital in south London, published a paper of barely six pages that made a great many therapists very angry. His name was Hans Eysenck. Born in Berlin, he had left Germany as a young man rather than live under the Nazis, trained in psychology at University College London, and in 1950 had been put in charge of psychology at the new Institute of Psychiatry attached to the Maudsley. He was clever, combative and suspicious of anything that could not be measured, and he had a particular suspicion: that the talking cures which had spread across the Western world since Freud had never actually been shown to cure anybody.

So he went looking for the evidence. He gathered the published reports in which therapists had recorded how many of their neurotic patients improved. Patients treated by psychoanalysis, he calculated, improved at a rate of about 44 percent; patients treated by the looser "eclectic" therapies of the day improved at about 64 percent. Then he asked the question nobody had bothered to ask: how many would have improved anyway? For an answer he turned to two groups who had received no psychotherapy at all: neurotic patients in New York state hospitals, who received only custodial care, and 500 people who had claimed disability insurance for neurotic illness and had been treated by their family doctors with reassurance, sedatives and advice. About 72 percent of the second group were back at work and functioning within two years.

Eysenck's conclusion was blunt: "roughly two-thirds of a group of neurotic patients will recover or improve to a marked extent within about two years of the onset of their illness, whether they are treated by means of psychotherapy or not." If psychotherapy could not beat the passage of time, what exactly were patients paying for? The reaction was furious. Psychotherapy was booming in the years after the Second World War, and the people who practised it did not take kindly to a young research psychologist telling them that their work was no better than waiting. Replies and rebuttals filled the journals for the next quarter of a century. Eysenck never backed down; a decade later he restated the case even more sharply.

This chapter is the story of how psychology answered Eysenck, and of what the answer turned up along the way. The answer to his question, it turns out, is yes: psychotherapy works, and works rather well. But the evidence that settled the question opened a harder one. If dozens of therapies built on contradictory theories all work about equally well, what is it that actually heals? The technique, the relationship, the hope, the client, or the particular therapist? Remember Eysenck's two-thirds; you will meet it again at the end of the chapter.

8.1 Eysenck's Challenge: Does Psychotherapy Work at All?Lower yield · not asked yet

The earlier chapters of this book described a crowded marketplace of therapies: psychoanalysis and its descendants, behaviour therapy, the cognitive therapies, the humanistic and existential therapies, family and group work. Each comes with a theory of why people suffer and a set of techniques for helping them. Each also comes with enthusiastic practitioners who can tell you about clients they have helped. This chapter steps back and asks the questions a sceptical outsider would ask. Does any of it work? How do we know? If it works, why? And what does the answer mean for the way a clinical psychologist should practise?

Research that asks whether, and how much, clients improve as a result of treatment is called outcome research. Research that asks what happens inside therapy sessions, and how those events produce change, is called process research. Most of this chapter is about outcome research, because that is where the story began; process research returns in Section 8.10.

What would count as proof?

Imagine Ravi, a 22-year-old engineering student in Mysuru, who begins having panic attacks in the run-up to his final examinations. His parents take him to a psychologist, he attends ten sessions over three months, and by the end he is sleeping well, has passed his exams and has not had an attack for six weeks. Did the therapy work?

It is tempting to say yes, but think about everything else that happened in those three months. The examinations ended, and with them much of the pressure. Ravi's family rallied round. He may have read about panic on the internet and realised he was not dying. And there is a statistical trap as well: people tend to seek help when they are at their worst, and anything measured at its most extreme point is likely to be closer to average the next time it is measured, simply because extreme states do not last. This tendency is called regression to the mean, and it guarantees that a group of people who start therapy at their lowest point will look better a few months later even if the therapy does nothing at all.

Add to these the tendency of many psychological problems to ease with time, life events and ordinary support from friends and family, which is called spontaneous remission. The word "spontaneous" is a little misleading: the improvement has causes, but they lie outside formal treatment. The only way to separate the effect of therapy from time, regression, life events and support is to compare people who receive therapy with similar people who do not. Before 1952, almost nobody had done that.

There is one more difficulty, and it is easy to overlook. What counts as "better"? Hans H. Strupp (1921 to 2006) and Suzanne W. Hadley pointed out in 1977 that outcome can be judged from three standpoints, which they called the tripartite model of outcome. Society (the family, the employer, the college) judges by behaviour: is Ravi attending classes and doing his work? The client judges by inner experience: does he feel less frightened and more content? The mental health professional judges by underlying structure: has the vulnerability that produced the panic changed? The three can disagree. A man who stops drinking may satisfy his wife and employer while feeling miserable; a woman who leaves an abusive marriage may feel freer while her family regards the outcome as a disaster. Every outcome study has to decide whose judgement, and which measures, will count.

Remember

Improvement after therapy is not proof that therapy caused it. The rival explanations are time and spontaneous remission, regression to the mean, life events, support from others, and expectancy (the placebo effect, Section 8.4). Only a comparison with similar untreated or differently treated people can rule them out.

The 1952 review

Hans Jürgen Eysenck (1916 to 1997) is better known to you from the Personality book, where his dimensions of extraversion, neuroticism and later psychoticism appear. But he was also the most important critic psychotherapy ever had. He believed clinical psychologists should be applied scientists who measured and experimented, and for some years he argued that they should not practise psychotherapy at all, because nobody had shown that it worked. His 1952 paper in the Journal of Consulting Psychology, "The effects of psychotherapy: an evaluation", set out to test that belief.

His method was simple. He took the published reports of treated neurotic patients and pooled the percentages that therapists had classified as cured, much improved or improved. He then needed a baseline: an estimate of how many comparable people would improve without psychotherapy. He used two sources. The first was data compiled by Carney Landis (1897 to 1962) on neurotic patients discharged from New York state mental hospitals, where they had received custodial care but no psychotherapy. The second was a follow-up by Peter G. Denker, published in 1946, of 500 people who had claimed disability insurance for psychoneurosis and had been treated only by their general practitioners. Denker found that about 72 percent had recovered (judged by their return to work, their having no more than slight complaints and a successful social adjustment) within two years, and about 90 percent within five. Table 8.1 sets out the comparison Eysenck made.

Table 8.1 The comparison at the heart of Eysenck's 1952 review.
GroupTreatment receivedImproved
Psychoanalytic casesPsychoanalysis (patients who broke off treatment counted as failures)About 44 percent
Eclectic casesMixed, non-analytic psychotherapyAbout 64 percent
Insurance claimants (Denker)General practitioner care: reassurance, sedatives, adviceAbout 72 percent within two years
State hospital patients (Landis)Custodial care, no psychotherapyA similar proportion (about 72 percent) discharged as recovered or improved

The numbers seemed to speak for themselves. The untreated groups did at least as well as the treated ones; if anything, psychoanalysis appeared to do worse than nothing. Eysenck's conclusion that roughly two-thirds of neurotic patients improve within two years "whether they are treated by means of psychotherapy or not" became one of the most quoted sentences in the history of clinical psychology. Notice exactly what he claimed. He did not say that psychotherapy harms people. He said that the published figures "fail to support the hypothesis" that psychotherapy speeds recovery beyond the rate of spontaneous remission. In 1965, and in a 1966 book called The Effects of Psychotherapy, he repeated the argument with more studies, adding that the only treatment with good evidence in its favour was the new behaviour therapy, which he had championed from the Maudsley (Chapter 4).

Key study: Eysenck's "The effects of psychotherapy: an evaluation" (1952)

Question: Do neurotic patients who receive psychotherapy improve more than those who do not? Method: A review of published reports of treated patients, compared with two untreated baselines (Landis's state hospital data and Denker's 500 insurance claimants treated by family doctors). Result: Psychoanalysis about 44 percent improved, eclectic therapy about 64 percent, untreated about 72 percent within two years. Conclusion: About two-thirds of neurotic patients improve within two years with or without psychotherapy, through spontaneous remission. Criticism: The groups were not comparable, the criteria for improvement differed from study to study, the "untreated" patients were in fact treated, and dropouts from psychoanalysis were counted as failures.

Exam trap

Eysenck's claim was that therapy adds nothing beyond spontaneous remission (about two-thirds improve within two years anyway), not that therapy is harmful and not that only behaviour therapy was tested. The 1952 paper is in the Journal of Consulting Psychology. Spontaneous remission means improvement without formal treatment; it is not the same thing as the placebo effect, which is improvement caused by the expectation of help (Section 8.4).

The replies: what was wrong with the comparison?

The first replies came within two years. Saul Rosenzweig (1907 to 2004), whom you will meet again as the originator of the dodo bird verdict, published "A transvaluation of psychotherapy: a reply to Hans Eysenck" in 1954, and Lester Luborsky (1920 to 2009) published a critical note the same year. Over the following decades the critics assembled a long list of problems. It is worth working through them, because every one of them is a lesson in how not to do outcome research.

  • The groups were not comparable. Insurance claimants were working people with jobs to return to, probably with milder and more situational problems than patients who sought out psychoanalysis. Hospital patients were different again. Comparing percentages from such different groups is like comparing the pass rates of two colleges without asking who was admitted.
  • "Improved" meant different things. For Denker, improvement meant returning to work and carrying on with few or no complaints. For a psychoanalyst, it meant a lasting change in the patient's personality and conflicts. A patient could easily meet the first standard while failing the second.
  • The "untreated" were treated. Denker's patients saw their doctors regularly and received reassurance, advice, encouragement and sedatives. That is not nothing; it contains several of the very ingredients that psychotherapy offers.
  • Dropouts were counted as failures. Eysenck counted patients who broke off psychoanalysis as unimproved. If you count only those who completed treatment, the psychoanalytic improvement rate rises considerably.
  • The time frames differed. Psychoanalysis typically lasted years; the baseline was recovery within two years of the onset of illness.

The most influential re-examination came from Allen E. Bergin (1934 to 2024), who in 1971, in the first edition of the Handbook of Psychotherapy and Behavior Change that he edited with Sol Garfield, went back to the very studies Eysenck had used. By making different, but defensible, decisions about dropouts and about what counted as improvement, Bergin showed that the improvement rate for psychoanalysis could be put above 80 percent. He also reviewed studies of untreated neurotic patients and argued that spontaneous remission was much lower than two-thirds, around 30 percent.

Then, in 1976, Michael J. Lambert published a careful review of spontaneous remission in adult neurotic disorders. Across the studies he could find, the median rate of improvement without formal treatment was about 43 percent, with a very wide range from study to study and from disorder to disorder. Spontaneous remission is real, Lambert concluded, but it is nowhere near as large or as uniform as Eysenck had claimed, and it varies with the problem: some conditions (mild, recent, situational distress) often lift by themselves, while others (long-standing obsessions, entrenched personality problems) rarely do. Lambert's later summaries drew the practical lesson: people do improve on their own, but therapy helps more people improve, and helps them improve faster.

Exam trap

Know whose number is whose. Eysenck (1952): about two-thirds improve without therapy. Bergin (1971): spontaneous remission nearer 30 percent, and psychoanalysis above 80 percent once dropouts and criteria are handled differently. Lambert (1976): median spontaneous remission about 43 percent, varying widely by disorder. The first critical replies were by Rosenzweig and Luborsky in 1954.

Why the challenge mattered

It would be easy to dismiss Eysenck as simply wrong, but that would miss his real contribution. He was right that the evidence of 1952 was worthless: case reports from enthusiastic therapists, with no comparison groups, prove nothing. By provoking the field, he forced it to do proper research. He also used the argument to make room for behaviour therapy, which he promoted through the journal Behaviour Research and Therapy, which he founded in 1963, and through the work of his Maudsley colleagues (Chapter 4).

Through the 1960s and early 1970s, reviewers tried to settle the question by counting. They gathered controlled studies and tallied how many showed therapy beating a control group, how many showed no difference and how many showed the control group ahead. This kind of review is called a box-score review (or vote-counting review). The box scores mostly favoured therapy; one large review by Julian Meltzoff and Melvin Kornreich in 1970 found that most adequately controlled studies came out positive. But Eysenck could always reply that the positive studies were badly designed, and there was no agreed way to weigh a large, careful study against a small, sloppy one.

The research of these years also produced two warnings that shaped everything after it. The first came from Bergin, who noticed in 1966 that treated groups often spread out more by the end of therapy than control groups did: some treated clients improved a great deal, but some got worse. An average improvement could hide real harm to a minority, which Bergin called the deterioration effect (Section 8.9). The second came from Donald J. Kiesler (1933 to 2007), who argued in 1966 that research was crippled by two uniformity myths: the patient uniformity myth, the assumption that patients entering a study are alike enough to be treated as one group, and the therapist uniformity myth, the assumption that therapists delivering a treatment are interchangeable. Both assumptions, as you will see in Section 8.6, are false.

The year after Kiesler, Gordon L. Paul put the new agenda into a single sentence that every psychotherapy researcher still quotes. The question, he wrote in 1967, is not "Does therapy work?" but "What treatment, by whom, is most effective for this individual with that specific problem, and under which set of circumstances?" Paul's question is the map for the rest of this chapter: treatment (Sections 8.3, 8.7 and 8.8), by whom (Section 8.6), for which individual (Section 8.6), with which problem (Section 8.8), under which circumstances (Sections 8.10 and 8.11).

Clinical link

In an Indian clinic you will often be asked, sometimes by a sceptical father who has paid for the bus fare, "Will talking really help? Won't he get better on his own once the exams are over?" Eysenck's question is a fair one, and you should be able to answer it honestly: many problems do ease with time, but controlled research shows that therapy helps more people, faster, and reduces the chance of relapse for several common conditions. Knowing the evidence lets you give hope without overselling.

8.2 Meta-Analysis: Measuring How Much Therapy HelpsLower yield · not asked yet

By the mid-1970s there were several hundred controlled studies of psychotherapy, and no agreed way of summarising them. Box scores could be argued about forever. What was needed was a way to put every study on the same scale, so that results could be added up like coins of a single currency. The tool that did this was invented by an educational statistician, and its first big test was psychotherapy.

From counting votes to measuring size

Box-score reviews have three weaknesses. First, they ignore how big an effect is: a study in which therapy produced a huge improvement and a study in which it produced a trivial one both count as one "win". Second, they confuse a non-significant result with a negative one: a small study may find a real effect that fails to reach statistical significance only because the sample was too small, and the box score counts it as a "tie". Third, every study gets one vote, whether it had 12 participants or 1,200. A better approach would measure the size of the effect in each study, in a common unit, and then combine those sizes, giving more weight to more precise studies.

That approach was named by Gene V. Glass (born 1940), who in his 1976 presidential address to the American Educational Research Association coined the term meta-analysis for "the analysis of analyses": the statistical combination of the results of many independent studies of the same question. The common unit he used is the effect size. In its usual form (often written d), the effect size is the difference between the mean of the treated group and the mean of the control group at the end of treatment, divided by the standard deviation (usually of the control group, or of both groups pooled):

d = (mean of treated group minus mean of control group) ÷ standard deviation

Dividing by the standard deviation is the clever step. It turns every result, whatever scale it was measured on (a depression inventory, a count of panic attacks, a therapist's rating), into the same unit: standard deviations. An effect size of 1.0 means the average treated person ended one standard deviation better off than the average untreated person. The statistician Jacob Cohen (1923 to 1998) suggested rough benchmarks that are still used: 0.2 is a small effect, 0.5 a medium effect and 0.8 a large effect.

An effect size can also be translated into a percentile, which is the way the famous psychotherapy findings are usually reported. If outcomes are normally distributed, the average treated client sits at the mean of the treated group, and we can ask what percentage of the untreated group scores worse than that. For d = 0.5 the answer is about 69 percent; for d = 0.68, about 75 percent; for d = 0.85, about 80 percent; for d = 1.0, about 84 percent. Figure 8.1 shows what this looks like.

A worked example makes the arithmetic concrete. Suppose a study of anxious college students uses an anxiety scale on which higher scores mean more anxiety. At the end of treatment the treated group averages 18 and the waiting-list group averages 24, and the standard deviation is 10. Because lower is better on this scale, the improvement is 24 minus 18, or 6 points, and d = 6 ÷ 10 = 0.6. The average treated student is now less anxious than about 73 percent of the untreated students. A second study, using a completely different questionnaire with a different range of scores, can be converted in the same way, and the two effect sizes can then be averaged.

In outline, a meta-analysis proceeds in a fixed sequence. The researcher states the question precisely (for example, "Does psychotherapy reduce symptoms in adults compared with no treatment?"); searches exhaustively for every relevant study, published and unpublished; applies stated inclusion criteria; codes the features of each study (type of therapy, type of client, length of treatment, quality of design, who did the rating); calculates an effect size for each comparison; combines the effect sizes, usually weighting larger studies more heavily; and finally tests moderators, features of the studies that make the effect larger or smaller. That last step is where meta-analysis becomes more than a summary, because it lets a researcher ask whether behaviour therapy beats psychodynamic therapy, or whether experienced therapists do better than novices, across hundreds of studies at once.

Smith and Glass (1977) and Smith, Glass and Miller (1980)

Glass's first major application of the new method was to the very question Eysenck had raised, and the story of how it happened shows that science has personal motives too. Glass had been through psychotherapy himself and found it helpful, and he was irritated by Eysenck's claims. With Mary Lee Smith, he set out to gather every controlled study of psychotherapy he could find.

Key study: Smith and Glass, "Meta-analysis of psychotherapy outcome studies" (1977)

Question: Across all controlled studies, how much better off are treated clients than untreated ones, and do the types of therapy differ? Method: 375 controlled studies of psychotherapy and counselling, yielding over 800 effect sizes, each comparing a treated group with an untreated or differently treated control group. Result: The average effect size was 0.68: "the typical therapy client is better off than 75 percent of untreated individuals." Once differences in the studies themselves were taken into account, the various types of therapy differed little in their effects. Conclusion: Psychotherapy is effective, and its effect is substantial. Criticism: Eysenck and others objected that the analysis mixed good and bad studies and very different outcomes (see below).

Three years later Smith and Glass, now joined by Thomas I. Miller, published a fuller analysis in a book called The Benefits of Psychotherapy (1980). With 475 controlled studies, the average effect size rose to 0.85: the average treated client was better off than about 80 percent of the untreated controls. The book added several findings that later research would build on. The different families of therapy, once the studies were made comparable, still produced broadly similar effects. The experience of the therapist was not strongly related to outcome. Benefits were found across ages, diagnoses and settings, and although they faded somewhat over time, they had not disappeared at follow-up. The authors also compared psychotherapy with drug treatment and with the combination of the two, and found psychotherapy holding its own.

These were not small numbers. An effect size of 0.85 is "large" on Cohen's benchmarks, and larger than the effects of many accepted medical and educational interventions. For the first time, the answer to Eysenck's question rested not on a selected handful of studies but on nearly the whole research literature.

Exam trap

0.68 and 75 percent belong to Smith and Glass (1977, 375 studies, in American Psychologist). 0.85 and 80 percent belong to Smith, Glass and Miller (1980, 475 studies, The Benefits of Psychotherapy). On Cohen's benchmarks 0.85 is a large effect and 0.68 lies between medium and large. The term meta-analysis was coined by Glass (1976), not by Smith, Eysenck, Rosenthal or Cohen; Cohen supplied the small, medium and large benchmarks (0.2, 0.5, 0.8).

Criticisms of meta-analysis, and the replies

Eysenck's response to Smith and Glass was a one-page article in 1978 titled "An exercise in mega-silliness". His complaints, and those of other critics, have become the standard list of the dangers of meta-analysis, and you should know them by their nicknames.

  • Garbage in, garbage out. If many of the studies pooled are badly designed, averaging them does not produce a good answer; it produces a precise-looking average of bad answers.
  • Apples and oranges. Smith and Glass mixed very different therapies, clients and outcome measures (fear of snakes in volunteer students, depression in outpatients, grades in schoolchildren). Is an average of such different things meaningful?
  • The file-drawer problem. Studies with significant, positive results are more likely to be published than studies that find nothing; the null results stay in the researchers' file drawers. Robert Rosenthal (1933 to 2024) named this the file-drawer problem in 1979. A meta-analysis that includes only published studies will therefore overestimate the true effect. The general tendency is called publication bias.
  • Non-independence. One study may contribute many effect sizes (one for each outcome measure), giving it more weight than it deserves.

The replies were empirical, which is to say they used meta-analysis to test the criticisms. Glass and Smith had coded the quality of every study, and well-designed studies produced effect sizes about as large as poorly designed ones, which answers "garbage in, garbage out". To answer "apples and oranges", they pointed out that whether fruit can be averaged depends on the question: if the question is about fruit, apples and oranges both count, and if the question is about apples, one can analyse apples separately, which moderator analysis does. Rosenthal himself devised a way to estimate how many unpublished null studies would have to be hidden in file drawers to wipe out a result (the "fail-safe N"); for the psychotherapy literature the number is very large, far more than could plausibly exist. Independent re-analyses also checked the findings. In 1981 Gavin Andrews and Robin Harvey re-analysed the Smith, Glass and Miller data using only the studies of genuinely distressed neurotic patients who had sought treatment, and still found an average effect size of about 0.72. In 1982 Judith Landman and Robyn M. Dawes (1936 to 2010) re-examined a sample of the original studies with stricter criteria and published their conclusion in the title: Smith and Glass's conclusions "stand up under scrutiny".

Exam trap

"Garbage in, garbage out" is the complaint about study quality; "apples and oranges" is the complaint about mixing different things; the "file-drawer problem" (Rosenthal, 1979) is about unpublished null results. Eysenck's 1978 reply to Smith and Glass was titled "An exercise in mega-silliness".

Lambert's estimates and the modern picture

Over the following decades, meta-analyses multiplied into the hundreds, and summarising them became a job in itself. In 1993 Mark Lipsey and David Wilson gathered 302 meta-analyses of psychological, educational and behavioural treatments and found that the great majority reported meaningful positive effects, which could not be explained away by publication bias or poor design. The person who did most to keep the running total for psychotherapy was Michael Lambert, whose reviews in successive editions of the Handbook of Psychotherapy and Behavior Change (the book Bergin and Garfield had begun in 1971, which Lambert later edited) became the standard reference. Lambert's estimates can be summarised in four statements.

  • The average effect of psychotherapy compared with no treatment is large, around 0.8: the average treated client ends up better off than about 80 percent of untreated people (Lambert and Benjamin Ogles, 2004).
  • Spontaneous remission is real but smaller than Eysenck claimed, with a median of about 43 percent and large variation between disorders. Therapy helps more people improve, and helps them improve faster, than time alone.
  • Most of the gains of therapy are maintained after treatment ends, although some clients relapse, particularly with recurrent conditions such as depression.
  • A minority of clients get worse during therapy, which is why outcome needs to be monitored (Section 8.9).

These four statements answer the question "How much does therapy help?". Lambert's other famous estimate answers a different question, where the improvement comes from (the client, the relationship, expectancy or technique), and is the "pie" of Section 8.4. Do not confuse its 40 percent slice for client and extratherapeutic factors with the 43 percent median rate of spontaneous remission given here.

Two further points complete the picture. First, the effects of psychotherapy for common conditions such as depression and anxiety are broadly comparable to those of medication in the short term, and several psychotherapies, cognitive therapy in particular, appear to protect better against relapse after treatment stops (Chapter 5). Second, the effects are real in routine practice as well as in research trials, although usually somewhat smaller (Section 8.7). Table 8.2 collects the milestones of this part of the story.

Table 8.2 Milestones in the question "Does psychotherapy work?"
YearWhoWhat
1952Hans EysenckTwo-thirds of neurotics improve within two years with or without therapy
1954Rosenzweig; LuborskyFirst critical replies to Eysenck
1966Bergin; KieslerDeterioration effect; the uniformity myths
1967Gordon Paul"What treatment, by whom, is most effective for this individual..."
1971Allen BerginPsychoanalysis above 80 percent improved on re-analysis; remission about 30 percent
1976Gene Glass; Michael LambertMeta-analysis named; median spontaneous remission about 43 percent
1977Smith and Glass375 studies, effect size 0.68, better off than 75 percent
1980Smith, Glass and Miller475 studies, effect size 0.85, better off than 80 percent
1981 to 1982Andrews and Harvey; Landman and DawesRe-analyses confirm the effect
2004Lambert and OglesAverage treated client better off than about 80 percent of untreated
Clinical link

Effect sizes are averages. "Better off than 80 percent of untreated people" describes the typical client, not every client. Some clients improve dramatically, many improve moderately, some do not change, and a few get worse. When you explain the evidence to a client, the honest message is that therapy gives good odds, not a guarantee, and that you will check together, session by session, whether it is working for them (Section 8.9).
