8.3 The Dodo Bird Verdict and the Specific-Effects DebateMedium yield · 3 Qs

Section 8.2 ended with an answer to Eysenck: psychotherapy works, and the average treated client ends up better off than about 80 percent of untreated people. But buried in Smith and Glass's numbers was a second finding that worried therapists almost as much. Once the studies were made comparable, the different families of therapy produced broadly similar effects. Psychoanalysts, behaviour therapists, client-centred counsellors and cognitive therapists, who had spent decades telling one another that their rivals were mistaken, seemed to be getting about the same results. If that is true, what is all the arguing about? And what are such different therapies doing that is the same? The finding had in fact been predicted in 1936, by a young psychologist who borrowed his argument from a children's book.

A caucus-race in Wonderland: Rosenzweig (1936)

Begin with the children's book. In the third chapter of Alice's Adventures in Wonderland (1865), by Lewis Carroll (the pen name of Charles Lutwidge Dodgson, 1832 to 1898), Alice and a crowd of birds and animals have been soaked in a pool of tears and need to get dry. The Dodo proposes a caucus-race. He marks out a course "in a sort of circle", the runners are placed along it here and there, and everyone starts and stops running whenever they like, so that it is not easy to know when the race is over. After half an hour the Dodo declares the race over, and the dripping creatures ask who has won. The Dodo thinks for a long time, one finger pressed to his forehead, and then delivers his judgement: "Everybody has won, and all must have prizes."

Seventy years later Saul Rosenzweig, whom you met in Section 8.1 as one of Eysenck's first critics, was a psychologist at Worcester State Hospital in Massachusetts. In 1936 he published a four-page paper in the American Journal of Orthopsychiatry, "Some implicit common factors in diverse methods of psychotherapy", with the Dodo's line as its epigraph. His starting point was an observation anyone could make. Every school of therapy has cures to its credit: psychoanalysis, treatment by persuasion, Christian Science and "any number of other psychotherapeutic ideologies". Each school takes its successes as proof that its theory is true. But if theories that contradict one another all produce cures, a cure cannot prove a theory. Perhaps, Rosenzweig suggested, the factors a therapist believes are operating are not the ones that actually are, and the factors actually operating in different therapies have much more in common than their theories admit. He even proposed, mischievously, that psychoanalysis might succeed because the analyst unwittingly allows the patient to "recondition certain inadequate social patterns" in the present relationship, "a phenomenon better explained by Pavlov's than by Freud's theories". He named three shared factors.

  1. Implicit, unverbalised factors. Every therapy offers a chance for catharsis (the relief of expressing pent-up feeling) and for social "reconditioning" in the relationship. Closely related is the "as yet undefined effect of the personality of the good therapist", which can seem to cure patients "by a sort of catalytic effect" whatever the method. No school has a monopoly on good therapists.
  2. The formal consistency of the therapeutic ideology. If disorder is a conflict between disintegrated parts of the personality, any consistent scheme that pulls the parts together will help. Whether the therapist talks psychoanalysis or Christian Science matters less than the consistency with which the doctrine is applied.
  3. Alternative formulations and the interdependence of personality. Psychological events can be described validly in many ways, and the personality is so interconnected that a change at any significant point spreads to the whole. A therapy need not be completely correct; it needs only enough truth to take hold somewhere.

From this paper come the three terms that organise the rest of this chapter. The common factors (sometimes called non-specific factors) are the ingredients that all or most therapies share: a relationship with a helper, a setting, an explanation, hope and a procedure to follow. The specific factors (specific ingredients) are the techniques unique to one therapy and claimed by its theory to cause change: interpretation in psychoanalysis, exposure in behaviour therapy, disputing irrational beliefs in REBT. The dodo bird verdict is the claim that different bona fide psychotherapies produce roughly equivalent outcomes, together with the explanation that common factors, rather than specific techniques, do most of the work.

Two things about the paper are usually forgotten. It contained no data, and Rosenzweig said he was assuming equal success only "for purposes of exposition"; he added that "in certain types of mental disturbances certain kinds of therapy are indicated", and that a good therapist needs a repertoire of methods. The founder of the dodo bird verdict did not believe that every therapy suits every problem. And a footnote pointed to a "far more pressing problem": why, in so many cases, all methods prove equally unsuccessful, a question that waited for Bergin's deterioration effect (Section 8.1) and the study of clients who do not improve (Section 8.9).

Exam trap

The Dodo comes from Lewis Carroll's Alice's Adventures in Wonderland (1865); Saul Rosenzweig first applied it to psychotherapy in 1936; the label "dodo bird verdict" was popularised by Luborsky, Singer and Luborsky (1975). It is not Eysenck's idea (his claim was that therapy adds nothing beyond spontaneous remission). Keep apart Rosenzweig's 1936 common factors paper and his Picture-Frustration Study, a semi-projective test that belongs to the Assessment book.

The tie score: Luborsky, Singer and Luborsky (1975)

For almost forty years Rosenzweig's paper was an argument without evidence. Then, in 1975, Lester Luborsky (Section 8.1) of the University of Pennsylvania and his colleagues Barton Singer and Lise Luborsky set out to test it, and borrowed the Dodo for their title: "Comparative studies of psychotherapies: is it true that 'everyone has won and all must have prizes'?"

Key study: Luborsky, Singer and Luborsky, "Comparative studies of psychotherapies" (1975)

Question: When different psychotherapies are compared in controlled studies, does any one do better? Method: A box-score review (Section 8.2) in the Archives of General Psychiatry of every reasonably controlled comparison of psychotherapies with each other and with other treatments, each scored as a win, a loss or a tie. Result: Most comparisons of one psychotherapy with another found no significant difference in the proportion of patients who improved, although most patients in all the therapies benefited: the "tie score effect". Combined treatments often did better than single ones, and only two especially beneficial matches of a kind of patient to a kind of treatment turned up. Conclusion: The likeliest explanation was the components the therapies share, "especially the helping relationship with a therapist". Criticism: Box scores ignore effect size and count underpowered non-significant studies as ties, so the method was biased towards finding equivalence.

Notice what the authors did not say. They explicitly rejected the idea that patients might as well be assigned to therapies at random, because their studies measured the amount of improvement, and equal amounts might hide differences in its kind. The finding was soon repeated with better tools. The Temple University trial of Sloane and colleagues (1975, Section 4.11) found behaviour therapy and short-term psychodynamic therapy about equally helpful for outpatients. Smith and Glass (1977) and Smith, Glass and Miller (1980) found that the families of therapy differed little once studies were made comparable (Section 8.2). And the NIMH depression trial (Section 8.8) found cognitive behaviour therapy and interpersonal psychotherapy producing similar results. By the mid-1980s this "equivalence paradox" was one of the best-replicated findings in the field.

Exam trap

The dodo bird verdict says that different bona fide therapies tend to produce roughly equivalent outcomes, and explains this by common factors such as the alliance, empathy and hope. "Psychotherapy is generally ineffective" is Eysenck (Section 8.1). "Only psychoanalysis produces lasting change" is an old partisan claim that no review supported. "Medication always outperforms psychotherapy" is false (Section 8.2). The specific technique, the clinician's theoretical orientation and the patient's diagnosis are what the verdict says matter least, which is why they make tempting distractors.

Wampold's test of bona fide therapies (1997)

The verdict had a weakness its opponents were quick to point out. Most of the evidence compared classes of therapy (behavioural against psychodynamic, say) across different studies with different clients and measures. Worse, many comparative studies pitted a favoured therapy against a vague "supportive" treatment that nobody expected to work. A comparison like that, an intent-to-fail control (a straw-man treatment designed to lose), can make any favoured therapy look superior. A fair test would compare real therapies, intended to help, head to head in the same study.

That test was designed by Bruce E. Wampold (born 1948), a counselling psychologist at the University of Wisconsin at Madison who had first trained in mathematics, with five colleagues. Their 1997 paper in Psychological Bulletin also carried the Dodo in its title: "A meta-analysis of outcome studies comparing bona fide psychotherapies: empirically, 'all must have prizes'". The key move was a strict definition. A bona fide psychotherapy is a treatment intended to be therapeutic: it is delivered by a trained therapist (at least a master's degree) who forms a relationship with the client and tailors the treatment to him, it addresses a problem that would reasonably be brought to a psychotherapist, and it meets at least two of four further conditions (it cites an established approach, it is described in terms of psychological processes, it follows a manual, or its active ingredients are named). Placebo controls, "alternative" therapies designed as controls, and dismantling studies were all excluded.

Key study: Wampold, Mondin, Moody, Stich, Benson and Ahn (1997)

Question: When two bona fide psychotherapies are compared directly, is the true difference zero, as the dodo bird conjecture predicts? Method: Every direct comparison of two bona fide therapies published in six leading journals between 1970 and 1995, 277 comparisons in the main analysis. Because which treatment is labelled "A" is arbitrary, each effect size was given a positive or negative sign at random; if the true difference is zero, the effects should then scatter around zero exactly as sampling error predicts. Result: They did. Taking the effects as absolute values (which deliberately overestimates any difference) gave an upper bound of about 0.2. Differences did not grow in later studies, when methods had improved, and therapies rated as very dissimilar did not differ more than similar ones. Conclusion: "The efficacy of bona fide treatments are roughly equivalent." Criticism: The sample was dominated by behavioural and cognitive-behavioural treatments and a limited range of problems.

How big is "at most about 0.2"? Wampold set his result beside the estimates Michael Lambert and Allen Bergin had compiled in 1994. Psychotherapy against no treatment: about 0.82, the large effect of Section 8.2. Psychotherapy against a placebo treatment (a credible but theoretically inert procedure): about 0.48. A placebo against no treatment: about 0.42. One bona fide therapy against another: between 0.00 and 0.21. The largest plausible difference between two real therapies is about half the effect of a placebo compared with nothing, as Figure 8.2 shows.

Wampold was careful, as Rosenzweig had been, about what his result did not show. Some therapies are built for particular disorders (he gave exposure for phobias as the example), and some disorders, such as obsessive-compulsive disorder, may not respond to many different treatments. "It would be unwarranted," he wrote, "to conclude from this study that all therapies are equally effective with all disorders." The equivalence finding has nevertheless held up. In 2002 Luborsky and seven colleagues pooled 17 meta-analyses of active treatments compared with each other and titled their paper "The dodo bird verdict is alive and well, mostly": the average difference was about 0.20, and it shrank further when researcher allegiance was taken into account (Section 8.6). In 2008 Pim Cuijpers and his colleagues compared seven treatments for mild to moderate adult depression (CBT, non-directive supportive therapy, behavioural activation, psychodynamic therapy, problem-solving therapy, interpersonal psychotherapy and social skills training) across 53 studies and found no large differences: interpersonal psychotherapy was slightly ahead (d = 0.20) and non-directive supportive therapy slightly behind (d = minus 0.13). The same year Benish, Imel and Wampold found no differences among bona fide therapies for post-traumatic stress disorder.

The verdict also had an unexpected sting. If no therapy beats any other, perhaps psychotherapy has no active ingredients and works only as a placebo. That was the argument pressed in 1996 by the American psychiatrist Donald F. Klein, who wanted psychotherapies tested against a pill placebo, as a new drug must be, and read their equivalence as a sign that they do nothing specific: their benefits, on this view, are the non-specific ones a placebo also provides. A verdict that began as a defence of psychotherapy could be turned into an attack on it. Section 8.4 explains why Wampold thought Klein had drawn the wrong conclusion.

Exam trap

Wampold and colleagues (1997, Psychological Bulletin, 277 comparisons) tested the dodo bird conjecture with bona fide therapies in direct comparisons and found an upper bound of about 0.2. A bona fide therapy is intended to be therapeutic; an intent-to-fail control is not. Do not confuse 0.2 (the most that therapies differ from each other) with 0.82 (therapy against no treatment).

The case for specific effects

Not everyone accepted the verdict. In The Effects of Psychological Therapy (1980), the behaviour therapists Stanley Rachman and G. Terence Wilson put the objection memorably: if prizes go to everyone, "we end up with the same advice for everyone: 'Regardless of the nature of your problem seek any form of psychotherapy.' This is absurd." No sensible clinician would tell a woman terrified of lifts and a man with a lifelong drinking problem that their choice of treatment did not matter. Researchers who hold that particular techniques, designed for particular problems, cause much of the change are often called specific-effects researchers, and the changes they attribute to those techniques are specific effects. Their evidence comes from four areas.

Exposure for anxiety disorders and OCD. The clearest case is obsessive-compulsive disorder. In exposure and response prevention (ERP, Section 4.3) the client faces the feared situation (touching the "contaminated" door handle) while giving up the ritual that normally follows (washing). If the Dodo were right about OCD, a credible treatment with a warm therapist and a sensible rationale but no exposure should do about as well.

Key study: Lindsay, Crino and Andrews, ERP against anxiety management (1997)

Question: Does ERP help OCD because of exposure and response prevention themselves, or because of the non-specific benefits of any credible therapy? Method: At St Vincent's Hospital in Sydney, 18 patients with OCD were randomly assigned to ERP or to general anxiety management, a credible alternative; both received about 15 hours of therapy over three weeks. Result: Obsessive-compulsive symptoms fell significantly with ERP; with anxiety management there was no change. Conclusion: The benefit comes from the specific techniques, not from non-specific aspects of therapy. Criticism: With so small a sample the study shows that a large difference exists rather than estimating its size.

Larger trials agree. In a 2005 multicentre trial, Edna B. Foa (1937 to 2026) and her colleagues assigned adults with OCD to intensive exposure and ritual prevention, the drug clomipramine, the two combined, or a pill placebo. After 12 weeks 62 percent of the exposure group had responded, against 42 percent on clomipramine and 8 percent on placebo, and adding the drug to exposure did not improve on exposure alone.

Cognitive therapy for panic disorder. In Oxford, David M. Clark and his colleagues (1994) compared cognitive therapy for panic, built on the idea that attacks come from catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations, with applied relaxation (a credible, well-developed treatment) and with the antidepressant imipramine, in 64 patients. All three beat a waiting list, but cognitive therapy was superior to both alternatives at three months and again at fifteen months, after several imipramine patients had relapsed. In 2007 Jedidiah Siev and Dianne L. Chambless meta-analysed studies comparing cognitive therapy directly with relaxation therapy. For generalised anxiety disorder the two were equivalent; for panic disorder, cognitive therapy (which included interoceptive exposure, deliberately bringing on the feared bodily sensations) won on every panic-related measure. Two bona fide treatments from the same CBT family, two anxiety disorders, yet equal for one and unequal for the other: "specificity of treatment to disorder".

Behavioural methods for childhood problems. In 1995 John R. Weisz and his colleagues analysed 150 outcome studies with children and adolescents. Behavioural treatments produced larger effects than non-behavioural ones, and outcomes were stronger for the problems targeted in treatment than for problems that were not, just as a specific-effects account predicts. In 1989 Alan E. Kazdin (born 1945) and his colleagues assigned 112 children aged 7 to 13 with severe antisocial behaviour to problem-solving skills training (a cognitive-behavioural treatment), the same training with real-life practice, or client-centred relationship therapy; both skills groups improved more, at home and at school, and a year later, while children in relationship therapy tended to stay where they had started. Section 4.11 adds the other childhood successes, from the bell and pad to parent management training.

Newer meta-analyses of CBT. In 2010 David F. Tolin analysed 26 trials (1,981 patients) comparing CBT with another bona fide therapy. CBT was superior to psychodynamic therapy, though not to interpersonal or supportive therapy, and only for anxiety and depressive disorders; researcher allegiance was correlated with CBT's advantage, but the advantage survived when allegiance was controlled. In 2014 David Marcus and his colleagues updated Wampold's analysis with 51 newer studies and did find differences between bona fide treatments on the primary outcome each treatment targeted at the end of treatment, though little difference on secondary outcomes, at the end of treatment or at follow-up. Table 8.3 collects this evidence.

Table 8.3 Evidence for specific effects: problems for which a particular treatment beats a credible alternative.
ProblemComparisonResultStudy
Obsessive-compulsive disorderERP against anxiety managementERP reduced symptoms; anxiety management did notLindsay and colleagues (1997)
Obsessive-compulsive disorderExposure and ritual prevention, clomipramine, placeboResponse 62, 42 and 8 percentFoa and colleagues (2005)
Panic disorderCognitive therapy, applied relaxation, imipramineCognitive therapy best at 3 and 15 monthsClark and colleagues (1994)
Panic and generalised anxietyCognitive therapy against relaxationEqual for generalised anxiety; cognitive therapy better for panicSiev and Chambless (2007)
Childhood problemsBehavioural against non-behavioural therapiesBehavioural larger, mainly on targeted problemsWeisz and colleagues (1995)
Antisocial childrenProblem-solving skills against relationship therapySkills training better, and a year laterKazdin and colleagues (1989)
Anxiety and depressionCBT against other bona fide therapiesCBT ahead of psychodynamic therapy (small effect)Tolin (2010)
Exam trap

Specific effects are clearest for OCD and the anxiety disorders (exposure), panic (cognitive therapy with interoceptive exposure) and childhood behaviour problems (behavioural methods), and weakest for mild to moderate depression. ERP is the treatment of choice for OCD, not for depression, schizophrenia or personality disorders. "Regardless of the nature of your problem seek any form of psychotherapy. This is absurd" is Rachman and Wilson's (1980) objection to the dodo verdict, not a statement of it.

The critiques of the verdict

Table 8.3 does not refute Wampold's meta-analysis; it suggests that the meta-analysis asked its question in a way that can hide real differences. In 2002 Luborsky's "alive and well" paper appeared alongside replies with titles that give the flavour: Chambless's "Beware the dodo bird: the dangers of overgeneralization" and Larry E. Beutler's "The dodo bird is extinct". The objections fall into five groups.

Averaging across disorders. A single average across all problems can hide large differences for particular ones. Imagine, purely for illustration, a meta-analysis of ten head-to-head comparisons. Eight are depression trials in which the two therapies truly differ by nothing (d = 0). Two are OCD trials in which ERP truly beats supportive therapy by a large margin (d = 0.8). The average is (8 × 0 + 2 × 0.8) ÷ 10 = 0.16, a "small" effect that seems to confirm the Dodo. Yet a person with OCD who followed that average would choose the wrong treatment. The average answers "Across everything, do therapies differ?"; a client is asking Gordon Paul's question (Section 8.1), "For my problem, which is best?"

Which comparisons were included. A meta-analysis can only average the studies that exist. Critics noted that a large share of Wampold's comparisons set one variant of behavioural or cognitive-behavioural therapy against another, and close cousins would be expected to tie whatever the truth about therapies in general.

Targeted against general outcomes. Wampold averaged across every outcome measure in a study. A trial of ERP might measure obsessions and compulsions but also general anxiety, mood and self-esteem, on which any good therapy helps. Averaging the target with general measures dilutes specific effects, which is why Weisz in children and Marcus in 2014 found differences on targeted outcomes and few on the rest.

Weak comparisons and low power. A non-significant difference is not proof of no difference. Kazdin and Donald Bass showed in 1989 that most comparative outcome studies had too few participants to detect the small to medium differences two active treatments are likely to show, so a string of "ties" is what you would expect whether or not real differences exist. (Power is taught in the Research Methodology book.)

Allegiance. Researchers tend to find that their own therapy wins (Section 8.6). Both sides use the point: dodo supporters say that once allegiance is removed differences shrink towards zero; specific-effects researchers reply, with Tolin, that some differences survive the correction.

The common-factors side answered that its prediction had been tested properly, that rated similarity of treatments was unrelated to the size of differences, and that even where differences exist they are small beside the differences between therapists delivering the same treatment (Section 8.6). Some exchanges continue disorder by disorder: when Benish and colleagues reported equivalence for PTSD, Anke Ehlers and her colleagues replied in 2010 ("Do all psychological treatments really work the same in posttraumatic stress disorder?") that the meta-analysis had selected its evidence in a biased way, and that trauma-focused treatments (trauma-focused CBT and EMDR) have the strongest evidence, while treatments that do not address the trauma memory or its meaning are less effective or not yet adequately studied. Table 8.4 sets out the exchange.

Table 8.4 The main critiques of the dodo bird verdict and the replies from the common-factors side.
CritiqueThe pointThe reply
Averaging across disorders (Chambless, 2002)A small overall average can hide large differences for particular disordersDisorder-specific meta-analyses (depression, PTSD) also find small differences
Similar treatments comparedMany comparisons set one CBT variant against anotherRated similarity was unrelated to the size of the difference
General outcomes dilute the targetSpecific effects appear on the targeted problemTargeted differences, where found, are small
Low power (Kazdin and Bass, 1989)Non-significant "ties" are not proof of equivalencePooling hundreds of comparisons supplies the power
Researcher allegianceResearchers tend to find for their own therapyCorrecting for allegiance shrinks differences further
Exam trap

The core critique is overgeneralisation: averaging across disorders, outcome measures and closely similar treatments, in studies too small to detect modest differences. "Beware the dodo bird" is Chambless (2002); "The dodo bird is extinct" is Beutler (2002); "alive and well, mostly" is Luborsky and colleagues (2002). "No significant difference" means a difference was not detected, not that the therapies are equal.

Taking therapies apart: dismantling and component studies

If one kind of study could settle the argument, it would be the component study, whose two designs Section 5.11 introduced. A dismantling study (component analysis) compares a full treatment with the same treatment minus one supposedly essential component; an additive design (constructive design) adds a component to a basic treatment. The logic is the logic of medicine: remove the active ingredient and the treatment should get worse, as a tablet without its active chemical becomes a placebo. You have met three famous results already. Exposure without Wolpe's relaxation and strict hierarchy still worked (Section 4.2); behavioural activation alone did as well as full cognitive therapy for depression (Jacobson and colleagues, 1996, Section 5.11); and EMDR's eye movements added nothing reliable to the rest of the procedure (Section 4.4).

Two meta-analyses have pooled the component studies. In 2001 Hyun-nie Ahn and Wampold collected 27 component studies under the title "Where oh where are the specific ingredients?" The difference between treatments with and without their supposedly critical components was not significantly different from zero, and the results were homogeneous. In 2013 Bell, Marcus and Goodlad analysed 66 studies published from 1980 to 2010 and separated the two designs. Dismantling studies again showed no significant difference. Additive studies, however, showed a small but significant benefit of the added component, d = 0.14 at the end of treatment and 0.28 at follow-up, but only on the problems that component targeted. Their conclusion: "added specific ingredients may contribute modestly to treatment outcomes".

A related test asks whether delivering a treatment faithfully predicts outcome. Raters in clinical trials score each therapist's adherence (delivering the prescribed components) and competence (delivering them skilfully). If specific ingredients matter, both should predict who improves. A 2010 meta-analysis by Christian Webb, Robert DeRubeis and Jacques Barber found that they barely did: correlations with outcome of only 0.02 for adherence and 0.07 for competence (effect sizes of about 0.04 and 0.14), neither significantly different from zero. Manuals and fidelity checks are described in Section 8.7; here notice one more result that the medical model of therapy (Section 8.4) finds hard to explain.

Exam trap

A dismantling study removes a component from a full package; an additive (constructive) study adds one to a basic package. Dismantling meta-analyses (Ahn and Wampold, 2001; Bell and colleagues, 2013) find essentially no difference; additive studies find a small gain on targeted outcomes. None of this shows that therapy does not work; it shows that the named "active ingredient" is not where most of the effect lies.

Where the debate stands now

Ninety years after Rosenzweig, the two camps agree on more than their rhetoric suggests. The evidence can be summed up in four statements.

  • For many common problems the Dodo is roughly right. Well-delivered bona fide therapies for mild to moderate depression and many general outpatient complaints differ by no more than an effect size of about 0.2, far less than the effect of therapy against no therapy.
  • For some problems the Dodo is wrong. Exposure for OCD and the anxiety disorders, cognitive therapy with interoceptive exposure for panic, and behavioural methods for many childhood problems beat credible alternatives, as Rosenzweig and Wampold both allowed.
  • Common and specific factors are not rivals. A technique is always delivered through a relationship, and a relationship always comes with some procedure. Exposure for OCD works far better when the client trusts the therapist enough to touch the door handle.
  • The question has changed. Researchers now ask how therapy works (process and mechanisms, Section 8.10), for whom and at whose hands (Section 8.6), and how to tell early when it is not working (Section 8.9).

The stakes are practical, which is why the debate never quite ends. Specific-effects researchers such as David H. Barlow (born 1942), whose 2004 paper "Psychological treatments" argued that evidence-based treatments for particular disorders belong in health care alongside medicine, built the lists of empirically supported treatments described in Section 8.8. Common-factors researchers, led by Wampold (for example Laska, Gurman and Wampold's 2014 paper "Expanding the lens of evidence-based practice in psychotherapy: a common factors perspective"), argue that the lists overvalue brand names and undervalue the relationship and the therapist. What they still disagree about is emphasis: whether training should begin with techniques for particular disorders, or with the relational skills through which every technique is delivered. Section 8.4 looks at the theory behind the second answer.

Clinical link

Think back to Ravi, the engineering student with panic attacks in Section 8.1. The specific-effects evidence says his best option is CBT for panic with interoceptive exposure, not general relaxation or open-ended supportive talk. The common-factors evidence says the same treatment will work better if he trusts his psychologist, accepts the explanation of his attacks and leaves the first session with hope. For a client with mild depression after a bereavement, by contrast, the evidence offers a genuine choice among bona fide therapies, which can follow her preferences and your skills. Knowing which kind of problem is in front of you is part of evidence-based practice.

Exam trap

The modern consensus is not "all therapies are equal" and not "only technique matters". Bona fide therapies differ little for many common problems; specific techniques add clearly for some (OCD, the anxiety disorders, panic, childhood behaviour problems); common and specific factors work together. An option saying the dodo verdict shows therapy "does not work" has confused Luborsky with Eysenck.

8.4 Common Factors: Frank, Lambert's Pie and Wampold's Contextual ModelMedium yield · 2 Qs

If the Dodo is even roughly right, the next question is Rosenzweig's: what do the therapies share? Researchers have produced many lists. When Lisa Grencavage and John Norcross reviewed 50 publications in 1990, they found 89 proposed common factors, and grouped the most often mentioned into five areas: client characteristics, therapist qualities, change processes, the structure of treatment and the therapeutic relationship. Three accounts have shaped the field more than any list, and examiners ask about all three: Jerome Frank's comparative study of healing, Michael Lambert's estimate of where change comes from, and Bruce Wampold's contextual model. Carl Rogers' necessary and sufficient conditions (1957), the most famous claim about a common factor of all, were taught in Section 6.2, and the evidence for them appears in Section 8.5.

Jerome Frank: Persuasion and Healing

Jerome David Frank (1909 to 2005) trained at Harvard and spent his career as a psychiatrist at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore. In the 1950s he and his colleagues studied the placebo in psychiatric outpatients (David Rosenthal and Frank's review "Psychotherapy and the placebo effect" appeared in 1956), and the puzzle that held him for the rest of his life was why people in all kinds of treatment, including inert ones, so often felt better. His answer was Persuasion and Healing: A Comparative Study of Psychotherapy (1961), revised in 1973 and again in 1991 with his daughter, the psychiatrist Julia B. Frank. The important word in the subtitle is "comparative". Frank compared psychotherapy not with other psychotherapies but with every form of healing he could find: religious healing at shrines such as Lourdes, revival meetings, the "thought reform" of prisoners in Communist China, the healing rites of traditional societies and the placebo in medicine.

Frank began with a question about who comes for help. Many people with psychiatric symptoms never seek treatment. What brings them, he argued, is not the symptoms alone but demoralisation: a state in which a person feels that he has failed to meet his own expectations and those of others, cannot cope with his problems, and is helpless, hopeless, isolated and diminished in self-esteem. Frank's 1974 paper was titled simply "Psychotherapy: the restoration of morale", and in 1982 he and John de Figueiredo described subjective incompetence, a feeling of being unable to act effectively and not knowing what to do, as the clinical hallmark of demoralisation. If demoralisation is what all clients share, then what all effective therapies share is remoralisation, the restoration of hope and of the sense of being able to cope. That would explain why clients often feel better within a session or two, long before any technique could have done much.

How do therapies remoralise? In the third edition, the Franks identified four features shared by all forms of psychotherapy and, indeed, by healing in every culture.

  1. An emotionally charged, confiding relationship with a helping person (often with the help of a group). The client trusts the healer with things he has told nobody else, and believes the healer is competent and cares whether he gets better.
  2. A healing setting. The place itself announces that healing happens here: the clinic with its waiting room and framed degrees, the hospital, the consulting room with its couch, the temple or shrine. The setting raises the client's expectation of help and gives him a protected place, away from daily life, in which to try something new.
  3. A rationale, conceptual scheme or myth that provides a plausible explanation for the client's symptoms and prescribes a ritual or procedure for resolving them. Frank used the word "myth" deliberately, and not as an insult: the explanation must be plausible to client and therapist and fit the culture's view of the world, but it need not be objectively true. Its power lies in turning a frightening, meaningless experience into one that makes sense and can be acted on.
  4. A ritual or procedure that requires the active participation of both client and therapist, and that both believe is the means of restoring the client's health: free association, thought records, exposure exercises, or a prayer and an offering.

These four features, the Franks argued, serve six functions in every therapy. They combat the client's sense of alienation and strengthen the relationship; inspire and maintain the client's expectation of help; provide new learning experiences; arouse emotion, which is needed for change; enhance the client's sense of mastery or self-efficacy (Albert Bandura's term for a person's belief in his capability to organise and carry out the actions needed to reach a specific goal); and provide opportunities for practice and success. Notice how much of this is about hope. Persuasion, in Frank's title, is not trickery; it is the process by which a demoralised person comes to believe, with good reason, that change is possible.

Exam trap

The book is Persuasion and Healing (1961) by Jerome Frank; a past paper reversed it as "Healing and Persuasion" and the answer was still Frank. The distractors were real people with other claims to fame: Alfred Pritz (born 1952), an Austrian psychoanalyst who was founding rector of the Sigmund Freud Private University in Vienna and president of the World Council for Psychotherapy; "Charles Winnicot", a garbling of Donald Woods Winnicott (1896 to 1971), the paediatrician and psychoanalyst of the transitional object and the "good enough" mother (Section 3.4), whose wife Clare Winnicott was his collaborator; and "Jeff Klerman", a garbling of Gerald Klerman (1928 to 1992), who developed interpersonal psychotherapy with Myrna Weissman (Section 3.7). Frank's four features are relationship, healing setting, rationale or myth, and ritual or procedure; his key concepts are demoralisation and remoralisation. Do not confuse him with Viktor Frankl (logotherapy).

Healers across cultures

Frank's boldest claim was that a psychotherapist in a Western clinic and a traditional healer are doing recognisably the same thing. Table 8.5 tests the claim on three very different settings, one of them Indian.

Table 8.5 Frank's four shared features in three healing settings.
FeatureA CBT clinicPsychoanalysisA healing temple
Confiding relationshipA psychologist who listens and takes the problem seriouslyAn analyst who hears everything, several times a weekPriests, healers and fellow pilgrims who accept the sufferer
Healing settingA clinic, an appointment, credentials on the wallThe consulting room and the couchThe temple, its sanctity and its reputation for cures
Rationale or mythThoughts and avoidance keep the problem goingAn unconscious childhood conflict is being repeatedA troubling spirit afflicts the person and can be made to leave
Ritual or procedureThought records, behavioural experiments, exposureFree association and interpretationPrayers, offerings and rites in which the spirit is summoned before the deity and made to depart
Indian context

The Indian psychoanalyst Sudhir Kakar (1938 to 2024) made a Frank-like comparison in Shamans, Mystics and Doctors: A Psychological Inquiry into India and Its Healing Traditions (1982). One of its best-known chapters describes the Balaji temple at Mehandipur in Rajasthan, where pilgrims who believe themselves troubled by spirits come for healing, and where the temple works like a court in which the afflicting spirit is summoned and tried. Kakar interpreted possession and its cure psychologically while insisting that healing cannot be separated from its cultural setting. The lesson for a clinical psychologist is double. Traditional healing can relieve distress because it carries Frank's common factors, which is why many Indian families consult a temple, a dargah or a healer before a clinic. But common factors do not make every practice safe or sufficient for severe mental illness: Section 9.6 tells how 28 chained patients died in a fire at a faith-healing home in Erwadi, Tamil Nadu, in 2001. Section 8.11 discusses how therapy can be adapted to Indian clients.

Frank's argument also explains a finding that will return in Section 8.11. If a therapy's explanation must fit the client's world view, then therapies adapted to the client's culture should work better. A 2011 meta-analysis by Benish, Quintana and Wampold found exactly that: for ethnic and racial minority clients, culturally adapted psychotherapies were modestly more effective than the same bona fide therapies unadapted (d = 0.32), and the one kind of adaptation that accounted for the advantage was adapting the explanation of the illness to the cultural group's own beliefs (its "myth", in Frank's sense).

Lambert's pie: four sources of change

Frank described the common factors; Michael Lambert tried to weigh them. In 1992, in the Handbook of Psychotherapy Integration edited by John Norcross and Marvin Goldfried, Lambert (whom you met in Section 8.1 as the reviewer of spontaneous remission and in Section 8.2 as the keeper of the running total of how much therapy helps) turned to a different question: how much of clients' improvement could be credited to each of four sources. His answers, drawn as a pie chart in Figure 8.3, became among the most reproduced numbers in counselling and clinical training.

  • Client and extratherapeutic factors, about 40 percent. Everything about the client and his life outside therapy that aids recovery whether or not he is in therapy. These extratherapeutic factors include the severity and duration of the problem, motivation, inner strengths, supportive family and friends, and chance events such as a new job or the end of examinations. Spontaneous remission and regression to the mean (Section 8.1) belong in this slice.
  • Common factors, about 30 percent. The relationship variables found in therapies of every school: empathy, warmth, acceptance, encouragement to take risks, the therapeutic alliance. Lambert called this slice "common factors"; later writers usually call it "relationship factors".
  • Expectancy (placebo), about 15 percent. The improvement that comes from the client's knowledge that he is being helped, his hope, and his belief in the rationale and power of the treatment.
  • Techniques, about 15 percent. The model-specific procedures unique to particular therapies: exposure, interpretation, disputing, systematic desensitisation, hypnosis, biofeedback.

Push Ravi from Section 8.1 through the pie. His examinations ended, his family rallied and his panic was at its worst when he came for help: all of that is the 40 percent slice. A psychologist who listened without judging and whom he trusted supplied the 30 percent. Learning that panic is a surge of adrenaline and not a heart attack, and believing that the treatment would work, gave him the expectancy slice. The interoceptive exposure exercises were the technique. The pie does not say that technique was unimportant for him; Section 8.3 showed that for panic it matters a good deal. It says that technique was one source of change among several.

Now the caveats, which matter as much as the numbers. Lambert himself said that the percentages were "not derived from a strict statistical analysis"; they were his judgement of what the research of the day suggested. They describe shares of improvement, not measured proportions of variance, and the categories overlap (a warm relationship raises expectancy, a credible technique strengthens the relationship). Other researchers, using other methods, have produced quite different numbers. The estimates spread widely after Asay and Lambert restated them in The Heart and Soul of Change (1999), a book edited by Mark Hubble, Barry Duncan and Scott Miller that argued for building practice around common factors, and the pie is now often quoted as if it were a measured result. It is not. Its lasting message is the ranking: the client brings the largest share of change, the relationship comes next, and the techniques that fill most textbooks (including Chapters 3 to 7 of this one) come last, level with hope.

Exam trap

Lambert (1992): client and extratherapeutic factors 40, relationship (common) factors 30, expectancy or placebo 15, techniques 15 percent. The largest slice is the client, not the relationship; technique and expectancy are equal. The figures are Lambert's rough estimates, not results of a meta-analysis, and they are not Frank's or Wampold's. Do not confuse Lambert's four factors with Frank's four features.

Placebo, expectancy and remoralisation

Two of Lambert's slices, expectancy and part of the relationship, overlap with what medicine calls the placebo. A placebo (Latin for "I shall please") is a treatment with no active ingredient for the condition treated, such as a sugar pill or a sham procedure; the placebo effect is the improvement that follows because the patient believes he is being treated. In 1955 the Harvard anaesthetist Henry K. Beecher (1904 to 1976) published "The powerful placebo", estimating that about a third of patients in the trials he reviewed (35 percent) improved on placebo alone. Later research has been more cautious. In 2001 Asbjørn Hróbjartsson and Peter Gøtzsche reviewed 130 trials that compared a placebo with no treatment at all and found little evidence of powerful effects in general, but possible small benefits for subjective outcomes such as pain. Much of what looks like a placebo response in a trial is the improvement that would have happened anyway: Eysenck's spontaneous remission and the regression to the mean of Section 8.1.

The psychologist who has done most to explain how expectations heal is Irving Kirsch (born 1943). His response expectancy theory (1985) holds that expecting a particular experience, such as less pain or less anxiety, can directly produce it, like a self-fulfilling prophecy. Kirsch then turned the idea on antidepressants. In 1998, with Guy Sapirstein, he meta-analysed 19 double-blind trials with 2,318 patients and concluded that about 75 percent of the response to the drugs was duplicated by placebo; in 2008, using the trial data submitted to the United States Food and Drug Administration, he and his colleagues found the difference between drug and placebo small except in very severe depression. These conclusions are contested, and drugs belong to the psychiatrists; the point here is how much of any healing, chemical or psychological, is carried by expectation.

Key study: Kaptchuk and colleagues, the components of the placebo effect (2008)

Question: Can the placebo effect be separated into parts, and how much does the relationship with the practitioner add? Method: Ted J. Kaptchuk of Harvard Medical School and his colleagues (Kirsch was one of the authors) randomly assigned 262 adults with irritable bowel syndrome to a waiting list; to sham acupuncture with a practitioner who kept conversation to a minimum ("limited"); or to the same sham acupuncture with an "augmented" relationship, in which the practitioner asked about the symptoms and their meaning with warmth, attention, active listening and confidence. Result: After three weeks, adequate relief of symptoms was reported by 28 percent on the waiting list, 44 percent in the limited group and 62 percent in the augmented group. Conclusion: Non-specific effects can be clinically significant, and "the patient-practitioner relationship is the most robust component". Criticism: Irritable bowel syndrome has subjective outcomes that respond well to expectation, so the size of the effect may not carry over to every condition.

Kaptchuk's trial shows the problem with the idea of a placebo in psychotherapy. A drug placebo is the real treatment minus its chemical. But what is psychotherapy minus its active ingredient? A "placebo" therapy (the placebo-attention control described in Section 8.7) is a series of meetings with a caring professional who listens and offers some explanation, which is to say it contains most of the common factors by design. In 2003 Baskin, Tierney, Minami and Wampold showed what follows. When the placebo control was structurally equivalent to the real therapy (the same number and length of sessions, equally trained therapists, the same format, clients free to talk about their problems), active treatments were barely better than it; only placebos that were structurally weaker produced large differences. Within psychotherapy itself, a 2011 meta-analysis led by Michael Constantino found a small but reliable relationship between clients' expectations of improvement and their outcomes (d about 0.24).

Frank's remoralisation was put into a testable form in 1993 by Kenneth Howard and his colleagues, whose dose-effect research appears in Section 8.9. Their phase model describes three phases of change in sequence: remoralisation, an early improvement in subjective well-being, often within the first few sessions; remediation, the relief of symptoms; and rehabilitation, the slower change in long-standing patterns of living and working. In a large sample of outpatients, improvement in well-being came first and was, in their words, "a probabilistically necessary condition" for symptomatic improvement, which in turn preceded improvement in life functioning. This may be one reason why so much change in CBT for depression happens before the main cognitive techniques have been taught (Section 5.11).

Exam trap

Keep four "improvements without the technique" apart: spontaneous remission (improvement without treatment), regression to the mean (extreme scores drift back), the placebo effect (improvement because the person believes he is treated) and remoralisation (restored hope, Frank). The phase model order is remoralisation, remediation, rehabilitation (Howard and colleagues, 1993). Response expectancy is Kirsch's theory; "The powerful placebo" (1955) is Beecher's. Do not mix them with Victor Vroom's expectancy theory of work motivation (1964), in which motivation is highest when a person believes effort leads to performance, performance leads to outcomes, and the outcomes are valued (valence).

Wampold's contextual model

Frank's ideas were given their most rigorous modern form by Bruce Wampold, whose meta-analysis you met in Section 8.3. In The Great Psychotherapy Debate: Models, Methods, and Findings (2001), and in a second edition written in 2015 with Zac E. Imel, Wampold argued that the field was really choosing between two models of how psychotherapy works, and that the evidence could decide between them.

The first is what Wampold called the medical model of psychotherapy. The name does not mean that therapy is biological; it means that therapy is understood on the analogy of a drug. The model has five components: the client has a disorder, problem or complaint; there is a psychological explanation of it; there is a mechanism of change; the therapy has specific ingredients that act on that mechanism; and the specific ingredients are what produce the benefit (specificity). Cognitive therapy for depression fits the model well: the disorder is depression, the explanation is distorted thinking, the mechanism is cognitive change, and the specific ingredients are the techniques for testing thoughts.

The second is the contextual model, built on Frank. It treats psychotherapy as a special case of a social healing practice, which benefits clients through capacities that human beings, the most social of species, have evolved for helping one another. Before anything else, an initial therapeutic relationship must form: within the first meeting the client decides whether this stranger is trustworthy, competent and willing to understand him, judging partly from the therapist's manner, dress and room. (More clients drop out after the first session than at any other point, Section 8.10.) Once that bond exists, therapy works through three pathways, shown in Figure 8.4.

  1. The real relationship. The real relationship, in the definition of Charles J. Gelso, is "the personal relationship between therapist and patient marked by the extent to which each is genuine with the other and perceives/experiences the other in ways that befit the other" (Section 3.3 traced the idea from Greenson to Gelso and Carter's tripartite model). Therapy is an unusual relationship: it is confidential, and the client can disclose shameful things without losing the bond. For a lonely or isolated client, simply having a genuine human connection with an empathic person is health-promoting.
  2. Expectations. Clients arrive with their own explanation of their distress, a "folk psychology" shaped by their culture, which usually offers no way out ("I am weak", "it is my fate"). Therapy provides an adaptive explanation that comes with something to do, and when the client accepts it, he comes to expect that by carrying out the tasks of therapy he will be able to cope. This is Frank's mastery, Bandura's self-efficacy and Kirsch's response expectancy at work. It requires agreement on the goals and tasks of therapy, two components of the alliance (Section 8.5).
  3. Specific ingredients that promote healthy actions. Every cogent therapy has specific ingredients, and they matter, but not, in this model, because each repairs one particular deficit. They matter because they get the client to do something healthy: think about the world less rigidly (CBT), improve relationships (interpersonal and dynamic therapies), accept himself (acceptance and self-compassion therapies), express difficult emotions (emotion-focused therapy) or take another person's perspective (mentalisation-based therapy).

The value of setting the two models side by side is that they make different predictions, and Wampold and Imel gathered meta-analytic evidence on each. Table 8.6 summarises the contest; the numbers are effect sizes, with Cohen's benchmarks of 0.2 (small), 0.5 (medium) and 0.8 (large) from Section 8.2.

Table 8.6 The medical model and the contextual model: what each predicts and what the evidence shows (effect sizes from Wampold's 2015 summary).
QuestionMedical model predictsContextual model predictsEvidence
Do bona fide therapies differ?Yes, the most potent ingredients winLittle, if each prompts healthy actionAt most about 0.2 (Section 8.3)
Does removing the "active" ingredient matter?Yes, outcome worsensLittleDismantling about 0.01
Does faithful delivery predict outcome?YesLittleAdherence 0.04; competence 0.14
Do relationship factors predict outcome?Only as backgroundStronglyAlliance 0.57; empathy 0.63; goal consensus 0.72
Do therapists differ within one treatment?LittleSubstantially0.35 in trials; 0.55 in routine practice
Does adapting therapy to the client's culture help?No reason it shouldYesAbout 0.32
Do expectations predict outcome?IncidentalYesAbout 0.24

On this scorecard the contextual model wins most rounds, and that is how Wampold answered Donald Klein (Section 8.3). Psychotherapy is not "merely a placebo", because a placebo is designed to lack a cogent explanation and healthy actions, whereas a real therapy delivers both through a real relationship; that structurally equivalent placebos do almost as well shows only that they are almost therapy. Wampold's 2007 paper put the conclusion in its title: psychotherapy is "the humanistic (and effective) treatment".

The scorecard needs two warnings, though. First, most of the common-factor numbers come from correlational studies, in which the alliance or empathy is measured and then correlated with outcome, whereas most of the specific-factor numbers come from experiments that manipulate a component. A correlation can run the wrong way: clients who are already improving may rate the alliance more highly. Wampold argued that the best longitudinal studies show the alliance predicting later change even after earlier improvement is controlled, and Section 8.5 examines that argument; but the two kinds of number are not quite like with like. Second, the scorecard averages across problems, so it says nothing against the disorder-specific findings of Section 8.3. Exposure for OCD is not refuted by a meta-analysis of depression. Specific-effects researchers would add that the contextual model, like the dodo verdict, is most persuasive where the specific techniques for a disorder have least to offer.

Exam trap

Wampold's contextual model (The Great Psychotherapy Debate, 2001; second edition 2015 with Zac Imel) has an initial therapeutic relationship followed by three pathways: (1) the real relationship, (2) expectations created by an explanation the client accepts, and (3) specific ingredients that promote healthy actions. His medical model is a model of how therapy works (disorder, explanation, mechanism, specific ingredients, specificity), not the biomedical model of mental illness. The real relationship (genuineness and realistic perception) is not the same as the working alliance (agreement on goals and tasks plus a bond, Section 8.5).

What common factors mean for practice

It is easy to hear "common factors" as "the vague, nice things therapists do alongside the real work". The research of this section says the opposite: these are among the most powerful things a therapist does, and they can be learned, practised and monitored. Translated into practice, the evidence suggests six habits.

  1. Build the bond first. The client decides early whether to come back, so the first session is spent listening, understanding and showing competence, not rushing to technique.
  2. Give an explanation the client can accept. A good rationale is plausible, fits the client's culture and points to something he can do. If it clashes with his world view, it will not create hope, however true it is.
  3. Remoralise honestly. Hope is therapeutic, but it must be realistic: the evidence (Section 8.2) allows you to say that therapy gives good odds, not a guarantee.
  4. Choose procedures that get the client acting, and use specific techniques where they matter. For OCD, phobias, panic and childhood conduct problems the specific techniques of Section 8.3 are not optional extras.
  5. Recruit the 40 percent. Family, friends, work, faith and the client's own strengths do more of the healing than any technique, so therapy should mobilise them.
  6. Monitor and adjust. Because therapists differ and alliances break down, check progress and the relationship session by session (Sections 8.5, 8.6 and 8.9).

Common factors also provide one of the routes to combining therapies, the common-factors approach to integration described in Section 8.11. And they carry a danger worth naming. "All therapies work through common factors" can become an excuse for never learning the specific treatments that some disorders need, which is exactly what Rachman and Wilson warned against. The mature position holds both ideas at once: the relationship is the vehicle, and the technique is part of what the vehicle carries. The next two sections look more closely at the two biggest common factors, the alliance itself (Section 8.5) and the therapist who forms it (Section 8.6).

Clinical link

Geeta, a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru, has been low and tearful since her mother's death a year ago, and her mother-in-law believes she has been affected by nazar (the evil eye). A common-factors reading of her first session would be this. Meet her with warmth and full attention, and let the family see that you take the problem seriously (relationship and setting). Offer an explanation she and her family can accept, for example that grief and exhaustion have drained her, and that she has withdrawn from the things that used to restore her, without ridiculing the family's beliefs (rationale). Agree on one small, concrete step, such as resuming her evening walk with a friend, and review it next week (ritual, healthy action and a chance for mastery). Whatever specific therapy follows, you have begun to remoralise her.

Exam trap

Match the name to the common-factors idea. Rosenzweig (1936): three implicit common factors, the dodo epigraph. Rogers (1957): necessary and sufficient conditions (Section 6.2). Frank (1961): demoralisation; relationship, setting, myth, ritual. Grencavage and Norcross (1990): 89 common factors in five groups. Lambert (1992): 40, 30, 15, 15. Howard and colleagues (1993): remoralisation, remediation, rehabilitation. Wampold (2001): medical model against contextual model, three pathways. Bordin (1979): the working alliance (Section 8.5).

Scan Your Brain

  2. Luborsky, Singer and Luborsky (1975) called the finding that most comparisons between psychotherapies ended in a draw the effect.
  3. A treatment intended to be therapeutic, delivered by a trained therapist and based on psychological principles, is a psychotherapy; a straw-man comparison treatment is an control.
  4. Wampold and colleagues (1997) put the upper bound of the difference between bona fide therapies at an effect size of about .
