8.5 The Therapeutic Alliance and the Core ConditionsHigh yield · 4 Qs

Sections 8.3 and 8.4 left you with a puzzle: if therapies built on contradictory theories produce broadly similar results, the healing must lie partly in what they share. Of all the shared ingredients, the one that has been studied most is the relationship between the client and the therapist. Every clinician knows this from experience. Picture Rekha, a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Bengaluru with depression, referred for cognitive behaviour therapy. Suppose she sees one therapist, does her homework, feels understood and recovers in twelve sessions. Suppose that in a parallel world she sees a second therapist, equally well trained and following the same manual, and leaves after three sessions saying, "She doesn't really get what my life is like." The manual is identical in both worlds. The relationship is not.

Turning that ordinary observation into science required three steps: a definition of the relationship that did not belong to any one school, a way of measuring it, and studies that tested whether it predicts outcome. This section follows those steps for the therapeutic alliance, and then asks the same questions about the older relationship variables, Carl Rogers' core conditions, and about the newer idea of the real relationship.

From friendly transference to the helping alliance

The idea is older than the word. Sigmund Freud noticed early that his patients' cooperation depended on their feelings towards him. In "The dynamics of transference" (1912) he separated the troublesome forms of transference, erotic and hostile, from the patient's friendly and affectionate feelings towards the analyst, which could be admitted to consciousness and which he treated as the vehicle of success in analysis; later analysts called these feelings the "unobjectionable positive transference". In "On beginning the treatment" (1913) he wrote that the first aim of analysis is to attach the patient to the treatment and to the person of the doctor, which takes time, a serious interest in the patient and sympathetic understanding. Freud was saying, in other words, that nothing technical can happen until a working tie exists.

Section 3.3 tells the psychoanalytic story that followed. Richard Sterba (1934) described the split between the patient's experiencing ego and the observing ego that allies itself with the analyst. Elizabeth R. Zetzel coined the term therapeutic alliance in 1956 for the patient's capacity to use the healthy part of the ego to join the analyst in the work. Ralph R. Greenson (1911 to 1979) named the working alliance in 1965: the relatively rational, non-neurotic rapport that lets a patient keep working even when the transference is stormy. All of these were defined against transference. The alliance was the realistic, conscious part of the relationship; the transference was the distorted, unconscious part.

The first researcher to make the idea measurable was Lester Luborsky, whom you met in Section 8.1 as one of Eysenck's first critics. Working at the University of Pennsylvania, Luborsky (1976) read transcripts of psychodynamic therapy and counted the signs that patient and therapist were working well together. He proposed the helping alliance, which develops in two phases. In the Type 1 alliance, typical of the early sessions, the patient experiences the therapist as warm, supportive and a potent source of help. In the Type 2 alliance, which develops later, the patient comes to feel that the two of them are working together in a joint struggle against what is impeding the patient, and invests in the process of therapy itself. Although Luborsky's thinking was psychodynamic, nothing in his definition required a psychodynamic therapy, and his group's Penn Helping Alliance scales were the first of the modern alliance measures.

Exam trap

Match the term to its author. Freud (1912): friendly, affectionate ("unobjectionable") positive transference. Zetzel (1956): coined "therapeutic alliance". Greenson (1965): named the "working alliance" (and later the "real relationship", Section 8.5.7). Luborsky (1976): the "helping alliance", Type 1 (therapist experienced as supportive) and Type 2 (working together in a joint struggle). Bordin (1979): the pantheoretical working alliance of goals, tasks and bond. "Alliance", "working alliance", "helping alliance" and "therapeutic alliance" are used almost interchangeably in modern research.

Bordin's working alliance: goals, tasks and bond

The decisive step came from a counselling psychologist rather than an analyst. Edward S. Bordin (died 1992), who directed the counselling centre at the University of Michigan for more than thirty years, published "The generalizability of the psychoanalytic concept of the working alliance" in the journal Psychotherapy: Theory, Research and Practice in 1979. His argument was that the working alliance between the person who seeks change and the person who offers to be a change agent is one of the keys, perhaps the key, to change in every kind of therapy, and that it can be described without any reference to the unconscious. Bordin defined the working alliance as a collaborative stance made of three components (Figure 8.5).

  • Agreement on goals. Client and therapist agree about what the therapy is for: which problems are to change and what a good outcome would look like.
  • Agreement on tasks. They agree about the activities of therapy, that is, what each of them will do in and between sessions to reach the goals (free association, keeping thought records, exposure exercises, role-plays, talking about the past or practising new behaviour). Each must see the tasks as relevant and feel able to carry them out.
  • The bond. There is a personal attachment between them, made of mutual trust, liking, respect and confidence, strong enough to carry the client through the difficult parts of the work.

Two further ideas in Bordin's paper are worth knowing. First, different therapies make different demands on the alliance. Psychoanalysis asks the patient to tolerate frustration, silence and painful interpretations, so it needs a deep bond; behaviour therapy asks the client to carry out exposure tasks that are frightening, so agreement on tasks matters most. The strength of an alliance therefore depends on the fit between what a particular therapy requires and what a particular client and therapist can offer. Second, Bordin later argued that strains in the alliance are inevitable and that the cycle of strain and repair is itself one of the ways in which clients change, an idea that Safran and Muran developed into a research programme (Section 8.5.5).

A worked example shows why the three components have to be assessed separately. Arjun, a 26-year-old software engineer in Hyderabad, has obsessive-compulsive disorder with fears of contamination (the disorder itself is described in the Disorders book). He likes his therapist from the first session: she is warm, she listens, and she does not laugh at his fears. The bond is strong. But Arjun's goal is "to stop the thoughts completely", whereas his therapist's goal is to help him tolerate the thoughts without washing. And the central task of the treatment, exposure and response prevention (Chapter 4), strikes him as absurd and dangerous: why would anyone touch a dustbin on purpose? With a strong bond but weak agreement on goals and tasks, the alliance as a whole is fragile, and Arjun is at risk of politely dropping out. The repair is not more warmth. It is a frank conversation about what he wants, a revised goal they can both accept (fewer hours lost to washing, rather than no thoughts at all), and a rationale for exposure that makes sense to him, the "rationale" that Jerome Frank counted among the common factors (Section 8.4).

Because Bordin's alliance is defined by these features rather than by any theory, it is often taught in social work and counselling as the working relationship, and an older formulation lists its characteristics as a realistic, collaborative contract to work together, an observable ability of the worker and the client to work together, and a committed belief in the helping relationship. What it does not need is a psychodynamic theory base. Its origins are psychoanalytic, but its modern form is pantheoretical: it applies equally to a cognitive therapist, a family therapist and a counsellor in a college. Fittingly, it was its school-free definition that made it the most researched relationship variable in psychotherapy.

Exam trap

Bordin's three components are goals, tasks and bond, two agreements and one emotional tie. Do not substitute Rogers' trio (empathy, unconditional positive regard, congruence) or the psychodynamic pair (transference, countertransference). Three questions are built on this: (1) the "working alliance" is the collaborative relationship and bond between clinician and client, focused on the tasks and goals of therapy or assessment, not a score on an outcome measure, not agreement between two clinicians on a diagnosis, and not the client's ability to do homework alone; (2) a working relationship has all of a realistic collaborative contract, an observable ability to work together and a committed belief in helping, but not a psychodynamic theory base; (3) Bordin's alliance is not a key concept of Rogers' client-centred therapy (conditions of worth, unconditional positive regard and the actualising tendency are), and person-centred therapy is the approach least likely to use the term, preferring to speak of the real relationship and the core conditions (Sections 6.2 and 8.5.7).

Measuring the alliance

A concept becomes a research variable only when it can be measured reliably. The measure that made Bordin's alliance the centre of process-outcome research was the Working Alliance Inventory (WAI), published in 1989 by Adam O. Horvath and Leslie S. Greenberg. The WAI turns Bordin's definition directly into items: 36 statements, each rated on a seven-point scale, divided into three subscales of 12 items each, one for goals, one for tasks and one for the bond. A client completes items of the kind "My therapist and I agree about the things I will need to do in therapy to help improve my situation" or "I believe my therapist likes me". There are parallel forms for the therapist, who rates the same relationship from the other chair, and for trained observers, who rate recordings of sessions.

Thirty-six items is a lot to ask of a client after every session, so short versions followed. Terence Tracey and Anna Kokotovic (1989) used factor analysis to pick four items per subscale, producing the 12-item WAI short form (WAI-S). Robert Hatcher and J. Arthur Gillaspy (2006) re-examined the items and produced the 12-item revised short form (WAI-SR), rated on a five-point scale, which is now one of the most widely used client measures. In the 2018 meta-analysis described below, about seven in ten of the new studies used an inventory based on WAI items.

The WAI was not the only measure. Luborsky's group at Pennsylvania developed the Penn Helping Alliance scales, including the Helping Alliance Questionnaire (HAq) and its 1996 revision, the HAq-II. Charles Marmar, Louise Gaston and their colleagues in California developed the California Psychotherapy Alliance Scales (CALPAS), which include the patient's working capacity and commitment as well as the therapist's understanding and involvement. Hans Strupp's group at Vanderbilt University developed observer scales for rating alliance-related behaviour in recorded sessions. Table 8.7 sets out the main instruments.

Table 8.7 The main measures of the therapeutic alliance.
MeasureDeveloped byFormatWhat to remember
Working Alliance Inventory (WAI)Horvath and Greenberg (1989)36 items, 7-point scale; client, therapist and observer formsBuilt on Bordin; Goal, Task and Bond subscales of 12 items each
WAI short form (WAI-S)Tracey and Kokotovic (1989)12 itemsFour items from each subscale
WAI short form revised (WAI-SR)Hatcher and Gillaspy (2006)12 items, 5-point scaleNow among the most used client measures
Helping Alliance Questionnaire (HAq, HAq-II)Luborsky and colleagues, PennsylvaniaClient and therapist questionnairesGrew out of Luborsky's Type 1 and Type 2 helping alliance
California Psychotherapy Alliance Scales (CALPAS)Marmar, Gaston and colleaguesClient, therapist and observer formsIncludes the client's working capacity and commitment
Vanderbilt scalesStrupp's group, VanderbiltObserver ratings of recorded sessionsRates alliance-related behaviour in the session

The measures overlap less than you might expect: the well-established instruments share less than half of their variance with one another. When Hatcher and Barends (1996) analysed the WAI, the CALPAS and the HAq together, the theme they had in common was a "confident collaborative relationship", which is a good one-line definition of the alliance to carry into an interview.

Whose rating counts? The alliance belongs to a relationship, so client, therapist and observer each see part of it, and their ratings agree only moderately. Therapists tend to rate the alliance lower than clients do, particularly early in therapy, and they are not especially good at knowing how their clients see it. The early meta-analyses found that client and observer ratings predicted outcome a little better than therapist ratings; in the 2018 meta-analysis the differences were small (clients about .25, observers and therapists about .22). The practical lesson is that if you want to know how the alliance stands, ask the client, preferably on a short questionnaire that makes it easy to be honest.

Exam trap

The Working Alliance Inventory is by Horvath and Greenberg (1989), not by Bordin, who supplied the theory. It has 36 items and three subscales (Goal, Task, Bond); the short forms have 12. The Helping Alliance Questionnaire belongs to Luborsky. Do not confuse alliance measures with the measures of Rogers' conditions (Truax and Carkhuff's rating scales, the Barrett-Lennard Relationship Inventory, Section 8.5.6) or with Stephenson's Q-sort, which measures the self-concept (Section 6.2).

What the alliance predicts

Once there were measures, the question could be put directly: do clients with stronger alliances do better? Because the alliance cannot be assigned at random, the answer has to come from correlations between alliance scores, usually collected early in therapy, and outcome scores at the end. Hundreds of such studies now exist, and they have been combined in a series of meta-analyses (the method is explained in Section 8.2).

Key study: the alliance meta-analyses, from Horvath and Symonds (1991) to Flückiger and colleagues (2018)

Question: Across all forms of therapy, how strongly does the alliance predict outcome? Method: Horvath and Dianne Symonds (1991) combined the 24 studies then available, based on 20 data sets (published in the Journal of Counseling Psychology); later reviews added studies as they appeared, ending with Christoph Flückiger, A. C. Del Re, Bruce Wampold and Horvath (2018), who analysed 295 independent studies covering more than 30,000 patients, published between 1978 and 2017. Result: The correlation has stayed remarkably stable: r = .26 in 1991, .22 across 79 studies (Martin, Garske and Davis, 2000), .28 across 190 studies (Horvath and colleagues, 2011) and .278 in 2018 (equivalent to d of about 0.58). Only 2 percent of the 295 correlations were negative. The figure for internet-based therapy was almost identical (r = .275). Conclusion: The alliance is a moderate, robust predictor of outcome across therapies, measures, raters, disorders and countries. Criticism: The studies are correlational, so they cannot by themselves show that the alliance causes improvement.

What does a correlation of about .28 mean? Squared, it says that the alliance accounts for roughly 8 percent of the variance in outcome, which sounds modest. A more intuitive translation is the binomial effect size display devised by Robert Rosenthal (Section 8.2) and Donald Rubin in 1982, which turns a correlation into a difference in success rates: the success rate in the "high" group is 50 plus half the correlation (expressed as a percentage), and in the "low" group 50 minus half. For r = .28 this gives 50 + 14 = 64 percent against 50 minus 14 = 36 percent. In plain words, if you imagine clients split into those with stronger and weaker alliances, about 64 percent of the first group would have a good outcome against about 36 percent of the second. That is a large practical difference, and it is larger than the typical difference between two bona fide treatments (Section 8.3).

The association is also remarkably even across schools. In the 2018 meta-analysis the adjusted correlations were about .20 for cognitive behaviour therapy, .24 for psychodynamic therapy, .26 for humanistic therapy and .28 for interpersonal therapy, and the differences between them were not significant. The alliance is not a humanistic luxury that matters only in "talking" therapies; it predicts outcome in the most structured, technique-driven treatments as well.

Timing matters in two ways. First, the alliance measured early, in the first five sessions, already predicts outcome (r about .22 in 2018), which is why clinicians are urged to build it from the first contact and to watch it closely in the first few weeks, when most dropouts happen. Second, alliances measured late in therapy correlate a little more strongly with outcome (about .30), partly because they are measured closer in time to the outcome itself. The alliance also predicts whether clients stay: weaker alliances go with dropout (Section 8.10).

Does the alliance cause improvement, or does improvement cause a good alliance? The second possibility is real. In 1990 Robert DeRubeis and Mary Feeley found, in cognitive therapy for depression, that early symptom change predicted later ratings of the alliance more clearly than early alliance predicted later change: clients who feel better start to like their therapist. If that were the whole story, the alliance would be an epiphenomenon, a by-product of improvement rather than a cause of it. Two kinds of evidence argue against that conclusion. In 66 studies that reported both raw correlations and partial correlations controlling for intake severity and early improvement, the association barely shrank (from about .25 to .22). And session-by-session studies have found that an improvement in the alliance in one session predicts a reduction in symptoms by the next, even within the same client. The careful conclusion of Flückiger and colleagues is that the alliance is a moderate causal facilitative factor for outcome, while admitting that an experiment is impossible: nobody can ethically randomise clients to a deliberately poor alliance.

Exam trap

The alliance-outcome correlation is about r = .28 (Flückiger and colleagues, 2018; 295 studies; more than 30,000 patients), a moderate effect that explains about 8 percent of outcome variance; the first meta-analysis was Horvath and Symonds (1991), r = .26. It holds about equally for CBT, psychodynamic, humanistic and interpersonal therapy and for internet therapy. An option saying the alliance matters "only in psychodynamic or humanistic therapy" is wrong. The early alliance (sessions 1 to 5) already predicts outcome. A correlation is not proof of causation: DeRubeis and Feeley (1990) showed early improvement can strengthen the alliance.

Alliance ruptures and their repair

If the alliance matters this much, what happens when it goes wrong? The most systematic answer comes from Jeremy D. Safran (1952 to 2018) and J. Christopher Muran, who ran the Brief Psychotherapy Research Program at Beth Israel Medical Center in New York. Their book Negotiating the Therapeutic Alliance: A Relational Treatment Guide (2000) is the standard text. They defined an alliance rupture as a deterioration in the alliance, shown by disagreement between client and therapist about the goals of treatment, a lack of collaboration on its tasks, or a strain in the emotional bond: Bordin's three components, seen from the side of failure. Ruptures are not rare accidents. They range from a moment of tension that passes in seconds to a breakdown that ends therapy, and nearly every course of therapy contains some.

Safran and Muran adopted a distinction first drawn by Harper in 1989 between two broad kinds of rupture, which you can remember by the direction in which the client moves (Figure 8.6).

  • A withdrawal rupture is one in which the client moves away from the therapist or the work. The markers include going silent or giving minimal answers, talking in vague or abstract terms, abruptly shifting the topic, telling a painful story with no feeling (a split between content and affect), denying a feeling that is plainly present, sinking into self-criticism and hopelessness that shuts the therapist out, and, most deceptively, being overly compliant: agreeing with everything, deferring and appeasing.
  • A confrontation rupture is one in which the client moves against the therapist or the work. The markers include complaining about the therapist, about the activities of therapy (the homework, the exercises), about the lack of progress or about the arrangements (fees, timings), rejecting the therapist's interventions, defending oneself against perceived criticism, and trying to control the session or pressuring the therapist for a quick fix.

Withdrawal ruptures are the more common and the easier to miss, because a withdrawn client may look like a cooperative one. Confrontation ruptures are more uncomfortable for the therapist, and in a study using Safran and Muran's observer measure, the Rupture Resolution Rating System (3RS), confrontation markers in early sessions of cognitive behaviour therapy predicted dropout (Catherine Eubanks and colleagues, 2019).

How is a rupture repaired? Safran and Muran distinguish two families of strategy. Immediate (or direct) strategies get the work back on track without making the rupture itself the topic: the therapist clarifies a misunderstanding, explains the rationale for a task again, or changes a task or goal that is not working. Expressive (or exploratory) strategies make the rupture the focus: the therapist invites the client to talk about what has just happened between them. The key skill here is metacommunication, communicating about the communication: "I notice that when I suggested the exposure exercise you went quiet and then said 'okay, fine'. I wonder whether something about what I said didn't sit right with you?" Metacommunication lets both people step out of the interaction they are caught in, which Safran and Muran called "disembedding", and look at it together. It works only if the therapist is genuinely curious and non-defensive and is willing to acknowledge his or her own part in the rupture.

Using the task-analytic method (studying many recorded resolution events to build and then test a model), Safran and Muran described in 1996 the typical course of a successful resolution: the therapist notices the rupture marker; steps back and draws attention to what is happening between them; explores with the client what the client experienced; explores what made it hard for the client to express this directly (the avoidance); and finally helps the client reach the wish or need underneath. The two kinds of rupture end in different places. A withdrawn client moves from vague, qualified statements towards clear self-assertion ("I didn't want to do that exercise, and I didn't feel I could say so"). A confronting client moves from anger and blame towards the disappointment and vulnerability underneath ("I felt you weren't really listening, and I'm afraid nobody can help me"). In both cases the repaired alliance is often stronger than before, and the client has had a corrective experience of a relationship in which conflict did not lead to rejection.

The evidence is encouraging but smaller than for the alliance itself. A meta-analysis by Safran, Muran and Catherine Eubanks-Carter in 2011 found only three studies relating rupture repair to outcome (r = .24). The update by Eubanks, Muran and Safran in 2018 found 11 studies with 1,314 patients, and a moderate association between rupture resolution and good outcome (r = .29). A second meta-analysis in the same paper looked at whether training therapists in rupture resolution improved their clients' outcomes and found no significant effect (r = .11, six studies), so the skill matters but is not easily taught in a short course. Safran and Muran also built a treatment around these ideas, brief relational therapy, which puts the negotiation of the here-and-now relationship at the centre of the work.

Indian context

Withdrawal ruptures can be especially hard to see in Indian settings. A client who has grown up to respect elders, doctors and teachers may never say "I disagree" to a therapist, particularly an older or senior one. Disagreement shows up instead as a polite "ji, haan, I'll try", homework not done, a family function that suddenly clashes with the next appointment, or simply not coming back. It helps to invite disagreement openly ("Many people find this exercise strange; tell me honestly what you think of it") and to ask about the relationship at the end of each session, so that the client has a permitted way to say no.

Exam trap

Ruptures and repair are the work of Safran and Muran (Negotiating the Therapeutic Alliance, 2000). Do not confuse this with Safran and Segal's earlier book on interpersonal process in cognitive therapy (1990). Withdrawal = moving away (silence, minimal response, topic shifts, abstract talk, and over-compliance); confrontation = moving against (complaints, rejecting tasks, pressure). An "excessively agreeable" client is showing a withdrawal rupture, not a strong alliance. Metacommunication is talking about what is happening between therapist and client. Rupture repair correlates with outcome at about r = .29 (Eubanks, Muran and Safran, 2018).

Rogers' core conditions as evidence

Long before the alliance was measured, Carl Rogers had made the boldest claim ever offered about the therapy relationship. Section 6.2 sets out his 1957 hypothesis that six conditions are necessary and sufficient for constructive personality change, and explains the three that the therapist provides, the core conditions: accurate empathic understanding, unconditional positive regard and congruence. Do not re-learn the definitions here. What concerns this chapter is that Rogers' claim was a scientific hypothesis, and one that could be tested: if the conditions matter, clients whose therapists offer more of them should improve more. Section 6.2 also describes the early Chicago studies with the Q-sort and the disappointing Wisconsin project with hospitalised patients; this section follows the evidence from there.

The first systematic tests used rating scales. Charles B. Truax and Robert R. Carkhuff built scales on which trained judges rated short segments of recorded sessions for accurate empathy, non-possessive warmth (their term for unconditional positive regard) and genuineness, and their book Toward Effective Counseling and Psychotherapy (1967) reported that clients whose therapists offered high levels of these conditions improved more, while those whose therapists offered low levels could actually deteriorate. Carkhuff went on to build a training programme in "human relations" skills on these scales. A second tradition measured the conditions as the client perceives them. Rogers' student Godfrey T. Barrett-Lennard (1962) developed the Barrett-Lennard Relationship Inventory, a questionnaire on which the client rates the therapist's level of regard, empathic understanding, unconditionality of regard and congruence. Rogers himself had said that the conditions work only if the client perceives them (his sixth condition), so client-perceived measures are closest to his theory.

Truax also produced an awkward finding for the non-directive ideal. Analysing recordings of Rogers' own sessions in 1966, he found that Rogers responded with more empathy and warmth to some kinds of client statements than to others, and that the client statements he favoured became more frequent over the course of therapy. Even the most non-directive therapist, it seemed, was selectively reinforcing his client (Chapter 4 explains reinforcement).

The early claims did not survive closer scrutiny intact. In 1977 Kevin Mitchell, Jerold Bozarth and Conrad Krauft reappraised the studies and concluded that the relation between the conditions and outcome was much weaker and less consistent than Truax and Carkhuff had said; in 1978 Michael Lambert, Stephen DeJulio and David Stein reached a similar verdict in the Psychological Bulletin, pointing to unreliable ratings and small samples. For a while it looked as though the core conditions might be one of psychotherapy's comfortable myths.

The modern meta-analyses, prepared for the APA task force described in Section 8.5.8 and published in 2018, restored them, though at a realistic size (Table 8.8). Robert Elliott, Arthur Bohart, Jeanne Watson and David Murphy combined 82 samples with 6,138 clients and found that empathy is a moderately strong predictor of outcome (r = .28), holding across theoretical orientations and presenting problems. Importantly, empathy as perceived by the client, or rated by observers or therapists, predicted outcome better than tests of the therapist's "empathic accuracy" in guessing exactly what the client felt: what heals is feeling understood, not being read correctly. Barry Farber and his colleagues at Teachers College, Columbia University, combined 64 studies of positive regard and found a small positive association with outcome (an effect size of 0.28, expressed as Hedges' g, which is a standardised mean difference rather than a correlation). Gregory Kolden and colleagues combined 21 studies with 1,192 patients and found that congruence or genuineness correlated .23 with outcome.

Table 8.8 Relationship elements and outcome: the 2018 meta-analyses and the verdicts of the third APA task force (2019).
ElementMeta-analysisStudiesAssociation with outcomeTask force verdict
Alliance (adult individual therapy)Flückiger, Del Re, Wampold and Horvath295 studies, over 30,000 patientsr = .28Demonstrably effective
EmpathyElliott, Bohart, Watson and Murphy82 samples, 6,138 clientsr = .28Demonstrably effective
Positive regard and affirmationFarber, Suzuki and Lynch64 studiesg = .28 (small)Demonstrably effective
Congruence or genuinenessKolden and colleagues21 studies, 1,192 patientsr = .23Probably effective
Real relationshipGelso, Kivlighan and Markin16 studies, 1,502 patientsr = .38Probably effective
Repairing alliance rupturesEubanks, Muran and Safran11 studies, 1,314 patientsr = .29Probably effective

So were the conditions necessary and sufficient, as Rogers claimed? The evidence supports a more modest statement: they are facilitative. They predict outcome in every kind of therapy, not just person-centred therapy, and the effects are as large as those of most technical factors. But they are not strictly necessary, since people improve with self-help books and therapist-free internet programmes, and with technically skilled therapists who are not especially warm; and they are probably not sufficient for every problem, since a severe obsessive-compulsive disorder or a specific phobia usually needs exposure as well. There is a measurement problem, too. Empathy, positive regard and the alliance bond overlap heavily, and a client who rates the therapist as empathic is often also a client who is improving, so the same chicken-and-egg question that dogs the alliance applies here. The fair conclusion is that Rogers was right that the relationship matters in every therapy and wrong that nothing else does.

Exam trap

Truax and Carkhuff (1967) rated accurate empathy, non-possessive warmth and genuineness from recordings; the Barrett-Lennard Relationship Inventory (1962) measures the conditions as the client perceives them. The modern verdict (2018): empathy r = .28, positive regard g = .28, congruence r = .23, so the conditions are facilitative across all therapies but not shown to be either necessary or sufficient. Client-perceived empathy predicts outcome better than "empathic accuracy". The conditions themselves are taught in Section 6.2.

The real relationship

The third strand of the relationship is the one that person-centred therapists care most about. Greenson coined the term real relationship in psychoanalysis for the genuine, undistorted, person-to-person bond between analyst and patient (Section 3.3). It was brought into modern research by the counselling psychologist Charles J. Gelso of the University of Maryland, who with Jean Carter proposed in 1985 that every therapeutic relationship, in every school, contains three parts: a working alliance, a transference configuration and a real relationship (the tripartite model drawn in Figure 3.2).

Gelso defines the real relationship as the personal relationship between client and therapist marked by the extent to which each is genuine with the other and perceives and experiences the other realistically, that is, as the person he or she actually is rather than through the lens of transference. A real relationship has two dimensions: its magnitude (how much genuineness and realism there is) and its valence (how positive the feelings are). It is different from the alliance: the alliance is about working together on a task, whereas the real relationship is about who the two people are to each other. A client can have a good working alliance with a therapist he does not much like as a person, and can like a therapist as a person while disagreeing with what she asks him to do.

Gelso and his colleagues developed the Real Relationship Inventory, with client and therapist forms, and a meta-analysis by Gelso, Dennis Kivlighan and Rayna Markin in 2018 combined 16 studies with 1,502 patients and found a moderate association with outcome (r = .38), whether the client or the therapist rated the real relationship. This is the largest correlation in Table 8.8, but it rests on far fewer studies than the alliance, so it should be read with caution.

Exam trap

The real relationship was named by Greenson in psychoanalysis; Gelso (with Carter, 1985) made it a research variable present in every therapy. It is defined by genuineness and realism, and differs from the working alliance (collaboration on goals and tasks) and from transference (distortion carried over from the past). Because person-centred therapists prefer the language of the real relationship and the core conditions, person-centred therapy is the keyed answer to "which approach is least likely to use the term therapeutic alliance?"

Clinical link

The research on relationships has a practical message for a trainee: monitor the relationship as deliberately as you monitor symptoms. A two-minute question at the end of a session ("How was today for you? Was there anything I said that didn't fit, or anything we should do differently?") catches withdrawal ruptures that would otherwise end in silent dropout, and a short client-rated alliance scale such as the WAI-SR in the first few sessions tells you whether goals and tasks are really shared. Interviewers at clinical programmes often ask "What makes therapy work?"; an answer that names Bordin's three components, the r of about .28, and rupture repair shows you know the evidence, not just the slogans.

The APA task forces on evidence-based relationships

In the 1990s the American Psychological Association's Division 12 (Clinical Psychology) began publishing lists of "empirically validated" (later "empirically supported") treatments, defined by manuals and randomised trials (the Chambless criteria, Section 8.8). Many researchers felt that the lists treated therapy as if it were a drug delivered by interchangeable technicians, and ignored the relationship through which every treatment is delivered. In response, Division 29 (Psychotherapy) set up a Task Force on Empirically Supported Therapy Relationships, chaired by John C. Norcross (born 1957), which reported in 2001 and published its evidence in the book Psychotherapy Relationships That Work (2002). It judged the alliance, cohesion in group therapy, empathy, and goal consensus and collaboration to be demonstrably effective, and several other elements (among them positive regard, congruence and feedback) to be promising.

Two further task forces followed. The second, an interdivisional task force of Divisions 12 and 29, reported in 2011. The third, the Interdivisional APA Task Force on Evidence-Based Relationships and Responsiveness, published 16 new meta-analyses in a special issue of the journal Psychotherapy in 2018 and a two-volume third edition of the book in 2019, edited by Norcross with Michael Lambert and with Bruce Wampold. Its expert panels judged each relationship element on the strength of the evidence, in three categories.

  • Demonstrably effective (nine elements): the alliance in individual adult psychotherapy, the alliance in child and adolescent therapy, the alliance in couple and family therapy, collaboration, goal consensus, cohesion in group therapy, empathy, positive regard and affirmation, and collecting and delivering client feedback.
  • Probably effective (seven elements): congruence or genuineness, the real relationship, emotional expression, cultivating positive expectations, promoting treatment credibility, managing countertransference, and repairing alliance ruptures.
  • Promising but insufficient research to judge (one element): self-disclosure and immediacy (Section 8.10).

The task forces also asked a second question: which client characteristics should a therapist adapt to? The 2011 task force judged adapting therapy to the client's reactance or resistance level, preferences, culture, and religion and spirituality to be demonstrably effective, and adapting to the client's stage of change and coping style to be probably effective. These are the client variables of Section 8.6.

The task forces' central conclusion was that the therapy relationship makes substantial and consistent contributions to outcome independent of the specific type of treatment, and that it accounts for why clients improve, or fail to improve, at least as much as the particular treatment method does. Notice how carefully that is worded. The task forces did not claim that technique is irrelevant, and the evidence is correlational. In real therapy the two are inseparable: a client does her exposure homework partly because she trusts the therapist who set it, and she trusts the therapist partly because the homework is working. The modern consensus is "relationship and technique", not "relationship versus technique".

Exam trap

Two different APA task forces are easily confused. The Division 12 task force (Chambless, 1995 and 1998) listed empirically supported treatments (Section 8.8). The Division 29 task force chaired by Norcross (2001; book 2002; later editions 2011 and 2019) listed evidence-based therapy relationships. In 2019, empathy and positive regard were "demonstrably effective", congruence and rupture repair "probably effective", and self-disclosure and immediacy only "promising".

8.6 Therapist Effects, Allegiance Effects and Client VariablesMedium yield · 3 Qs

Recall the two uniformity myths that Donald Kiesler attacked in 1966 (Section 8.1): the assumption that the therapists in a study are interchangeable, and the assumption that its patients are alike. Recall too Gordon Paul's question of 1967: what treatment, by whom, is most effective for this individual? Section 8.5 looked at the relationship between therapist and client. This section takes the two people one at a time. First the therapist: how much does it matter which therapist you see, and what makes one therapist better than another? Then a third person who is usually invisible, the researcher who designs the study, and whose loyalties turn out to predict its results. Finally the client: who benefits from therapy, and why do clients so often appear to improve more than they really have?

Therapist effects: some therapists are better than others

Key study: Ricks and the "supershrink" (1974)

Question: Do different therapists in the same clinic produce different outcomes? Method: David F. Ricks followed up, into adulthood, seriously disturbed adolescents who had been treated at the same clinic by two different therapists. Result: Of the 15 adolescents treated by one therapist, 4 (27 percent) went on to develop schizophrenia as adults; of the 13 treated by the other, 11 (85 percent) did. The adolescents had a nickname for the first therapist, "supershrink". This therapist put more effort into the most disturbed cases, supported the young people's autonomy, made use of resources outside therapy and built better relationships with their parents; the other therapist became depressed and had little energy for the most difficult cases. Conclusion: The therapist can matter enormously, especially with the most severely disturbed clients. Criticism: Two therapists and small, non-random samples; a vivid case study rather than a test.

For decades after Ricks, outcome research behaved as though his finding did not exist. Trials compared treatments and treated the therapists who delivered them as interchangeable. A therapist effect is the proportion of the variation in clients' outcomes that is due to which therapist they saw, over and above the treatment and the clients' own characteristics. Measuring it requires care, because clients are nested within therapists: the clients of one therapist share something, namely that therapist. The right tool is a multilevel (hierarchical linear) model, which separates the variance in outcome into a between-therapist part and a within-therapist part; the therapist effect is the between-therapist share (statistically, an intraclass correlation). For the results to generalise to therapists in general, the therapists in a study must be treated as a random sample from the population of therapists. The statistics belong to the Research Methodology book; the idea is what matters here.

The first quantitative summary, by Paul Crits-Christoph and colleagues in 1991, found that across 27 treatment groups therapists accounted for an average of 8.6 percent of the variance in outcome, with a range from 0 to almost half. The most thorough modern review, by Scott A. Baldwin and Zac E. Imel in 2013, examined 46 studies using random-effects models and put the therapist effect at about 5 percent overall: about 3 percent in efficacy trials and about 7 percent in naturalistic studies of routine practice. Trials shrink therapist effects because they select therapists carefully, train them, supervise them, check their adherence to a manual and exclude the most complicated clients (Section 8.7). On average, the most effective therapists in these studies were about twice as effective as the least effective. An update by Robert Johns, Michael Barkham and colleagues in 2019 again found a weighted average of about 5 percent, ranging from almost nothing to 29 percent in different settings, and noted that therapist effects grow with the severity of the clients' problems, exactly as Ricks had observed.

Five percent sounds small until you compare it with the alternative. When bona fide treatments are compared with one another, the differences between them typically account for around 1 percent of the variance or less (Section 8.3). When Dong-Min Kim, Bruce Wampold and Daniel Bolt (2006) re-analysed the psychotherapy conditions of the NIMH depression trial (Section 8.8) with therapists treated as random, they estimated that about 8 percent of the variance in outcome was due to the therapists and none of it to which of the two psychotherapies was delivered, although a re-analysis of the same data by Irene Elkin and colleagues, making different analytic decisions, found no significant therapist effects, and the dispute has not been settled. The same logic applies to medication. McKay, Imel and Wampold (2006) re-analysed the medication arms of that trial, in which psychiatrists gave either imipramine or a placebo with clinical management, and found that the psychiatrist accounted for more of the variance in depression scores (about 9 percent on the Beck Depression Inventory) than the drug did (about 3 percent).

Routine practice shows the spread most vividly. In "Waiting for supershrink" (2003), Okiishi, Lambert and their colleagues analysed 1,841 clients seen by 91 therapists over two and a half years at a university counselling centre, where clients filled in a symptom questionnaire every session. The clients of the most effective therapists improved about ten times faster than the average for the centre; the clients of the least effective therapists, on average, got worse. The therapists' sex, level and type of training, and theoretical orientation (cognitive behavioural, humanistic or psychodynamic) explained very little of this difference.

Exam trap

Therapist effects are about 5 percent of outcome variance on average (Baldwin and Imel, 2013), smaller in trials (about 3 percent) than in routine practice (about 7 percent), and typically larger than the differences between bona fide treatments. The word "supershrink" comes from Ricks (1974); Okiishi and colleagues (2003) titled their counselling-centre study "Waiting for supershrink". The assumption that therapists are interchangeable is Kiesler's therapist uniformity myth (1966). Do not confuse the therapist effect (which therapist) with the allegiance effect (the researcher's loyalty, Section 8.6.3).

What makes an effective therapist?

If some therapists are reliably better than others, the obvious question is what the better ones have. The surprising answer is that most of the things a clinic records about its therapists do not predict their effectiveness. Smith, Glass and Miller (1980) had already found little relation between therapist experience and outcome (Section 8.2), and later research has kept confirming it. The most direct test was a longitudinal study by Simon Goldberg, Tony Rousmaniere, Scott Miller, Bruce Wampold and colleagues (2016), which followed 170 therapists treating 6,591 patients for up to 18 years. On average, the therapists' outcomes did not improve with experience; if anything they declined very slightly as the years and the cases accumulated, although fewer of their clients dropped out early. Profession, level of training, gender and theoretical orientation have likewise shown little consistent relation to outcome, as the Okiishi study found. Even therapists' ratings of their own social skills do not predict their results.

What does predict them is what therapists actually do with difficult people.

Key study: facilitative interpersonal skills (Anderson, Ogles, Patterson, Lambert and Vermeersch, 2009)

Question: Why do therapists in the same counselling centre differ in their results? Method: Timothy Anderson and colleagues studied 25 therapists who had treated 1,141 clients, whose progress had been tracked session by session. Each therapist watched eight brief video scenes of interpersonally difficult moments in therapy, re-enacted by actors from real session transcripts, and spoke a response to each as if in session. Trained judges rated the recorded responses on facilitative interpersonal skills: verbal fluency, emotional expression, persuasiveness, hopefulness, warmth, empathy, the capacity to form an alliance bond, and keeping a focus on the problem. The therapists also rated their own social skills, and their age, sex, training and orientation were recorded. Result: Facilitative interpersonal skills predicted how fast the therapists' clients improved; self-rated social skills, orientation and training did not, and the small advantage of older therapists disappeared once interpersonal skill was taken into account. Conclusion: Part of what separates more effective therapists is their ability to respond well in interpersonally challenging moments. Criticism: One centre, 25 therapists, and a correlational design; later work by Anderson's group has found similar results with trainee therapists.

This finding fits several others. Baldwin, Wampold and Imel (2007) studied 331 patients seen by 80 therapists and separated the alliance into two parts: differences between therapists in the alliances they typically formed, and differences between the patients of the same therapist. Only the therapist part predicted outcome. In other words, the alliance-outcome correlation of Section 8.5.4 is driven largely by the fact that some therapists are better at forming alliances with all kinds of clients. Hans Strupp's group at Vanderbilt found the other side of the coin: when therapists responded to difficult clients with subtly hostile, controlling or dismissive communication, outcomes were worse. Good therapists are not people who never have hard moments; they are people who handle hard moments without becoming defensive, which is the skill of rupture repair (Section 8.5.5).

If experience alone does not make therapists better, can anything? The most promising candidates are routine outcome monitoring (feedback from clients' session-by-session scores, Section 8.9) combined with deliberate practice, the effortful, repeated rehearsal of specific skills just beyond one's current level, with expert feedback, borrowed from research on expertise in music, chess and sport. In an agency in Calgary that built feedback, consultation and deliberate practice into its routine, Goldberg and colleagues (2016) found that outcomes improved slightly but steadily year on year across 153 therapists and 5,128 patients, and that individual therapists improved within their own caseloads. Supervision and training are taken up in Section 8.12.

Clinical link

For a trainee this research is both humbling and hopeful. Years in practice will not by themselves make you better, and neither will your choice of school. What you can work on are the skills in Anderson's list: speaking clearly and warmly, conveying hope without false promises, staying empathic and focused when a client is angry or silent, and noticing and repairing ruptures. Record your sessions (with consent), watch the difficult moments with your supervisor, and track your clients' outcomes with a standard measure instead of trusting your impression of how they are doing.

Exam trap

What does not reliably predict a therapist's effectiveness: years of experience (Goldberg and colleagues, 2016, found a tiny decline), theoretical orientation, training level, profession, gender, self-rated social skill. What does: facilitative interpersonal skills measured on a performance task (Anderson and colleagues, 2009), the ability to form alliances across many clients (Baldwin, Wampold and Imel, 2007), and handling difficult moments without hostility. An option claiming "more experienced therapists get better outcomes" is the bait.

The researcher allegiance effect

There is a third person in every outcome study: the researcher. The researcher allegiance effect is the tendency for the treatment favoured by the investigators (the one they developed, practise, train others in or have written in support of) to come out ahead in their comparative studies. It is different from therapist allegiance, the therapist's own belief in the treatment he or she delivers, which is usually counted among the common factors (Section 8.4). Researcher allegiance is about whose study it is.

Key study: Luborsky and colleagues, "The researcher's own therapy allegiances: a 'wild card' in comparisons of treatment efficacy" (1999)

Question: When two therapies are compared, does the researchers' allegiance predict which one wins? Method: Lester Luborsky, Louis Diguer, Robert Rosenthal and colleagues examined 29 comparative studies of psychotherapies, after eight earlier reviews had hinted at an allegiance trend. They rated allegiance in three ways: from the published paper itself (the usual "reprint" method, judging which treatment the authors favoured from how they wrote about it), from ratings by colleagues who knew the researchers, and from the researchers' own self-ratings. Result: Each measure of allegiance correlated significantly with the outcome differences between the treatments, and together the three measures explained 69 percent of the variance in which treatment did better. Conclusion: Allegiance is a "wild card" that can distort comparisons of treatment efficacy and must be controlled. Criticism: A correlation cannot show whether allegiance biases results or whether researchers become loyal to treatments that genuinely work better.

Later work confirmed the finding on a larger scale. In 2013 Thomas Munder and colleagues combined 30 meta-analyses that had estimated the allegiance-outcome association and found a mean correlation of about .26, robust across treatments, populations and ways of measuring allegiance. With a nice irony, they also found that the association was stronger in meta-analyses whose authors believed in the allegiance hypothesis: allegiance to the idea of allegiance.

How can allegiance produce its effect? Several routes are possible, and none requires dishonesty. The favoured treatment is often delivered by the investigators' own well-trained, enthusiastic therapists, while the comparison is delivered by therapists with less training or less conviction. The comparison treatment may be a weakened version (sometimes called an "intent-to-fail" condition) rather than the treatment as its own advocates would deliver it. Researchers may choose outcome measures that suit their treatment, or report the measures on which it did best. And their expectations may be conveyed, without anyone meaning it, to therapists, clients and assessors, the experimenter expectancy effect that Robert Rosenthal documented in the 1960s. Yet an allegiance association is not automatically a bias, as Leykin and DeRubeis cautioned in 2009: researchers may also come to favour treatments that have genuinely worked in their hands, so the arrow can point both ways.

The remedies follow from the routes. Comparative trials should include investigators allied to each treatment (an "adversarial collaboration"), use therapists who are expert in and committed to each treatment, check that each is delivered competently, use outcome assessors who do not know which treatment a client received (Section 8.7), and register their outcome measures in advance. Because allegiance effects are large enough to account for many of the apparent differences between treatments, they are one of the strongest arguments for the dodo bird verdict (Section 8.3).

Exam trap

The researcher allegiance effect (Luborsky and colleagues, 1999, "a wild card"; 29 studies; allegiance explained 69 percent of the variance in outcome differences) is about the investigator's loyalty, not the client's expectation (expectancy or placebo, Section 8.4), not the therapist's skill (therapist effects), and not cues in the research setting (demand characteristics, Section 8.6.6). The usual way of rating it is the "reprint method", judging allegiance from the published paper.

Client variables: who benefits from therapy?

Whatever the therapist and the researcher bring, it is the client who does the changing. Arthur C. Bohart and Karen Tallman made this the theme of their book How Clients Make Therapy Work (1999): clients are active self-healers who use whatever the therapist offers, sometimes in ways the therapist never intended. Lambert's estimates, which put client and extratherapeutic factors at the top of the list of what produces change, are discussed in Section 8.4. Here the question is which characteristics of the client predict a good outcome.

Two terms help. The prognosis of a problem is the forecast of its likely course and outcome: whether, how far and how fast the person is likely to recover, or to relapse or become chronic. It is different from diagnosis (identifying the condition) and from aetiology (its causes). A prognostic variable predicts outcome whatever treatment is given (for example, more severe and long-standing problems tend to have a poorer prognosis in any therapy). A prescriptive variable, also called a moderator, predicts which treatment will work better for whom (for example, a client's reactance, below). Prognostic variables tell you who will find therapy harder; prescriptive variables tell you what to do about it.

The most famous statement about client variables is less a finding than an accusation. In 1964 William Schofield, a psychologist at the University of Minnesota, published Psychotherapy: The Purchase of Friendship. He argued that psychotherapists, consciously or not, prefer clients who are YAVIS: young, attractive, verbal, intelligent and successful. Such clients resemble therapists themselves, are rewarding to work with, talk easily about their feelings and make the therapist feel competent, and so they are the ones most often accepted for therapy and given the most time and effort, often at the expense of people with much greater need. Schofield's point was critical: the "YAVIS syndrome" describes a bias in who receives psychotherapy, not a law about who can benefit from it. His Minnesota colleague Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003) took it up in his essay "Why I do not attend case conferences" (1973), noting that poorer patients were more likely to be given medication, electroconvulsive therapy or supportive therapy, and better-off patients the intensive psychotherapy that therapists themselves found most interesting. The mocking opposite of YAVIS that appears in some textbooks and exam notes is HOUND (homely, old, unsuccessful, nonverbal and dumb); its origin is obscure, and it is not Schofield's.

Does YAVIS predict outcome? Only weakly. Once people are actually in therapy, demographic characteristics such as age, sex, ethnicity and social class are poor predictors of how much they improve, although they predict who is offered therapy and who drops out. The client variables that do predict outcome are psychological, and the strongest of them have been the subject of their own meta-analyses (Table 8.9).

  • Readiness to change. The further along the stages of change a client is at the start (from precontemplation through contemplation and preparation to action, Section 6.9), the better the outcome; a meta-analysis by Paul Krebs, John Norcross and colleagues (2018) of 76 studies and 25,917 patients found a moderate effect (d = 0.41).
  • Expectations. An outcome expectation is the client's belief about whether therapy will help; a role expectation is the client's belief about what will happen in therapy and what client and therapist will each do. Clients with more optimistic early outcome expectations do better: r = .18 across 81 samples and 12,722 patients (Michael Constantino and colleagues, 2018). Clients whose role expectations clash with what actually happens (a client who expects a doctor to give advice and medicine, and meets a therapist who mostly listens) are more likely to drop out. Albert Bandura's (1977) distinction is useful here. An outcome expectation is a belief that a course of action will lead to a result; a self-efficacy expectation is the person's belief in his or her own capability to organise and carry out the actions needed to reach a specific goal. Self-efficacy is specific to a task, unlike global self-esteem or self-worth; it is not a belief that outcomes depend on fate or chance (that is an external locus of control); and it has nothing to do with Alfred Adler's striving to overcome inferiority. Bandura argued that all therapies work in part by raising self-efficacy.
  • Severity, chronicity and complexity. Clients with more severe, long-standing or complicated problems, including those with coexisting personality disorders, tend to need more sessions and are less likely to reach full recovery, even though, starting from further back, they may show large absolute changes.
  • Social support. Clients with supportive relationships outside therapy tend to do better, partly because the support itself helps and partly because it makes the alliance easier to form.
  • Attachment style. In a meta-analysis of 36 studies with 3,158 patients, Kenneth Levy and colleagues (2018) found that clients securely attached before treatment had better outcomes than insecurely attached clients, and that gains in attachment security during therapy went with better outcomes.
  • Psychological mindedness and motivation. The classic selection criteria for psychodynamic therapy (Section 3.6), the capacity to think about one's own feelings and motives and the wish to change rather than merely to feel better, predict who engages in insight-oriented work.
  • Preferences and reactance. These are prescriptive variables. When clients receive the kind of therapy they prefer, they are less likely to drop out (odds ratio 1.79) and do somewhat better (d = 0.28), according to a meta-analysis of 53 studies by Joshua Swift and colleagues (2018). The term reactance means a person's tendency to resist being controlled or directed; highly reactant clients do much better with a non-directive, reflective therapist than with a directive one (d = 0.79 in a meta-analysis of 13 studies by Larry Beutler and colleagues, 2018), and the reverse is somewhat true for clients low in reactance.
Table 8.9 Client variables and outcome: what the evidence says.
Client variableFindingWhat it means in practice
YAVIS characteristics (Schofield, 1964)Predict who therapists prefer and accept, more than how much clients improveWatch your own biases about whom you take on
Readiness (stage of change)Further along the stages, better outcome (d = 0.41; 76 studies)Match the intervention to the stage (Section 6.9)
Outcome expectationsOptimism predicts outcome (r = .18; 81 samples)Offer a credible rationale and realistic hope
Role expectationsMismatch predicts dropoutExplain early what therapy involves
Severity, chronicity, personality disorderSlower progress, less full recoveryPlan for longer or more intensive help
Attachment securitySecure attachment predicts better outcome (36 studies)Give extra attention to the relationship
PreferencesAccommodation: fewer dropouts (odds ratio 1.79), better outcome (d = 0.28)Ask what the client wants
ReactanceHigh reactance does better with non-directive work (d = 0.79)Be less directive with reactant clients
Exam trap

Schofield's (1964) YAVIS (young, attractive, verbal, intelligent, successful) describes patient characteristics, the clients therapists prefer, so it is not a therapy technique, not a psychiatric illness and not a set of therapist qualities, even though it is called a "syndrome". The book is Psychotherapy: The Purchase of Friendship. YAVIS is about therapists' bias in selecting clients, not proof that YAVIS clients benefit more. Prognosis means the likely course and outcome of a disorder, not its history, its immediate symptoms or its diagnostic criteria. Self-efficacy (Bandura) is belief in one's capability to carry out the actions needed for a specific goal, not self-worth, not fate or chance, and not Adler's striving against inferiority.

Matching clients to treatments

Paul's question implies that the best treatment depends on the client, and the obvious research strategy is to look for interactions: client characteristics that make one treatment work better than another. Educational psychologists had already named this idea. Lee Cronbach and Richard Snow (1977) called it the aptitude-treatment interaction: a method of teaching that suits one kind of student may not suit another, so the right question is not "which method is best?" but "which method is best for which student?" Applied to therapy, the "aptitudes" are client variables of the prescriptive kind described above.

The most developed matching scheme is systematic treatment selection, proposed by Larry E. Beutler and John Clarkin in 1990. Beutler argued that therapists should select and adjust their interventions according to a few client dimensions that research had shown to matter, among them functional impairment, subjective distress, reactance and coping style. Coping style is the client's habitual way of handling distress: externalising clients act out, blame others and seek stimulation, while internalising clients withdraw, blame themselves and ruminate. The predicted matches are that externalising clients do better with symptom-focused, skill-building interventions and internalising clients with insight-oriented and interpersonal work, and that, as above, highly reactant clients do better with less directive approaches. The 2011 APA task force rated adaptation to reactance as demonstrably effective and adaptation to coping style as probably effective (Section 8.5.8).

Large-scale tests of matching have been sobering. The biggest of them, Project MATCH, randomised more than 1,700 people with alcohol problems to three different treatments specifically to test a series of predicted client-treatment matches, and found very few (Section 8.8). The lesson most researchers have drawn is not that client differences are unimportant, but that matching probably works less by assigning clients to one of a few fixed treatment packages and more by responsiveness: a skilled therapist adjusting what he or she does, moment by moment and session by session, to the person in the room. Newer statistical approaches try to combine many client variables into a single prediction of which treatment would suit a particular person best, but these are still being tested.

Exam trap

An aptitude-treatment interaction is a client characteristic that changes which treatment works best (a prescriptive variable), not a characteristic that predicts a good outcome in any treatment (a prognostic variable). Systematic treatment selection is Beutler's (with Clarkin, 1990), built on coping style (externalising versus internalising), reactance, distress and impairment. Project MATCH, the largest matching trial, found few of its predicted matches.

Why clients look better than they are: the hello-goodbye effect and other artefacts

Much of the evidence a practising clinician sees is a comparison of a client's scores at the start and the end of therapy, with no comparison group. Such pre-post change is easy to collect and easy to over-read, because several processes can make clients look better at the end even if therapy has done less than it seems. Section 8.1 introduced two of them, spontaneous remission and regression to the mean. This subsection adds the others and shows how each one inflates change.

The first was described in 1948 by Starke Rosecrans Hathaway (1903 to 1984), the Minnesota psychologist best known as co-author of the Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (the test itself belongs to the Assessment book). Writing about non-directive counselling in the Journal of Clinical Psychology, he described what is now called the hello-goodbye effect: clients tend to exaggerate their distress when they arrive and to exaggerate their improvement when they leave. At the "hello", a client has every reason to overstate how bad things are: to justify asking for help, to be taken seriously, to be sure of being accepted for treatment, or simply because people seek help at their worst moment and describe it vividly. At the "goodbye", the pressures run the other way: to thank a therapist who has been kind, to justify the time, money and effort spent, to avoid disappointing someone, and to convince oneself that the decision to end is right. Neither exaggeration need be deliberate. Together they inflate the difference between intake and termination scores, so that the apparent change is larger than the real change (Figure 8.7).

Work through the figure. A client's true distress on a 40-point scale is 30 when she starts and 20 when she finishes, a real improvement of 10 points. But at intake she rates herself at 36, and at the end at 14. The questionnaire therefore shows an improvement of 22 points, more than double the true change. If a clinic adds up such scores across hundreds of clients and reports the average pre-post change as evidence that its service works, the hello-goodbye effect alone can make a modest service look excellent. It also distorts the reliable change index (Section 8.9), which assumes that any change larger than measurement error is real.

The second artefact is regression to the mean, introduced in Section 8.1. Because people come for help when they are at their worst, and because any single measurement contains some random error, a group selected for high scores at intake will on average score lower at the next measurement even if nothing happens to them. Suppose a counselling service accepts only students scoring 20 or more on a depression inventory. Some of them scored that high partly because they were having a particularly bad week; at the next testing their bad weeks will mostly be over, and the group average will fall without any help at all. Regression and the hello-goodbye effect both work in the same direction, but they are different things: regression is a statistical property of selection and measurement error, whereas the hello-goodbye effect is a motivated bias in how clients report.

The third is demand characteristics, a term introduced by Martin Theodore Orne (1927 to 2000) in a famous 1962 paper in the American Psychologist. Orne showed that research participants are not passive: they look for cues to what the study is about and what the researcher hopes for, and many try to be "good subjects" who help to confirm the hypothesis. A therapy client filling in a questionnaire at the end of treatment, often in the therapist's room or handed to the therapist afterwards, knows exactly which answers the therapist hopes for. Demand characteristics overlap with the goodbye half of the hello-goodbye effect, but they are a property of the situation (the cues), whereas the hello-goodbye effect is a pattern in the client's self-presentation at the two ends of therapy.

The fourth is placebo responding: real improvement produced by the expectation of help rather than by the specific ingredients of the treatment. Placebo and expectancy are discussed as common factors in Section 8.4, and placebo control groups in Section 8.7. Here the point is only that expectancy alone produces substantial change. In a well-known meta-analysis of antidepressant trials, Irving Kirsch and Guy Sapirstein (1998) estimated that about three-quarters of the improvement seen in patients taking the drug was also seen in patients taking placebo. A pre-post comparison in a single treated group cannot separate placebo responding from the effect of the treatment.

Table 8.10 Processes that inflate pre-post change, and how research controls them.
ProcessWhat happensHow it is controlled
Spontaneous remission (Section 8.1)Problems ease with time, life events and supportAn untreated or waiting-list comparison group
Regression to the mean (Section 8.1)Extreme intake scores drift back towards averageComparison group selected the same way; several baseline measurements
Hello-goodbye effect (Hathaway, 1948)Clients overstate distress at intake and gains at the endIndependent assessors, collateral reports, behavioural measures, follow-up
Demand characteristics (Orne, 1962)Clients respond to cues about what is hoped forAssessors who do not know the treatment; measures collected away from the therapist
Placebo responding (Section 8.4)Expectation of help produces real improvementPlacebo or attention control group (Section 8.7)

The remedies, as Table 8.10 shows, are the ordinary tools of good outcome research: a comparison group that shares everything except the treatment, assessors who do not know which treatment the client received, measures that do not depend only on the client's self-report (behavioural counts, reports from family members, observer ratings), more than one baseline measurement, and follow-up after the goodbye pressures have faded. Section 8.7 describes how a randomised controlled trial builds these safeguards in.

Exam trap

The hello-goodbye effect (Hathaway, 1948) is the tendency of clients to exaggerate distress at intake ("hello") and exaggerate improvement at termination ("goodbye"), which inflates pre-post change. Do not confuse it with regression to the mean (a statistical drift of extreme scores towards the average), with demand characteristics (Orne, 1962: cues in the research situation that tell participants what is expected), with spontaneous remission (real improvement without treatment) or with the placebo effect (real improvement caused by expectation). Only the hello-goodbye effect and demand characteristics are biases in reporting; the others produce change that is real but not due to the treatment.

Client factors in Indian settings

Most of the research in this section comes from Europe and North America, from individual clients who refer themselves. Indian clinics differ in ways that change how client variables work, and you should be able to discuss three of them: the family, the route to help and stigma.

The first is the family. In most Indian clinics the client does not come alone. A parent, spouse or sibling usually accompanies the client, gives much of the history, takes part in decisions about treatment and may expect to sit in on sessions. Families are a resource; Section 7.1 describes how Vidya Sagar at the Amritsar Mental Hospital in the 1950s involved relatives in care, and Section 7.2 describes the lower rates of high expressed emotion found among relatives in Chandigarh. But the family also changes Bordin's alliance. There may be several sets of goals in the room (a father who wants his son to return to his engineering course, a son who wants to change his course, a mother who wants the son married), so goal consensus has to be negotiated with the family as well as with the client, and confidentiality has to be agreed openly at the start. A therapist unaware of a family's culture and customary practices is likely to make wrong assessments and misinterpret behaviour: an adult son who consults his parents about his career, or a young wife who defers to her mother-in-law, may be behaving normally for that family, not showing "enmeshment" or a dependent personality.

The second is the route to help. Many Indian clients reach a psychologist late, after consulting general physicians for bodily complaints and faith healers, temples or dargahs for help that carries no psychiatric label, and they often describe distress in bodily terms such as "weakness", "tension" or "gas" (Section 9.6). They frequently arrive with role expectations formed by medical consultations: a brief visit, a diagnosis, advice and a prescription. A client who expects an active, benevolent guide (the guru-chela relationship described by J. S. Neki, Section 8.11) and meets a silent, reflective therapist may decide that nothing is being done and not return. Explaining early what therapy involves, and offering more structure than a Western manual might, protects both the alliance and attendance.

The third is stigma. Fear of being labelled "mental" keeps many people from seeking help at all, and is one reason why the treatment gap in India is so large (between about 70 and 92 percent for different disorders in the National Mental Health Survey, Section 9.6). Families worry, in particular, about the effect of a psychiatric label on the marriage prospects of the client and of the client's siblings, and may ask for discretion about records and certificates. Stigma also shapes the hello and the goodbye: some clients minimise their distress at intake because admitting it is shameful, and some families declare a client "fine now" and stop treatment as soon as the most visible symptoms ease.

Finally, Schofield's YAVIS bias has an Indian form. Psychotherapy in India is often delivered in English, in cities, to educated and articulate clients who resemble their therapists, while a rural, older, less educated client who speaks only Kannada, Tamil or Hindi may be offered medication and advice instead. Offering therapy in the client's own language and idiom is one of the simplest ways of correcting the bias; cultural adaptation of therapy is the subject of Section 8.11.

Exam trap

Poor awareness of a family's culture and customary practices leads to wrong assessments and misinterpretations, pathologising behaviour that is normal for that family; it does not produce an accurate understanding of the family's dynamics, effective communication or a strong alliance. In Indian settings, remember three client factors: family involvement (several sets of goals, negotiated confidentiality), the route to help (general physicians and faith healers first, bodily idioms of distress, medical role expectations) and stigma (label avoidance, marriage concerns, a treatment gap of about 70 to 92 percent).

8.7 Testing a Treatment: Efficacy, Effectiveness and the Randomised Controlled TrialHigh yield · 6 Qs

Sections 8.1 and 8.2 answered Eysenck by adding up hundreds of studies. But a meta-analysis is only as good as the studies that go into it ("garbage in, garbage out", Section 8.2), and the arguments of the sections that follow it, about why therapy works (Sections 8.3 to 8.6), can only be settled by well-built studies. So it is time to look inside a single study and ask how it should be built. Imagine a clinical psychologist at a teaching hospital in Mysuru, Dr Anita Rao, who has developed a twelve-session cognitive-behavioural programme for depression delivered in Kannada. She wants to know whether it works. Every decision she now faces (whom to treat, whom to compare them with, how to decide who gets what, who rates the outcome, what to do about the people who drop out, how many people she needs) has been fought over by psychotherapy researchers, and each has a name you may be examined on.

The general logic of experimental design (variables, confounds, sampling, statistical tests) belongs to the Research Methodology book. This section teaches design only as psychotherapy research uses it.

Efficacy and effectiveness: can it work, and does it work?

Two different questions hide inside "Does this therapy work?" The first is whether the therapy can produce benefit when everything is arranged in its favour: carefully chosen clients with one clear problem, well-trained therapists following a manual, close supervision and careful measurement. This is the question of efficacy. The second is whether the therapy produces benefit when it is delivered in ordinary practice, by ordinary clinicians, to the clients who actually turn up, with all their overlapping problems, missed appointments and family pressures. This is the question of effectiveness. A third question, whether the benefit is worth what it costs, is the question of efficiency or cost-effectiveness. The Canadian clinical epidemiologist R. Brian Haynes put all three into the title of a short paper in 1999: "Can it work? Does it work? Is it worth it?"

The two kinds of study prize different kinds of validity. The first is internal validity: the degree to which a study allows you to conclude that the treatment, and not something else, caused the change you observed.
