The "something else" is the list of rival explanations from Section 8.1: time and spontaneous remission, regression to the mean, life events, the effect of being assessed, and the expectation of help. The second is external validity: the degree to which the result generalises beyond the study, to other clients, other therapists, other settings and other times. The difficulty is that the two pull against each other. Every step that protects internal validity (excluding clients with a second disorder, fixing the number of sessions, training therapists to follow a script) makes the study less like the clinic down the road, and so weakens external validity. Efficacy research buys internal validity at the price of external validity; effectiveness research makes the opposite bargain. Table 8.11 sets the two side by side.

Table 8.11 Efficacy and effectiveness studies compared.
FeatureEfficacy studyEffectiveness study
Question askedCan the treatment work under ideal conditions?Does it work in ordinary practice?
Validity prizedInternalExternal
ClientsRecruited; one diagnosis; many exclusionsOrdinary help-seekers; several problems allowed
AllocationRandom assignment to conditionsOften none, or randomised by clinic
TherapistsSelected, trained, supervised; follow a manualOrdinary clinicians; flexible and eclectic
LengthFixed number of sessionsAs long as needed
OutcomesTarget symptoms, rated blindGeneral functioning, satisfaction, often self-report
Classic exampleNIMH depression trial (1989, Section 8.8)Consumer Reports survey (Seligman, 1995)

The distinction became famous through a magazine survey. In 1995 Martin E. P. Seligman (born 1942), the University of Pennsylvania psychologist you met in Chapter 6 as the founder of positive psychology, published in the American Psychologist an analysis of a survey run by Consumer Reports, the American magazine that tests washing machines and cars for its readers. Seligman had advised the magazine on the survey, and he used it to argue that the field had become obsessed with efficacy and had forgotten effectiveness.

Key study: the Consumer Reports survey (Seligman, 1995)

Question: How do people who have actually received psychotherapy in the ordinary marketplace say it went? Method: The magazine's 1994 annual questionnaire, sent to its subscribers, included a section on mental health. About 180,000 readers received this version; about 22,000 replied; about 7,000 answered the mental health questions, and about 2,900 of these had seen a mental health professional (others had seen family doctors or joined self-help groups). Respondents rated how they had felt when they began, how much treatment had helped with the problem that brought them, their satisfaction, and how their functioning had changed. Result: Most respondents said treatment had helped, many of them a lot. Long-term therapy was reported to do better than short-term therapy; psychotherapy alone did as well as psychotherapy combined with medication; no particular type of therapy did better than any other for any problem; psychologists, psychiatrists and social workers did equally well, and all did better than marriage counsellors and than long-term care from a family doctor; members of Alcoholics Anonymous did especially well; and clients whose choice of therapist or length of therapy was limited by insurance did worse. Conclusion: Psychotherapy as it is practised in the field is effective. Criticism: There was no control group, improvement was self-rated and remembered rather than measured at the time, only a small fraction of readers replied, and nobody was randomly assigned to anything.

Seligman's article was as much about method as about results. He listed eight features of the ideal efficacy study: random assignment of patients to conditions; rigorous controls, including credible placebos; treatment that follows a manual, with checks that therapists stuck to it; a fixed number of sessions; well-defined outcome measures; raters and diagnosticians who do not know which condition a patient is in; patients who meet the criteria for a single diagnosed disorder; and a fixed period of follow-up. Then he listed five features of therapy as it is practised in the field which that design leaves out. Real therapy is not of fixed duration: it goes on until the client improves or quits. It is self-correcting: when one technique fails, the therapist tries another. Clients often shop actively for a therapist and a type of therapy. Clients usually have several interacting problems rather than one. And real therapy aims at improving general functioning, not only at removing a target symptom. An efficacy trial, Seligman concluded, cannot by itself validate psychotherapy as it is actually done, because it omits too much of what is done.

The critics were not persuaded that a survey could do the job. In the October 1996 issue of the American Psychologist, several of them, among them Neil S. Jacobson (1949 to 1999) and Andrew Christensen, pointed out the problems you can probably list yourself. Without a control group, improvement could not be separated from time and spontaneous remission. Respondents rated their own improvement, from memory and without any blinding. Those who had done well may have been more willing to reply. And because nobody had been randomly assigned, comparisons such as "therapy alone versus therapy plus medication" were confounded by who had chosen what. Seligman accepted most of these points; his reply was that every method has flaws and that the survey's flaws were different from, not worse than, those of the efficacy trial. The lasting result of the exchange was not a winner but a vocabulary. Since 1995 every psychotherapy researcher distinguishes efficacy from effectiveness, and most agree that a treatment needs both kinds of evidence.

The same two questions run through the American Psychological Association's thinking about treatment guidelines. A task force of the Association produced a template for developing clinical practice guidelines, approved in 1995 (and followed in 2002 by criteria for evaluating guidelines), which asked that every treatment be judged on two axes: a clinical efficacy axis (does controlled research show that it works?) and a clinical utility axis (is it feasible, acceptable and generalisable in real settings, and at what cost?). Guidelines return in Section 8.8.

Exam trap

An efficacy study tests a treatment under ideal, controlled conditions (high internal validity): random assignment, a treatment manual that controls what the therapist does, a fixed number of sessions, a homogeneous sample with one diagnosis, blind raters. Therapist flexibility belongs to ordinary practice and to effectiveness research, so it is the "EXCEPT" answer when you are asked for the features of efficacy studies (RML 2020). Watch a quieter error in such options: efficacy trials depend on random assignment of the people they recruit, but they almost never achieve random selection (a random sample of all patients); their samples are volunteers who passed the inclusion criteria. The Consumer Reports survey (Seligman, 1995) is the classic effectiveness study.

Before the big trial: analogue studies, case studies and single-case designs

Before a treatment reaches a full-scale trial it usually passes through smaller and cheaper kinds of study. Three of them appear in examinations, and each answers a different question.

Analogue research. The term analogue research refers to the study of a treatment under conditions that only resemble, or are analogous to, real clinical practice. The resemblance can be weakened on any of several dimensions, as the American methodologist Alan E. Kazdin (born 1945) has described: the participants (volunteer university students with mild fears, rather than patients who have sought help), the problem (fear of snakes or of public speaking, rather than a disabling disorder), the therapists (graduate students rather than experienced clinicians), the treatment (a few standardised sessions) and the setting (a laboratory rather than a clinic). In 1963 Peter Lang and David Lazovik tested systematic desensitisation (Chapter 4) on college students who were afraid of snakes, and for the next decade the snake-fearful student was the white rat of behaviour therapy research. The advantages of analogue research are control, speed, low cost and fewer ethical problems, since nobody with a serious illness is denied treatment. Its weakness is external validity: a student who volunteers to approach a snake for course credit is not a mother who has not left her house for a year. Analogue studies are best used to test mechanisms, which must then be checked with real patients.

Key study: Gordon Paul's insight versus desensitisation experiment (1966)

Question: Is systematic desensitisation better than insight-oriented therapy, and is either better than the attention and hope that any treatment provides? Method: Gordon Paul (whose 1967 question you met in Section 8.1) took university students with severe fear of public speaking and assigned them to systematic desensitisation, insight-oriented psychotherapy, an attention-placebo condition or no treatment. The attention-placebo clients took a capsule they were told was a fast-acting tranquilliser and practised a dull "stress" task, so they received contact, attention and a believable rationale without any supposed active ingredient. Five experienced therapists, all insight-oriented by training, each delivered all three treatments, in five sessions per client, so differences between treatments could not be blamed on differences between therapists. Anxiety was measured by self-report, by physiological measures and by observers rating a real speech. Result: Desensitisation produced the greatest improvement; insight-oriented therapy and the attention-placebo did about equally well, and both did better than no treatment on some measures. Conclusion: Desensitisation had a specific effect beyond attention and expectancy, while the insight therapy's benefit could be matched by a placebo. Criticism: It was an analogue study (students, one circumscribed fear, five sessions), and insight therapy squeezed into five sessions was not insight therapy as its practitioners knew it.

The case study. The oldest form of evidence in psychotherapy is the case study: a detailed narrative account of one client's problem, treatment and outcome, usually written by the therapist. Breuer's Anna O., Freud's Little Hans and Wolpe's early desensitisation cases (Chapters 3 and 4) are all case studies. They are valuable for generating hypotheses, for describing rare conditions and new techniques, and for showing how a treatment is actually carried out with a living person. Their weakness is that they rule out none of the rival explanations. There is no comparison, the outcome is judged by a therapist who has a stake in it, and the cases that get written up tend to be the successes. Researchers have tried to make case studies more rigorous by adding standardised measures taken repeatedly through treatment, an explicit formulation, and an independent judgement of whether therapy caused the change; such reports are often called systematic case studies.

Single-case experimental designs. A single-case experimental design keeps the focus on one client (or a few) but adds the logic of an experiment. The target problem is measured repeatedly, first during a baseline phase (A) until it is stable, and then during treatment (B). In the reversal or ABAB design, treatment is withdrawn and reinstated; if the behaviour changes each time the treatment does, the treatment is very probably responsible. Where withdrawal would be unethical or impossible (a client cannot "unlearn" a skill on request), the multiple-baseline design introduces treatment at staggered times across different behaviours, settings or clients, and shows that each one changes only when the treatment reaches it. Section 4.6 described these designs in applied behaviour analysis, and the Research Methodology book teaches them fully. They matter here for one reason: as Section 8.8 shows, the criteria for an empirically supported treatment accept a series of well-designed single-case experiments in place of group trials.

Exam trap

Analogue research studies treatment under conditions that resemble clinical practice, most often with non-clinical participants (volunteer students with mild fears) in place of patients, which is why "research on healthy, non-clinical populations" is the keyed answer (RML 2022). It is not the same as efficacy research, which uses real patients under controlled conditions, and it is the opposite of research on clinical populations. Its strength is internal validity; its weakness is external validity. A case study has no control at all; a single-case experimental design uses repeated measurement and phases (ABAB, multiple baseline) to control rival explanations within one client.

The randomised controlled trial, step by step

Medicine learned to test treatments properly in 1948, when the British Medical Research Council published its trial of the new drug streptomycin for pulmonary tuberculosis. The trial's statistician, Austin Bradford Hill (1897 to 1991), insisted that patients be allocated to streptomycin with bed rest or to bed rest alone according to a series of random numbers kept in sealed envelopes, so that no doctor could choose which patients got the new drug. That study is usually counted as the first modern randomised controlled trial (RCT): a study in which participants are allocated by chance to the treatment being tested or to one or more comparison conditions, and their outcomes compared. Psychotherapy researchers took up the design more slowly, but by the 1980s it was the standard, and every landmark trial in Section 8.8 is an RCT. Follow Dr Rao's depression trial through its steps.

Step 1: the question, the outcome and registration. Before treating anyone, Dr Rao must state her hypothesis, name her primary outcome (say, the score on a standard depression rating scale at the end of treatment) and say when it will be measured. Deciding in advance matters, because a researcher who measures twenty outcomes and reports the two that came out significant has found nothing at all. Trials are now registered publicly, with their primary outcomes, before the first participant is enrolled: in ClinicalTrials.gov in the United States, for example, or in the Clinical Trials Registry of India (CTRI), which the Indian Council of Medical Research launched in July 2007 and in which registration became compulsory in June 2009 for trials that need the permission of the Drugs Controller General of India.

Step 2: inclusion and exclusion criteria. Dr Rao must decide exactly who may take part. The stated rules that define the sample are its inclusion and exclusion criteria: who must be included (adults aged 18 to 60 with a diagnosis of major depression on a structured interview, with a minimum score on the rating scale) and who must be kept out (people with psychosis or bipolar disorder, active suicidal plans, dependence on alcohol, or current antidepressant medication). Each exclusion makes the sample more alike and the result cleaner. Each also makes the sample less like the clients in her outpatient department, many of whom have anxiety, an alcohol problem or a personality difficulty as well as depression. This is the trade-off between efficacy and effectiveness, met earlier in this section, at the level of a single decision.

Step 3: randomisation. In randomisation, participants are allocated to conditions by a chance process, so that each has a known (usually equal) probability of receiving each condition. Its great virtue is that it balances the groups, within the limits of chance, on every characteristic at once: severity, motivation, family support, life events in the months ahead, and the characteristics nobody thought to measure. No other method does this. Allocation by alternation, by date of birth, by the day the client attends, or by the therapist's judgement of who would suit which treatment is not randomisation, because each lets something other than chance decide. In practice the sequence is generated by computer; blocked randomisation keeps the group sizes equal as recruitment proceeds, and stratified randomisation balances a strong predictor (such as initial severity, or the site in a multisite trial) by randomising separately within each level of it.

Step 4: allocation concealment. A perfect random sequence can still be ruined at the door. The safeguard is allocation concealment: the person who enrols participants cannot know or predict the condition the next participant will receive until that participant has been irrevocably entered into the trial. Suppose the recruiting psychologist can see that the next envelope says "CBT". Without any dishonesty she is aware of, she may decide that the very depressed, very discouraged man in front of her is "not quite suitable" this week and wait for a more promising client. The groups are now biased even though the sequence was random. Kenneth F. Schulz and his colleagues showed in 1995 how much this matters: in 250 controlled trials drawn from 33 meta-analyses, trials with inadequate allocation concealment exaggerated the estimated treatment effect (expressed as an odds ratio) by about 41 percent, and trials that did not say how concealment was done exaggerated it by about 30 percent, compared with trials that concealed allocation properly. Concealment is achieved by central randomisation (the recruiter telephones or logs on to an office elsewhere after enrolling the participant) or, more cheaply, by sequentially numbered, opaque, sealed envelopes.

Step 5: blinding. The term blinding (also called masking) means keeping people unaware of which condition a participant is in. In a drug trial both the patient and the doctor can be kept unaware, because the active pill and the placebo look identical; that is a double-blind trial. Psychotherapy cannot be double-blind. The client knows she is doing CBT homework, and the therapist knows she is giving it. What can be blinded is the assessor: outcomes are rated by independent evaluators who interview the client without knowing the condition, and clients are asked not to tell them. Schulz's 1995 study found that trials which were not double-blind exaggerated treatment effects by about 17 percent, so blind assessment is worth the trouble. The two biases blinding cannot remove in psychotherapy, the client's expectancy (Section 8.4) and the researcher's allegiance (Section 8.6), can only be measured and discussed.

Step 6: delivering and checking the treatment. The therapists follow a manual, are trained to a standard, are supervised, and have their recorded sessions rated to show that they delivered the treatment as intended (see the subsection on manuals and fidelity below).

Step 7: analysing everyone by intention to treat. Some participants never start, some stop halfway, and some cannot be found at follow-up. In Dr Rao's trial (Figure 8.8, later in this section) 15 of the 90 clients allocated to CBT either never started or stopped early. Should they be counted? In an intention-to-treat analysis every participant is analysed in the group to which he or she was randomised, whether or not the treatment was received or completed. The alternative, a completer (or per-protocol) analysis, includes only those who finished. The completer analysis is tempting and misleading. The clients who drop out of an active treatment are often the ones it was not helping, so dropping them flatters the treatment; and once you remove people after randomisation, the groups are no longer the chance-balanced groups randomisation produced. Section 8.1 showed the same argument in the Eysenck debate: counting dropouts from psychoanalysis as failures pulled its improvement rate down to 44 percent, and Bergin's decisions about them pushed it above 80 percent. Intention to treat settles the question in advance. Missing outcome data are handled statistically (older trials simply carried each dropout's last observation forward; modern ones use methods such as multiple imputation and mixed models).

Step 8: enough participants, or statistical power. The statistical power of a study is the probability that it will detect an effect of a given size if the effect really exists. The convention is to aim for 80 percent power at the usual 0.05 level of significance. Using Jacob Cohen's benchmarks (Section 8.2), a two-group trial needs about 26 participants per group to detect a large effect (d = 0.8), about 64 per group for a medium effect (0.5) and about 393 per group for a small effect (0.2). Therapy compared with a waiting list usually produces a large effect, so modest trials suffice. But two active therapies compared with each other usually differ, if at all, by a small effect, so a fair comparison needs hundreds of clients in each arm. When Kazdin and Debra Bass examined comparative outcome studies in 1989, they found that most were far too small to detect such differences. The lesson is important for the dodo bird debate (Section 8.3): a small trial that finds "no significant difference" between two therapies has not shown that they are equal, because absence of evidence of a difference is not evidence of no difference. To show that a new treatment is as good as an established one, researchers now run equivalence or non-inferiority trials, which state in advance the largest difference that would still count as "no worse", and recruit enough participants to rule it out.

One more design choice is worth knowing. In the usual parallel-group trial each participant receives only one condition. In a crossover design each participant receives both conditions in sequence, in random order (half get A then B, half B then A), and the comparison is made within each person. Crossover trials are efficient for drugs whose effects wear off quickly, but they are rarely used for psychotherapy, because what a client learns does not wash out: a woman who has learned to challenge her automatic thoughts cannot be returned to her baseline before the second phase. Whatever the design, the condition the researcher assigns (the type of treatment) is the explanatory, or independent, variable and the outcome is the response, or dependent, variable; and because the researcher rather than the participant decides who gets what, the study is an experiment, not an observational study.

Finally, the result. When the outcome is a score, the groups' means are compared (with three or more arms, by analysis of variance; Research Methodology book) and the difference is expressed as an effect size (Section 8.2). When the outcome is an event that either happens or does not (response, remission, relapse), the result is often given as a risk ratio (or relative risk): the proportion of the treated group who experience the event divided by the proportion of the control group who do. If 20 percent of Dr Rao's CBT group relapse within a year and 40 percent of the treatment-as-usual group do, the risk ratio for relapse is 0.20 ÷ 0.40 = 0.5: CBT halves the risk. A risk ratio of exactly 1.0 means the risk is the same in both groups, so the treatment made no difference; a ratio above 1.0 means the event is more common in the treated group. The odds ratio, which compares odds rather than proportions and is the natural statistic of case-control studies (Section 8.8), is read the same way: 1.0 means no association. Clinicians also like the number needed to treat, which is one divided by the difference in risk: here 1 ÷ (0.40 minus 0.20) = 5, so for every five clients given CBT rather than usual care, one relapse is prevented.

Exam trap

Allocation concealment hides the upcoming allocation from the person enrolling participants until each one is enrolled, and so prevents selection bias at entry. Blinding hides the allocation from participants, therapists or assessors after assignment, and prevents biased treatment and biased rating. Concealment is always possible; in psychotherapy, blinding of clients and therapists is not, so trials blind the assessors. Intention to treat analyses everyone as randomised, dropouts included; a completer analysis keeps only those who finished. A risk ratio or odds ratio of 1.0 means no difference. In any trial the assigned treatment is the explanatory (independent) variable and the outcome is the response (dependent) variable.

Choosing the comparison: control conditions and what they rule out

The comparison group decides which question a trial answers. The logic is subtraction. Whatever the control group receives is subtracted out of the result, so the effect that remains is the effect of whatever the treated group got that the control group did not. If Dr Rao compares her programme with no treatment, she learns whether it beats time; if she compares it with a credible placebo, she learns whether it beats hope and attention; if she compares it with another therapy, she learns whether it beats that therapy. Kazdin set out the family of treatment evaluation strategies that follow from this logic: the treatment package strategy (does the whole treatment work, compared with no treatment?), the dismantling strategy (which parts are necessary?), the constructive strategy (does adding a part help?), the parametric strategy (does more of it, or a different amount of it, work better?), the comparative outcome strategy (which of two treatments works better?), client and therapist variation (for whom, and in whose hands?), and process research (how does it work? Section 8.10). Table 8.12 summarises the control conditions.

No treatment. The control group is assessed at the start and the end and receives nothing in between. This rules out time, spontaneous remission, regression to the mean and the effect of being assessed, which is exactly what Eysenck's challenge required. It does not rule out the effects of attention and expectancy. It is also hard to sustain: suffering people who receive nothing seek help elsewhere or drop out, and it is ethically difficult to leave them for long.

Waiting list. In a waiting-list control the control participants are told that they will be treated after a delay equal to the length of the treatment, and they are assessed at the same points as the treated group. This is the ethical compromise that made most of the early controlled trials possible, and it rules out the same things as no treatment. It has two drawbacks. Because the waiting-list group is treated once the waiting period ends, there is no untreated comparison at long-term follow-up. And waiting may not be neutral. People told that help is coming may put their own efforts on hold ("I will sort this out once therapy starts"). In 2014 Toshi Furukawa and his colleagues combined 49 trials of CBT for depression in a network meta-analysis and found that the odds of responding were about 2.9 times higher in no-treatment groups than in waiting-list groups, which suggests that a waiting list can act as a nocebo and so inflate the apparent effect of the therapy being tested. The evidence was not of high quality, but the warning stands: a therapy that beats a waiting list has cleared the lowest possible bar.

Placebo and attention-placebo. In drug research the control is a pill placebo, an inert tablet identical in appearance to the drug. Psychotherapy cannot offer an identical inert version of itself, so it uses an attention-placebo control (also called a psychological placebo): a condition that gives the client contact, attention and a believable rationale, but not the ingredient that the tested therapy claims is active. Paul's capsule and "stress" task were an attention-placebo; so are "supportive listening" conditions and pseudo-treatments such as relaxing to neutral pictures in place of a fear hierarchy. Attention-placebo controls rule out the effects of attention, contact and expectancy. They have two problems. The first is credibility: a placebo the client does not believe in controls for nothing, so researchers rate how believable each condition seems, for example with the credibility questionnaire devised by Thomas Borkovec and Sandra Nau in 1972. The second is deeper. The common-factors argument of Section 8.4 says that attention, a rationale and a caring relationship are themselves active ingredients of therapy, so a psychological "placebo" is not inert in the way a sugar pill is. Section 8.3 shows how this point shapes the dodo bird debate.

Treatment as usual. In a treatment as usual (TAU) design the control group receives whatever care it would normally have received: in Dr Rao's hospital, a psychiatrist's review every month and medication if prescribed. TAU is ethically attractive, because nobody is denied care, and it answers the question a health service most wants answered: is the new treatment better than what we already do? Its weakness is that "usual" care varies enormously, from almost nothing to good treatment, and often involves far less contact than the new therapy, so a trial against TAU may show only that more attention beats less. Many trials therefore use enhanced usual care, in which the control clinics receive training or guidelines so that the comparison is fair; the MANAS and PREMIUM trials in Goa, which tested treatments delivered by lay counsellors (Sections 5.11 and 9.6), did exactly this.

Active comparison. In a comparative outcome study the new therapy is compared with another established, or bona fide, therapy. This is the strongest test, because it rules out everything the two therapies share, and it is the design behind the dodo bird verdict (Section 8.3). Its cost is the problem of power described above: comparisons of two good therapies need very large samples, and their results are vulnerable to the researchers' allegiance to one of the treatments (Section 8.6).

Dismantling and additive designs. Once a package works, the next question is which of its parts do the work. In a dismantling design (or component analysis) the full treatment is compared with versions from which one or more components have been removed; if removing a component makes no difference, it was not necessary. The best-known example is Jacobson and colleagues' 1996 study of Beck's cognitive therapy for depression, in which behavioural activation alone did as well as the full package (Section 5.11); the dismantling of EMDR, which showed that the eye movements add little (Section 4.4), is another. An additive design (the constructive strategy) works the other way: it starts with a treatment and adds a component, such as cognitive restructuring added to exposure, or family sessions added to individual therapy, to see whether the addition improves the result. A parametric design varies the amount of a treatment (the number of sessions, the length of each exposure session) to find the best dose, a question Section 8.9 takes up.

Table 8.12 Control conditions in psychotherapy trials and what each rules out.
ComparisonWhat the comparison group receivesWhat it rules outMain drawback
No treatmentAssessment onlyTime, spontaneous remission, regression, being assessedHard to justify for long; no control for attention or hope
Waiting listTreatment after a delay equal to the treatment periodThe same as no treatmentNo long-term comparison; waiting may make people worse
Pill placebo or attention-placeboContact, attention and a credible rationale without the supposed active ingredientAttention, contact and expectancyHard to make credible; the "placebo" contains common factors
Treatment as usualWhatever care clients normally receiveOrdinary care: is the new treatment better than what we do now?Usual care varies and is often thin
Active comparisonAnother established or bona fide therapyEverything the two therapies shareNeeds very large samples; allegiance effects
Dismantling designThe full package minus one componentTests whether the removed component is necessarySmall differences need large samples
Additive designA basic treatment plus one extra componentTests whether the added component adds benefitThe basic treatment must already work
Exam trap

A waiting-list control rules out time and spontaneous remission but not expectancy; an attention-placebo control rules out attention and expectancy too. Treatment as usual asks "better than what we do now?", not "better than nothing?". A dismantling study removes components from a full package; an additive (constructive) study adds them to a basic one; a parametric study varies the amount. Do not confuse a crossover design (each participant gets both conditions in turn) with a waiting-list design (the control group is treated only after the comparison is over).

Treatment manuals, adherence and competence

Until the late 1970s the "treatment" in a psychotherapy study was often whatever the therapists said they did. Two studies of "psychodynamic therapy" might be testing quite different things, and nobody could check. The solution was the treatment manual: a written guide that sets out the theory of a treatment, its goals, the techniques to be used and the order in which to use them, often session by session, together with what the therapist should not do. The first manual to be widely used in research was Cognitive Therapy of Depression (1979) by Aaron Beck, John Rush, Brian Shaw and Gary Emery (Chapter 5). Others followed quickly: Gerald Klerman, Myrna Weissman and their colleagues' Interpersonal Psychotherapy of Depression (1984; Section 3.7), Lester Luborsky's manual for supportive-expressive psychodynamic therapy (1984), and Hans Strupp and Jeffrey Binder's manual for time-limited dynamic psychotherapy, Psychotherapy in a New Key (1984). Luborsky and Robert DeRubeis called the change "a small revolution in psychotherapy research style" (1984).

Manuals did three things at once. They defined what was being tested, so that another team could replicate it. They made it possible to train therapists to a standard. And they made it possible to check whether the treatment had actually been delivered. By the 1990s a manual had become a condition for a treatment to be called empirically supported (Section 8.8).

Checking delivery is the business of treatment fidelity (also called treatment integrity): the degree to which a treatment was delivered as intended. Fidelity has three parts. The first, adherence, is whether the therapist used the techniques the manual prescribes and avoided the ones it forbids; it is a question of what was done, and raters can judge it by coding recorded sessions. The second, competence, is how skilfully it was done: the timing, the judgement, the responsiveness to this particular client. A therapist can adhere perfectly and still be wooden, like a cricketer who plays every textbook stroke to the wrong ball. The third part is differentiation: whether the treatments being compared were really different from each other, which matters if, say, the "cognitive" therapists in fact spent most sessions in supportive listening. In 1993 Jennifer Waltz, Michael Addis, Kelly Koerner and Jacobson set out the standard scheme for checking fidelity. A therapist's behaviours in a trial should be sorted into four kinds: those unique to the treatment and essential to it; those essential but not unique (such as warmth and a good alliance); those compatible with the treatment but neither necessary nor unique; and those proscribed. Competence is usually rated by experts with scales such as the Cognitive Therapy Scale devised by Jeffrey Young and Aaron Beck in 1980, on which a rater scores a recorded CBT session on items such as setting an agenda, collaboration, guided discovery and homework.

You might expect that the more faithfully and skilfully a therapist follows the manual, the better the client does. The evidence is surprisingly weak. In 2010 Christian Webb, DeRubeis and Jacques Barber pooled 36 studies that related adherence or competence to outcome, and found average correlations of about .02 for adherence and about .07 for competence, neither significantly different from zero (although the studies varied a great deal). Manual training can even have side effects. In the Vanderbilt II study, Strupp's group (William Henry and colleagues, 1993) trained 16 therapists for a year in the manual for time-limited dynamic psychotherapy. Their techniques changed as the manual intended, but some interpersonal aspects of their work with clients unexpectedly got worse, and the therapists who adhered most closely to the techniques were those who, on a self-report measure, treated themselves in the most hostile and controlling way. Following the steps, it seems, is not the same as being a good therapist. Defenders of manuals reply that a good manual specifies principles rather than a script, and Philip Kendall's phrase "flexibility within fidelity" describes the ideal: the therapist adapts the manual to the client in front of her without abandoning its active ingredients. Sections 8.4 and 8.5 pursue the point.

Exam trap

Adherence asks whether the therapist did what the manual prescribes and avoided what it forbids; competence asks how skilfully; differentiation asks whether the compared treatments were really different. Together they make up treatment fidelity (integrity). The first widely used research manual was Beck, Rush, Shaw and Emery's Cognitive Therapy of Depression (1979); "a small revolution in psychotherapy research style" is Luborsky and DeRubeis (1984). Manuals are a hallmark of efficacy research and a requirement of the Chambless criteria (Section 8.8). Webb, DeRubeis and Barber (2010) found adherence and competence almost unrelated to outcome.

Reporting a trial: CONSORT and the flow diagram

A trial can be well designed and badly reported, and for a long time most were. Reports often failed to say how the random sequence was generated, whether allocation was concealed, how many people dropped out and whether they were analysed, which made it impossible for a reader, or a meta-analyst, to judge the trial. In 1996 a group of trialists, statisticians and journal editors published the CONSORT statement (Consolidated Standards of Reporting Trials) in the Journal of the American Medical Association: a checklist of what every report of a randomised trial must state, together with a flow diagram that tracks every participant from first contact to final analysis. It was revised in 2001 and 2010 (the 2010 checklist had 25 items) and again in 2025, when the checklist grew to 30 items, including a new section on open science. An extension for trials of social and psychological interventions, CONSORT-SPI, appeared in 2018. Most major journals now refuse to publish a trial that does not follow CONSORT.

Read Figure 8.8 from the top, and at each stage ask the question a sceptical reader should ask. At enrolment: how many people were screened, and why were some excluded? Here 80 of 260 were excluded before randomisation, which tells you about external validity: the result applies to people like the 180 who remained, not to everyone who was referred. At allocation: how many were randomised to each arm, and did they receive what they were allocated? At follow-up: how many were lost, and were the losses similar in the two arms? Here 11 and 7 were lost; a much larger or more lopsided loss would be a warning, because the people who vanish from a trial are rarely a random sample. At analysis: were all randomised participants analysed (intention to treat), or only those who finished? A good report answers all four questions; a report that cannot draw this diagram is hiding something.

Exam trap

CONSORT (1996; revised 2001, 2010 with 25 items, and 2025 with 30 items) is the reporting standard for randomised controlled trials, and its flow diagram has four stages: enrolment, allocation, follow-up and analysis. Do not confuse it with PRISMA, the reporting standard for systematic reviews and meta-analyses, or STROBE, the standard for observational studies such as cohort and case-control studies. CONSORT tells authors what to report; it does not tell them how to design the trial.

From the trial to the clinic: practical trials, benchmarking and practice-based evidence

An efficacy trial tells you that a treatment can work. It does not tell a district hospital whether it will work in its own clinics, with its own staff and its own patients. Three responses to this gap have grown up, and the examiners like all three.

Practical clinical trials. The first response is to change the trial. As early as 1967 two French statisticians, Daniel Schwartz and Joseph Lellouch, distinguished explanatory trials, which ask whether a treatment works under ideal conditions and so tell you about its mechanism, from pragmatic trials, which ask whether it works under the conditions in which it will actually be used and so help someone decide what to do. In 2003 Sean Tunis, Daniel Stryer and Carolyn Clancy called, in the Journal of the American Medical Association, for more practical clinical trials (pragmatic trials), defined by four features: they compare clinically relevant alternatives (usually a new treatment against usual care rather than placebo), include a diverse population with few exclusions, recruit from a range of ordinary practice settings, and measure a broad range of outcomes that matter to patients and services, such as functioning, quality of life and cost. A practical trial keeps randomisation, and so keeps internal validity, while gaining much of the external validity of an effectiveness study. The MANAS trial in Goa (Section 9.6), which randomised 24 primary care clinics rather than individual patients and compared lay health counsellors with enhanced usual care in 2,796 patients, is an Indian example.

Benchmarking. The second response is to compare routine practice with the trials. In benchmarking, the outcomes achieved in an ordinary service are compared with the results of published efficacy trials, which serve as the benchmark. The classic example comes from a community mental health centre in Indiana. Wade, Treat and Stuart (1998) delivered a 15-session CBT protocol for panic disorder to 110 ordinary clients, without excluding anyone on grounds of medication, severity or agoraphobia, and compared the results with those of two controlled efficacy trials. Despite the differences in clients, therapists and setting, the outcomes were comparable: 87 percent of those who completed treatment were free of panic at the end, and at a one-year follow-up 89 percent of the completers who were reassessed were panic-free (Stuart, Treat and Wade, 2000). Benchmarking cannot prove that therapy caused the change, because there is no control group, but it shows whether a service is getting the results that the trials say are possible, and any clinic can afford to do it.

Practice-based evidence. The third response reverses the direction of travel. Evidence-based practice (Section 8.8) carries research findings into the clinic; practice-based evidence builds evidence out of the clinic, by having ordinary services measure the outcomes of all their clients and pooling the results. The British researchers Frank Margison, Michael Barkham and their colleagues put the phrase into circulation in 2000, arguing that networks of clinics sharing a common measure (practice research networks) could answer questions that trials cannot: how many clients in routine care improve, how quickly, and which services and therapists do better than others. Their CORE system is built on the CORE-OM (Clinical Outcomes in Routine Evaluation, Outcome Measure), a 34-item self-report questionnaire covering subjective well-being, problems or symptoms, functioning and risk, short enough to give to every client (Chris Evans and colleagues, 2002).

When such a measure is given at every session, or every few sessions, and the results are fed back to the therapist, the practice is called routine outcome monitoring (ROM; also called measurement-based care or client feedback). Its leading champion is Michael Lambert, whom you met in Section 8.2. His Outcome Questionnaire-45 (OQ-45) asks about symptom distress, interpersonal relations and social role functioning; each week a client's score is compared with the expected course of recovery for similar clients, and the therapist is warned when the client is "not on track". In a series of trials beginning in 2001, Lambert and his colleagues found that this feedback improved outcomes, and that the benefit was concentrated in the clients who were going off track, many of whom would otherwise have worsened without the therapist noticing. Section 8.9 explains the reliable change index, on which judgements of reliable improvement and deterioration rest, and how often clients get worse.

Clinical link

You can practise practice-based evidence with your own clients from your first placement. Give every client a short validated questionnaire (the Assessment book covers measures such as the nine-item Patient Health Questionnaire for depression) at the first session and at regular intervals, plot the scores, and look at the graph together. It takes three minutes. It shows the client who says "I am fine, madam" that his score has doubled, it warns you early when therapy is not working, and over a year it gives you a benchmark of your own to set beside the trials. If an interviewer asks how you would know whether your therapy is helping, this is the answer.

Exam trap

Evidence-based practice carries research into practice; practice-based evidence (Margison, Barkham and colleagues, 2000) builds evidence from routine practice. A practical (pragmatic) clinical trial is still randomised; it differs from an efficacy (explanatory) trial in having few exclusions, ordinary settings, usual-care comparisons and broad outcomes (Schwartz and Lellouch, 1967; Tunis, Stryer and Clancy, 2003). Benchmarking compares a routine service's results with those of efficacy trials (Wade, Treat and Stuart, 1998) and has no control group. The CORE-OM has 34 items; the OQ-45 is Lambert's.

8.8 Landmark Trials, Empirically Supported Treatments and Evidence-Based PracticeLower yield · 1 Q

Section 8.7 described the tools. This section is about what happened when the field used them on a grand scale. Two enormous trials, one in depression and one in alcohol problems, were meant to settle arguments, and instead taught researchers how hard arguments are to settle. Then, in the 1990s, psychologists began to draw up lists of the treatments that had passed the tests, and the lists started a quarrel that ended, as far as it has ended, with a broader idea borrowed from medicine: evidence-based practice.

The NIMH Treatment of Depression Collaborative Research Program

By the late 1970s cognitive therapy and interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT) for depression each had promising trials behind them, but almost all had been run by the therapies' own developers (Section 5.3 told the story of Rush, Beck, Kovacs and Hollon's 1977 trial). The United States National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH) decided to do what nobody had yet done: test two psychotherapies against an antidepressant and a placebo, at several sites at once, with therapists trained to a standard and checked, in a study run by investigators who were not the treatments' inventors. The plan was published in 1985 by Irene Elkin, Morris Parloff, Suzanne Hadley (whom you met in Section 8.1) and Joseph Autry, and it became the Treatment of Depression Collaborative Research Program (TDCRP). When its first results appeared in the Archives of General Psychiatry in 1989, they were the most eagerly awaited in the history of psychotherapy research.

Key study: the NIMH TDCRP (Elkin and colleagues, 1989)

Question: Under the most carefully controlled conditions yet arranged, how do two brief psychotherapies compare with an antidepressant and with a placebo? Method: 250 outpatients with major depressive disorder, diagnosed by the Research Diagnostic Criteria, at three university sites (the University of Pittsburgh, George Washington University and the University of Oklahoma) were randomly assigned to one of four 16-week treatments (Table 8.13). The psychotherapists were trained and supervised in the treatment manuals and their sessions were rated for adherence; the two pill conditions were double-blind. Result: Patients in all four conditions improved significantly. The treatments fell into a consistent order (imipramine generally best, placebo worst, the two psychotherapies in between and generally closer to imipramine), but on mean scores there were few significant differences. Neither psychotherapy was significantly less effective than imipramine, and the two did not differ from each other. Against placebo, there was limited evidence of a specific effect for IPT and none for CBT, although IPT and imipramine both produced more recoveries than placebo. Conclusion: For most outpatients, two brief, manualised psychotherapies did about as well as a standard antidepressant; differences appeared only among the more severely ill. Criticism: The placebo group received weekly supportive contact and was not a no-treatment group; results varied between sites; and supporters of CBT argued that the trial's cognitive therapists were less expert than those at Beck's own centre.

Table 8.13 The four arms of the NIMH Treatment of Depression Collaborative Research Program.
ArmWhat patients received for 16 weeksWhy it was included
Cognitive behaviour therapy (CBT)Beck's cognitive therapy, following the 1979 manualThe leading cognitive therapy
Interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT)Klerman and Weissman's IPT, following its manual (Section 3.7)The leading interpersonal therapy
Imipramine plus clinical managementA tricyclic antidepressant, with brief weekly supportive meetings with a psychiatristThe standard reference treatment
Placebo plus clinical managementAn inert pill (double-blind), with the same supportive meetingsControls for expectancy, attention, support and time

The headline was rough equivalence, and attention moved at once to the secondary analyses, which divided the patients by how ill they were at entry. For the less severely depressed and impaired patients there were no significant differences among the four treatments at all, placebo plus clinical management included: for milder depression, weekly support from a concerned psychiatrist and a pill the patient believed might be an antidepressant did as well as anything. For the more severely depressed (defined chiefly by a score of 20 or more on the Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression) and more impaired patients, differences did appear: there was strong evidence for imipramine, some evidence for IPT, and CBT did not do significantly better than placebo. This is the TDCRP's famous severity finding, and for some years it was taken to mean that severe depression needs medication. A reanalysis by Elkin and her colleagues in 1995, using more powerful statistical methods (random regression models), confirmed that initial severity predicted which treatments differed, and found more differences among the active treatments for the severely ill than the first analyses had, largely because of the greater statistical power.

The second complication was the site differences. The trial had been run at three sites so that its results would not depend on one team, but the treatments did not perform identically at each. In 1996 Jacobson and Steven D. Hollon pointed out that the apparent advantage of medication over CBT for the more severely depressed was not robust across the three sites and did not fit the results of other trials, and CBT's supporters argued that the cognitive therapists at some sites had less experience of the method than those at others. Site differences of this kind are a reminder that a "treatment" in a trial is always a treatment delivered by particular therapists in a particular place, which is the problem of therapist effects (Section 8.6). The trial's rich data were later mined for many such questions, including which therapists did best and how much the alliance mattered (Sections 8.5 and 8.6).

The third complication came at follow-up. In 1992 Tracie Shea and her colleagues reported what happened over the 18 months after treatment ended. Of all the patients who had entered treatment and could be followed, only 30 percent in CBT, 26 percent in IPT, 19 percent in imipramine and 20 percent in placebo recovered and stayed well without a relapse, and the four did not differ significantly. Their conclusion was sobering: 16 weeks of these treatments was not enough for most patients to achieve full recovery and lasting remission. The questions the TDCRP left open were taken up by later trials, notably the pooled "mega-analysis" of four trials by DeRubeis and colleagues in 1999 and the trial by DeRubeis, Hollon and colleagues in 2005, which showed well-delivered cognitive therapy matching medication in moderate to severe depression (Section 5.11).

Exam trap

The TDCRP (Elkin and colleagues, 1989, Archives of General Psychiatry) compared exactly four conditions for 16 weeks in 250 depressed outpatients at three sites: CBT, IPT, imipramine plus clinical management, and placebo plus clinical management. Imipramine was the "standard reference treatment", and the placebo group received supportive clinical management, so it is not a no-treatment control. Overall the psychotherapies were roughly equal to each other and to imipramine; differences appeared only for the more severely depressed, where imipramine did best, IPT showed some effect and CBT did not beat placebo. The trial was coordinated by Irene Elkin at the NIMH, not by Beck, Klerman or Weissman. Do not confuse it with Project MATCH, which was about alcohol.

Project MATCH: does each client need a matching treatment?

If the TDCRP asked which treatment is best, Project MATCH asked a subtler question: is there a best treatment for each kind of client? This is the matching hypothesis, the idea that outcomes will improve if each client is assigned to the treatment that suits his or her particular characteristics, rather than everyone receiving the same treatment. It is Gordon Paul's 1967 question (Section 8.1) turned into a research programme. In the alcohol field the idea was so widely believed that the United States Institute of Medicine made matching a central recommendation of its 1990 report Broadening the Base of Treatment for Alcohol Problems. The National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism funded Project MATCH (Matching Alcoholism Treatments to Client Heterogeneity) to test it, and the Project MATCH Research Group published the main results in 1997.

The design was really two trials run in parallel. In the outpatient arm, 952 clients with alcohol dependence (72 percent of them men) came for treatment from the community; in the aftercare arm, 774 clients (80 percent men) entered after inpatient or day-hospital treatment. In each arm clients were randomly assigned to one of three twelve-week, manual-guided treatments, delivered individually.

  • Cognitive-behavioural coping skills therapy (twelve weekly sessions) drew on social learning theory to teach clients to recognise their high-risk situations, manage craving, refuse drinks and cope with slips.
  • Motivational enhancement therapy (MET; only four sessions, spread over the twelve weeks) used the style of motivational interviewing developed by William R. Miller (Section 6.9), with personalised feedback from the client's own assessment, to strengthen the client's motivation and his or her own plan for change.
  • Twelve-step facilitation therapy (twelve weekly sessions) prepared clients to join Alcoholics Anonymous and to work its first steps, treating alcoholism as a disease for which abstinence is the goal.

The investigators specified in advance ten client attributes that, on theoretical grounds, should predict which treatment suited whom, among them the severity of alcohol involvement, psychiatric severity, cognitive impairment, gender, motivational readiness to change, meaning seeking, sociopathy, and the support of the client's social network for drinking or for abstinence. The main outcomes were the percentage of days abstinent and the number of drinks per drinking day in the year after treatment, and more than 90 percent of living participants were interviewed at the one-year follow-up.

The results were clear, and not what the matching enthusiasts had hoped for. Clients in all three treatments improved substantially, drinking far less in the year after treatment than before, and the three treatments differed little. Of the ten attributes, only one produced a significant matching effect in the main analyses: in the outpatient arm, clients low in psychiatric severity had more abstinent days after twelve-step facilitation than after CBT. A three-year follow-up of the outpatient arm, published in 1998, found two further matches. Clients high in anger did better with MET (on average about 76 percent of days abstinent, against about 66 percent in the other two treatments), plausibly because MET's non-confrontational style did not provoke them, while clients low in anger did better in CBT and twelve-step facilitation. Clients whose social networks supported drinking did better with twelve-step facilitation than with MET, plausibly because Alcoholics Anonymous gave them a new, sober network. The psychiatric severity effect, by contrast, had disappeared. Across all three treatments the strongest predictors of how clients fared were not matches at all but the clients' own readiness to change and self-efficacy, and almost 30 percent were completely abstinent three years on.

The conclusion was that the matching hypothesis, at least as tested with these attributes and these three treatments, was largely not supported: providers could assign clients to any of the three without worrying much about their characteristics. In cost terms the trial's most important practical message was that four sessions of MET did about as well as twelve sessions of the other therapies. The criticisms are worth knowing too. There was no no-treatment or minimal-treatment control, so the trial cannot say how much of the improvement any treatment caused; the participants were volunteers who passed strict entry criteria; the very thorough research assessments and follow-ups may themselves have helped; and all three treatments were delivered to a higher standard than routine services usually manage. Section 9.9 presents Project MATCH as a landmark of addiction rehabilitation, and Section 6.9 describes the motivational interviewing on which MET is built.

Exam trap

Project MATCH (1997) was an alcohol trial of 1,726 clients (outpatient and aftercare arms) testing the matching hypothesis with three treatments: cognitive-behavioural coping skills therapy (twelve sessions), motivational enhancement therapy (four sessions) and twelve-step facilitation (twelve sessions). Matching was largely not supported, and the treatments did about equally well. The few matches: low psychiatric severity favoured twelve-step facilitation over CBT (first year, outpatients); high anger favoured MET, and a drinking-supportive network favoured twelve-step facilitation (three years). MET is built on Miller's motivational interviewing.

Empirically supported treatments: the Division 12 Task Force

By the early 1990s American psychologists had a political as well as a scientific problem. Managed-care companies were deciding which treatments they would pay for, and the medical guidelines then appearing, such as the American Psychiatric Association's 1993 practice guideline for major depressive disorder, seemed to many psychologists to give psychological treatments too little weight. Psychologists feared that their treatments would be squeezed out, not because they did not work but because nobody had gathered the evidence in a form that guideline writers recognised. In 1993 David H. Barlow (born 1942), then president of the Division of Clinical Psychology (Division 12) of the American Psychological Association, appointed a Task Force on Promotion and Dissemination of Psychological Procedures, chaired by Dianne L. Chambless, to identify psychological treatments with good evidence and to urge that they be taught.

The task force's report, published in 1995 in The Clinical Psychologist, listed 18 treatments as "well-established" and 7 as "probably efficacious". Most were cognitive or behavioural, such as CBT for panic disorder, exposure and response prevention for obsessive-compulsive disorder and parent training for children with oppositional behaviour, and Beck's cognitive therapy and Klerman and Weissman's IPT for depression were among them. The report recommended that training programmes teach such treatments. Updates followed in 1996 and 1998; by the 1998 update the list had grown to 16 well-established and 56 probably efficacious treatments. Along the way the label changed. The early lists spoke of "empirically validated" treatments, but "validated" suggested that the question was closed for ever, so the more modest phrase empirically supported treatment (EST) was adopted: a treatment for a specific problem whose efficacy has been shown in controlled research meeting stated criteria. Table 8.14 gives the criteria in the form used in the 1998 update.

Table 8.14 The Division 12 criteria for well-established and probably efficacious treatments (Chambless and colleagues, 1998 update).
CriterionWell-established treatmentProbably efficacious treatment
Group experimentsAt least two good between-group experiments showing the treatment significantly better than a pill or psychological placebo or another treatment, or equivalent to an established treatment with adequate samplesTwo experiments showing it significantly better than a waiting-list control; or one or more experiments meeting the well-established standard
Single-case experiments (alternative route)A large series (more than nine) of well-designed single-case experiments comparing it with another treatmentA small series (three or more) of single-case experiments otherwise meeting the well-established standard
Manual and sampleConducted with a treatment manual; client sample clearly specifiedThe same, for experiments meeting the well-established standard
Independent replicationEffects shown by at least two different investigators or research teamsNot required: one team is enough

In 1998 Chambless and Hollon set out a slightly different scheme in the Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology, written for researchers rather than trainers. A treatment is efficacious if its benefit over no treatment has been shown in at least two studies by independent research teams; efficacious and specific if it has also been shown better than a placebo or an alternative bona fide treatment in at least two independent settings, which shows that it works for reasons beyond the common factors; and possibly efficacious if its support comes from only one study or one team, pending replication. Their paper stressed two principles that are now standard: independent replication matters more than any single trial, however large, and efficacy trials should be followed by research on effectiveness in clinical settings and on cost-effectiveness. Later versions kept the idea while changing the words. The Society of Clinical Psychology's online list now rates the research support for each treatment as strong, modest or controversial, and in 2015 David Tolin and his colleagues proposed basing the ratings on systematic reviews graded by the GRADE method (described at the end of this section) instead of on counting trials.

Exam trap

The Division 12 Task Force was appointed in 1993 by David Barlow and chaired by Dianne Chambless; its first report (1995) listed 18 well-established and 7 probably efficacious treatments. Well-established requires at least two good group experiments showing superiority to a placebo or another treatment (or equivalence to an established one), or a large series of single-case experiments, with a manual, a specified sample and two independent research teams. Beating a waiting list twice earns only probably efficacious, and so does one team's work alone. Chambless and Hollon (1998) used the labels efficacious, efficacious and specific, and possibly efficacious. "Empirically validated" was replaced by "empirically supported".

The case against the lists

The lists provoked one of the fiercest arguments in the history of the profession. The criticisms are high-yield, because examiners like to ask which statement is not one of them, and they fall into six groups.

  • Efficacy is not effectiveness. The criteria count controlled efficacy trials, whose clients have one carefully diagnosed disorder, whose therapists follow manuals, and whose treatments last a fixed and usually short time. As Seligman argued (Section 8.7), success under these conditions does not guarantee success in a clinic where most clients have several problems. The 1998 update itself acknowledged that its criteria evaluated efficacy, so that how far the listed treatments generalised to clinical settings was unknown.
  • The criteria favour some therapies over others. Brief, structured, symptom-focused treatments are far easier to manualise and to test in a sixteen-week trial than long-term psychodynamic, humanistic or experiential therapies, so the lists were dominated by short-term cognitive and behavioural treatments, and were almost empty of treatments for personality disorders. Critics argued that this reflected the criteria rather than the therapies, and that "not on the list" was being read as "shown not to work" when it usually meant "not yet tested". Absence of evidence is not evidence of absence.
  • The dodo bird objection. If bona fide therapies produce roughly equal outcomes, as Bruce Wampold and his colleagues argued from their 1997 meta-analysis (Section 8.3), then lists of brand-name treatments for specific disorders mislead, because they credit specific techniques with effects that come largely from the factors all therapies share.
  • The therapist and the relationship are left out. The lists name treatments, not therapists or relationships, although therapist effects (Section 8.6) and the alliance (Section 8.5) predict outcome at least as well as the choice of technique. In response, the APA's Division of Psychotherapy (Division 29) set up its own task force on empirically supported therapy relationships (Section 8.5).
  • The hidden assumptions of the trials. In 2004 Drew Westen, Catherine Novotny and Heather Thompson-Brenner argued in the Psychological Bulletin that the trials behind the lists rest on assumptions that are often false: that psychopathology is highly malleable in brief treatment, that most patients can be treated for a single problem, that symptoms can be treated independently of the personality in which they are embedded, and that controlled experiments are the gold standard for identifying useful packages of treatment. They also noted how many applicants the trials excluded, and that even in successful trials many treated patients remained symptomatic or sought further treatment.
  • The politics. Sol L. Garfield (1918 to 2004), who had edited the first Handbook of Psychotherapy and Behavior Change with Bergin (Section 8.1), warned in 1996 of the problems of calling particular forms of therapy "validated", and another critic, Wade Silverman, attacked "cookbooks, manuals, and paint-by-numbers" psychotherapy in the same year. Many practitioners feared that insurers would refuse to pay for anything not on the list, although its authors said it was not meant to guide decisions about payment.

The defenders, led by Chambless and Thomas H. Ollendick in a 2001 review in the Annual Review of Psychology, made three replies. First, the lists claim only that the listed treatments have been shown to work, not that unlisted ones do not; the remedy for an untested therapy is to test it, and the evidence for psychodynamic therapies has since grown considerably (Section 3.11). Second, the alternative to research evidence is not wise clinical judgement but untested habit, and clients have a right to know which treatments have passed a fair test. Third, the effectiveness research that followed has been largely reassuring: benchmarking studies such as Wade, Treat and Stuart's (Section 8.7) found that ESTs carried into ordinary clinics often achieve results close to those of the trials. Most researchers now hold a middle position. ESTs are a sensible starting point, but treatments are delivered by people to people, and a list of treatments cannot by itself say what to do for the client in front of you. That was the gap evidence-based practice was designed to fill.

Exam trap

Match the critic to the criticism. Seligman (1995): efficacy trials omit what happens in real practice. Wampold and colleagues (1997): bona fide therapies are about equal, so brand-name lists mislead. Westen, Novotny and Thompson-Brenner (2004): the trials' assumptions (malleable, single-problem, personality-free symptoms) are false. Garfield (1996): the problems of calling therapies "validated". Silverman (1996): "cookbooks, manuals, and paint-by-numbers". The defence: Chambless and Ollendick (2001). That ESTs require manuals and replicated controlled trials is a feature of the criteria, not itself a criticism, although critics attacked both.

Clinical link

Interviewers often ask, "Should a clinical psychologist use only empirically supported treatments?" A good answer has three parts. Yes, start from the treatments with the best evidence for the client's problem, because clients deserve treatments that have passed a fair test. But that evidence comes from trials whose clients may not resemble yours, so adapt the treatment to the person, the family and the culture (Section 8.11) without discarding its active ingredients. And measure progress, so that your own data tell you when to change course (Section 8.7). That combination has a name: evidence-based practice.

From evidence-based medicine to evidence-based practice in psychology

The idea that calmed the quarrel came from medicine, and its story begins with a Scottish doctor. Archibald Cochrane (1909 to 1988), an epidemiologist who had worked as a doctor in prisoner-of-war camps during the Second World War, argued in his 1972 book Effectiveness and Efficiency: Random Reflections on Health Services that much of medicine was practised without good evidence that it worked, and that, because resources are always limited, treatments should be judged by randomised trials. The international network that now prepares systematic reviews of trials, founded in 1993 as the Cochrane Collaboration, is named after him. In 1991 Gordon Guyatt, at McMaster University in Canada, coined the term "evidence-based medicine", and in 1992 his Evidence-Based Medicine Working Group announced it to the profession in the Journal of the American Medical Association as a new approach to teaching the practice of medicine.

The best-known definition came from Guyatt's colleague David L. Sackett (1934 to 2015), who had moved from McMaster to direct a new Centre for Evidence-Based Medicine at Oxford. In a 1996 editorial in the British Medical Journal, "Evidence based medicine: what it is and what it isn't", Sackett and his colleagues defined evidence-based medicine as "the conscientious, explicit, and judicious use of current best evidence in making decisions about the care of individual patients", which means integrating individual clinical expertise with the best external evidence from systematic research. It is not "cookbook" medicine, they insisted: without clinical expertise, practice risks being tyrannised by evidence that may not apply to the individual patient, and without current best evidence it risks becoming rapidly out of date. In the 2000 edition of their textbook, Sackett and his co-authors gave the three-part definition that psychology would borrow: evidence-based medicine is "the integration of best research evidence with clinical expertise and patient values".

In 2005 the president of the American Psychological Association, Ronald F. Levant, set up a Presidential Task Force on Evidence-Based Practice, chaired by Carol D. Goodheart, partly in response to the quarrel over the lists. Its policy statement was adopted by the Association's Council of Representatives in August 2005, and its report was published in the American Psychologist in 2006. It defined evidence-based practice in psychology (EBPP) as "the integration of the best available research with clinical expertise in the context of patient characteristics, culture, and preferences". Figure 8.9 shows the three components as overlapping circles, a picture also described as a three-legged stool: take away any leg and the stool falls over.

Each circle needs unpacking, because each has been misunderstood.

  • Best available research. This includes randomised trials but is not limited to them. The APA report recognised a range of designs, from clinical observation, qualitative research and systematic case studies, through single-case experiments, public health and ethnographic research, process-outcome studies and effectiveness studies in naturalistic settings, to randomised trials and meta-analyses, each suited to different questions. "Best available" means the strongest evidence that exists for the question at hand, which for some problems and some populations is not very strong.
  • Clinical expertise. This is not the same as years of experience or personal opinion. The report described it as competence in assessment, diagnosis, case formulation and treatment planning; in clinical decision-making, delivering treatment and monitoring progress; in interpersonal skill and forming an alliance; in continual self-reflection and learning; in evaluating and using research; in understanding how individual, cultural and contextual differences affect treatment; in seeking consultation and other resources when needed; and in having a convincing rationale for the strategy chosen. Expertise is what lets a clinician apply evidence about groups to one person.
  • Patient characteristics, culture and preferences. The client's problems and strengths, readiness to change, social support, age and developmental stage, gender, ethnicity, religion, social class, language and personal values, and what the client wants from treatment, all shape what will work and what will be acceptable.

Notice how different this is from a list of ESTs. An EST list starts with a treatment and asks whether it works for a disorder; the answer is a list of treatment names, the same for every patient with that diagnosis. EBPP starts with the patient and asks what the research evidence, the clinician's expertise and the patient's own characteristics and preferences together say should be done for this person; the answer is a decision, which may differ for two patients with the same diagnosis. To use a familiar pair of terms, an EST list is nomothetic (it concerns the average patient) and EBPP is idiographic (it concerns this patient). The two are not enemies: ESTs are among the most important sources of the "best available research" in the first circle. Table 8.15 sets them side by side.

Table 8.15 Empirically supported treatments and evidence-based practice in psychology compared.
FeatureEmpirically supported treatmentsEvidence-based practice in psychology
Starting pointA treatment for a disorderAn individual patient
QuestionHas this treatment met the criteria?What is best for this person?
ProductA list of treatmentsA clinical decision
Evidence usedControlled trials meeting stated criteriaAll relevant research, weighed by quality
Role of the clinicianDeliver the listed treatment faithfullyIntegrate research, expertise and patient preferences
LandmarksDivision 12 Task Force (1995); Chambless and Hollon (1998)APA Presidential Task Force (policy 2005; report 2006)

A worked example shows the three circles at work. Lakshmi, a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru, lives with her husband, two children and her parents-in-law, and has been depressed for eight months. Research: for moderate depression in adults, CBT, behavioural activation and IPT all have strong support (Chapter 5 and Section 3.7), and so do antidepressants. Patient characteristics, culture and preferences: Lakshmi does not want medication, partly because her mother-in-law disapproves of "English medicine for the mind"; she has little private time for written homework; and her depression began when her husband's job took him to another city during the week, leaving her alone with heavy household duties. Clinical expertise: her psychologist recognises that the onset is a role transition (one of IPT's four problem areas), that behavioural activation can be done with very little writing, and that a joint session with her husband may be needed if any plan is to stick. The evidence-based decision might be IPT focused on the role transition, with activity scheduling and one or two family sessions, and weekly monitoring with a depression questionnaire. A different woman with the same diagnosis might receive a different treatment, and both decisions could be equally evidence-based.

Exam trap

The APA (policy 2005, report 2006) defines evidence-based practice in psychology as the integration of the best available research with clinical expertise in the context of patient characteristics, culture and preferences: three components, not "RCT evidence only" and not "the therapist's experience". Its root is Sackett's evidence-based medicine: "the conscientious, explicit, and judicious use of current best evidence in making decisions about the care of individual patients" (1996), later "the integration of best research evidence with clinical expertise and patient values" (2000). The term evidence-based medicine was coined by Guyatt (1991). An EST is a treatment; EBPP is a process of decision-making.

Clinical practice guidelines and the hierarchy of evidence

No clinician can read every trial, so the evidence is usually digested for them. A clinical practice guideline is a set of systematically developed recommendations, based on a review of the evidence, that helps clinicians and patients decide on appropriate care for a specific condition. Unlike an EST list, which is organised by treatment, a guideline is organised by problem: it starts from depression or psychosis and recommends what to offer, to whom and in what order.

The most influential guideline body in mental health is England's National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE). It was set up in 1999 as the National Institute for Clinical Excellence, became the National Institute for Health and Clinical Excellence in 2005, and took its present name in 2013. For each condition NICE convenes a committee of clinicians, researchers and people with experience of the condition, which commissions systematic reviews of the evidence and analyses of cost-effectiveness and then issues recommendations for the National Health Service. NICE guidelines helped make CBT, behavioural activation, IPT and family intervention standard offers in England, and shaped the stepped-care psychological therapy services England built (Section 5.11). They are also notable for what they advise against, such as single-session psychological debriefing after trauma (Section 9.7).
