The American Psychological Association began issuing clinical practice guidelines of its own later, for post-traumatic stress disorder in 2017 and for depression in 2019, and the World Health Organization's mhGAP programme provides guidance for non-specialist health workers in low- and middle-income countries.

Every guideline must decide how much weight to give each kind of study, and for questions about whether a treatment works the usual answer is a hierarchy of evidence: a ranking of study designs by how well they protect against bias. One of the first formal hierarchies was published in 1979 by the Canadian Task Force on the Periodic Health Examination, and many versions have followed. Figure 8.10 shows a typical one. At the top sit systematic reviews and meta-analyses of randomised trials. A systematic review answers a stated question by searching for all the relevant studies with an explicit, reproducible method, appraising their quality and synthesising their results, often with a meta-analysis (Section 8.2); the reviews of the Cochrane Collaboration are the best-known examples. Below them come individual randomised controlled trials; then controlled studies without randomisation (quasi-experiments, in which, for example, one clinic offers the new treatment and a similar clinic does not); then observational studies; then case series and case studies; and at the bottom, expert opinion and clinical experience unsupported by systematic data. Some versions, meant for decisions about a single patient, put at the very top an "n-of-1" trial, a randomised single-case experiment run on the patient in question.

The two observational designs in the middle of the pyramid are favourites of examiners, and the Research Methodology book teaches them fully; here is what you need. In a cohort study the researcher identifies people who have and have not been exposed to something (a childhood adversity, a new community programme) and follows them forward in time (which is why it is also called a prospective study), comparing how often the outcome develops in each group. The result is naturally expressed as a risk ratio, read exactly as in Section 8.7: a risk ratio of 1.0 means the outcome was equally common in exposed and unexposed people. In a case-control study the researcher works backwards. She starts with people who already have the outcome (the cases) and comparable people who do not (the controls), and looks back for differences in their past exposures; the result is expressed as an odds ratio. Because it does not have to wait for a rare outcome to develop, the case-control design is the most feasible way to study a rare disorder and its possible past causes, such as a rare dissociative disorder and childhood trauma; a prospective cohort would have to follow thousands of people for years to collect a handful of cases, and an RCT is impossible, because nobody can be randomly assigned to a childhood trauma. Its classic demonstration was Richard Doll and Bradford Hill's 1950 study linking smoking to lung cancer, the same Bradford Hill who had designed the streptomycin trial. A third observational design, the cross-sectional study, measures exposure and outcome at a single point in time in one sample, like a snapshot; a national survey of how many people have a disorder and what goes with it, such as India's National Mental Health Survey (Section 9.6), is the usual example. It is quick and good for estimating how common a condition is, but it cannot show which came first, and to find enough people with a rare disorder it would need an enormous sample. None of these designs involves random assignment, so none can rule out confounding as an RCT can, which is why they sit below trials in the hierarchy.

Two cautions keep the pyramid in its place. First, it is a hierarchy for questions about the effects of treatment. For other questions the best design differs: for prognosis, the likely course of a disorder, a long-term cohort study is usually best, and for understanding what treatment is like from the client's side, qualitative research is. Second, design is not everything. A small, badly run RCT with heavy dropout can be less trustworthy than a large, careful cohort study. Modern guideline bodies, NICE and the World Health Organization among them, therefore rate the whole body of evidence on each question rather than ranking single studies by design alone, using the GRADE system (Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development and Evaluation). In GRADE, evidence from randomised trials starts as high quality and is downgraded for risk of bias, inconsistency between studies, indirectness (the studies did not quite test the question being asked), imprecision and publication bias; evidence from observational studies starts as low quality and can be upgraded, for instance when an effect is very large. The final rating (high, moderate, low or very low) tells the reader how confident to be.

Indian context

In India the main source of guidelines for mental disorders is the Indian Psychiatric Society, which publishes clinical practice guidelines in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry; they cover psychological treatments as well as medication, as in its 2020 guidelines on supportive psychotherapy (Section 3.10). Much of the evidence behind any Indian guideline still comes from trials run in Europe and North America, on clients who differ from Indian clients in language, family structure and expectations of a healer (Section 8.11). That is why Indian trials such as MANAS and PREMIUM in Goa (Sections 5.11 and 9.6) matter so much, and why the Clinical Trials Registry of India (Section 8.7) asks Indian researchers to register their trials before they begin.

Exam trap

At the top of the usual hierarchy of evidence are systematic reviews and meta-analyses of RCTs, then single RCTs, non-randomised controlled studies, cohort and case-control studies, case series and case studies, and expert opinion at the bottom. For a rare outcome and past exposures the most feasible design is the case-control study; a cohort study follows exposed and unexposed people forward; a cross-sectional study takes one snapshot and cannot show which came first; an RCT cannot randomise people to a past exposure. A risk ratio or odds ratio of 1.0 means no association. NICE (1999) is England's guideline body, not a therapy and not an Indian institution. A guideline is organised by problem; an EST list by treatment.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. The Consumer Reports survey analysed by in 1995 is the classic effectiveness study.
  3. Research on volunteer students with mild fears, standing in for patients, is called research.
  4. Hiding the upcoming allocation from the person enrolling participants, to prevent selection bias, is called .
  5. Analysing every participant in the group to which he or she was randomised, dropouts included, is an analysis.
  6. The four stages of the CONSORT flow diagram are enrolment, allocation, and analysis.
  7. The NIMH TDCRP compared CBT, IPT, imipramine plus clinical management and plus clinical management.
  8. Project MATCH tested the hypothesis with CBT, motivational enhancement therapy and twelve-step facilitation.
  9. Under the Division 12 criteria, a treatment that has twice beaten a waiting list, but never a placebo or another treatment, is classed as .

Answers: (1) efficacy; effectiveness, (2) Martin Seligman, (3) analogue, (4) allocation concealment, (5) intention-to-treat, (6) follow-up, (7) placebo, (8) matching, (9) probably efficacious, (10) clinical expertise.

8.9 How Much Therapy, How Much Change: Dose, Clinical Significance and DeteriorationLower yield · not asked yet

The sections so far have asked whether psychotherapy works on average, which therapies work for which problems, and how a trial can prove it. Every one of those answers is a statement about groups: the average treated client, the mean of the experimental condition, the effect size across a hundred studies. A clinician sitting with one client needs answers of a different kind. How many sessions will this person probably need? Has she really changed, or has her questionnaire score simply wobbled? Is she now within the range of ordinary, untroubled people, or merely a little less unwell? And is there any chance that therapy is making her worse? This section takes those questions in turn: the amount of therapy (the dose), the meaning of change for an individual (clinical significance), and the minority who deteriorate.

The dose-effect relationship

Suppose a large hospital in Bengaluru has to decide how many sessions of psychotherapy to offer each outpatient. Offer too few, and many people leave before they have been helped; offer too many, and the waiting list stretches to months. "How much therapy is enough?" is therefore a practical and financial question as well as a scientific one. In the United States of the 1980s, where insurance companies were beginning to cap the number of sessions they would pay for, it had become urgent.

The first systematic answer came from Kenneth I. Howard of Northwestern University and his colleagues Kopta, Merton Krause and David E. Orlinsky, in a 1986 paper in American Psychologist titled "The dose-effect relationship in psychotherapy". They borrowed their method from pharmacology. When a new drug is tested, different patients receive different doses, and the percentage who respond is plotted against the dose; the result is a dose-response curve. Howard's team treated one session of psychotherapy as one dose and measurable improvement as the response. They gathered 15 sets of data, covering 2,431 patients, in which both the number of sessions each patient had attended and whether that patient had improved were known. They then fitted a curve with probit analysis, a technique from drug research that relates the probability of a response to the logarithm of the dose. The relationship they described, between the number of sessions and the probability of improvement, is called the dose-effect relationship (or dose-response relationship).

The results, drawn in Figure 8.11, became some of the most quoted numbers in psychotherapy research. Between 10 and 18 percent of patients had already improved before the first session, probably because deciding to seek help, booking an appointment and expecting it mobilises hope. About 30 percent had improved after 2 sessions, 53 percent after 8, 74 percent after 26 (six months of weekly sessions) and about 83 percent after 52 (a year). The rounded version, which is the one you should remember, is that about half of patients are measurably improved by 8 sessions and about three-quarters by 26.

Look at the shape of the curve. It rises steeply over the first few sessions and then flattens, so that each additional session produces less extra improvement than the one before. A curve of this shape is called negatively accelerated, and it expresses a law of diminishing returns: going from 0 to 8 sessions helps about half of all patients, while going from 26 to 52 sessions, which doubles the cost, adds only about another tenth. The curve also never reaches 100 percent. Some patients do not improve however long they attend.

Patients were not all alike. Those with depression or anxiety improved sooner than those the authors classed as borderline or psychotic, who needed many more sessions for the same chance of improvement. Kopta, Howard and their colleagues pursued this in 1994 with 854 outpatients who completed symptom checklists during treatment. Sixty-two symptoms fell into three classes with different speeds: acute distress and chronic distress symptoms (anxiety, low mood and their relatives) responded within weeks to months, while characterological symptoms, the long-standing problems of trust, anger and relationships that belong to personality, responded much more slowly. A typical outpatient, they calculated, needed about a year of therapy for a 75 percent chance of symptomatic recovery.

The dose-effect method also gave a neat answer to Eysenck. In 1991 McNeilly and Howard applied the same probit analysis to Eysenck's own figures (Section 8.1) and showed that psychotherapy produced in about 15 sessions the amount of improvement that spontaneous remission took two years to produce. Therapy, on this reading, does not so much change where patients end up as how quickly they get there, and for a person who is suffering, speed matters.

The 1986 study had limits that its authors acknowledged. Improvement was judged mostly by clinicians at the end of treatment or from case records, rather than by standardised questionnaires; "improved" meant any measurable improvement, not recovery; and the improvement at each session number was estimated statistically from pre-treatment and post-treatment data rather than observed session by session. When later researchers measured patients before every session and used the stricter standard of recovery explained below, the numbers grew. Michael Lambert's group at Brigham Young University followed about 6,000 patients in ordinary American services, from employee assistance programmes to community mental health centres, who completed the Outcome Questionnaire-45 before each session. Across their studies, the session by which half the patients had recovered ranged from the 11th to the 21st, and the session by which three-quarters had recovered from the 25th to the 45th. A review by Nathan Hansen, Lambert and Evan Forman (2002) concluded that about 13 to 18 sessions are needed for half of patients to recover. Yet in routine care the same patients attended, on average, fewer than five sessions, and only about a fifth to a third recovered. Many people in ordinary clinics, in other words, receive less therapy than the research says they need.

Key study: Howard, Kopta, Krause and Orlinsky, "The dose-effect relationship in psychotherapy" (1986)

Question: How many sessions of psychotherapy are needed for patients to improve? Method: A probit analysis, borrowed from drug research, of 15 data sets covering 2,431 patients, treating each session as a dose and measured improvement as the response. Result: 10 to 18 percent improved before the first session, about 30 percent by session 2, 53 percent by session 8, 74 percent by session 26 and 83 percent by session 52. The curve was negatively accelerated, and depressed and anxious patients responded faster than borderline or psychotic ones. Conclusion: Most of the benefit comes early, but a substantial minority need much longer treatment. Criticism: Improvement was crudely defined and mostly rated by clinicians, session-by-session change was estimated rather than observed, and pooling patients who stopped at different times may itself produce the curve's shape (see the good-enough level, below).

Exam trap

Eight sessions, about 50 percent; twenty-six sessions, about 75 percent (and about 83 percent by one year). The authors are Howard, Kopta, Krause and Orlinsky (1986, American Psychologist). The "dose" is a session and the "response" is improvement. The curve is negatively accelerated (steep early gains, diminishing returns), not a straight line. Do not confuse these figures for any improvement with the later figures for recovery (about 13 to 18 sessions for half of patients, Hansen, Lambert and Forman, 2002).

Three phases of change: remoralisation, remediation, rehabilitation

If improvement comes in a curve, does everything improve at the same rate? Clinicians had long noticed that it does not. A client often says after the first or second session that she feels lighter and more hopeful, although her symptoms are much as before; weeks later the symptoms ease; only after months does her life (work, marriage, friendships) really change. Jerome Frank, whose common-factors model you met in Section 8.4, had argued that people come to therapy not only with symptoms but demoralised: feeling helpless, hopeless and unable to cope, and that every therapy first restores morale (his 1974 paper was titled "Psychotherapy: the restoration of morale").

In 1993 Howard, Robert Lueger, Michael Maling and Zoran Martinovich turned these observations into the phase model of psychotherapy outcome. It proposes three phases, each defined by what improves, and claims that they occur in a fixed order:

  1. Remoralisation is the improvement of subjective well-being: hope returns, the client feels understood and less overwhelmed. It happens fastest, often within the first few sessions, and corresponds to Frank's restoration of morale.
  2. Remediation is the reduction of symptoms: the anxiety, low mood, panic or obsessions that brought the client to therapy. It takes weeks to months, and it is the target of most specific techniques.
  3. Rehabilitation is the improvement of life functioning: returning to work, repairing relationships, changing the long-standing habits and patterns that keep problems going. It is the slowest phase and may take many months.

Howard's team tested the model with a large sample of outpatients who rated their well-being, symptoms and life functioning before therapy and after sessions 2, 4 and 17. The averages moved in the predicted order: well-being first, symptoms next, functioning last. More strikingly, when each patient was classified as improved or not improved on each variable, improvement in well-being came before, and was what the authors called a "probabilistically necessary condition" for, improvement in symptoms; and improvement in symptoms came before, and was probabilistically necessary for, improvement in functioning. In plain words, patients seldom improved in their symptoms without first feeling better, and seldom functioned better in life without first improving in their symptoms. Table 8.16 summarises the model.

Table 8.16 The phase model of psychotherapy outcome (Howard, Lueger, Maling and Martinovich, 1993).
PhaseWhat improvesTypical timingWhat helps most
1. RemoralisationSubjective well-being, hopeFirst few sessionsBeing heard, a credible plan, the alliance
2. RemediationSymptomsWeeks to monthsSpecific techniques aimed at the symptoms
3. RehabilitationLife functioning, long-standing patternsMany monthsLonger work on relationships, habits and roles

The model has three practical uses. First, it tells you what to measure and when: a questionnaire on symptoms given after two sessions may show little change, while a well-being measure already shows a lot. Second, it explains Kopta's finding that distress symptoms respond quickly and characterological problems slowly, and it fits the dose-effect curve, whose steep early rise is largely remoralisation. Third, it warns against stopping too soon. A client who feels much better after three sessions has usually been remoralised, not cured.

Clinical link

Sunita, a 41-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru, comes for help with depression after her husband's death. After the second session she tells you that for the first time someone has understood her, and she is sleeping a little better. Her brother, who brings her, asks whether she is now cured. The phase model gives you an honest answer: she has been remoralised, which is real and important, but her low mood and poor concentration (remediation) have not yet shifted, and returning to her classroom and her own social life (rehabilitation) will take longer. Explaining this to the family is one of the best protections against premature dropout (Section 8.10).

Exam trap

The order is remoralisation, remediation, rehabilitation (well-being, then symptoms, then functioning), proposed by Howard and colleagues in 1993. Remoralisation is about feeling better and hopeful, not about symptom relief; rehabilitation here means restored life functioning, not the rehabilitation services of Chapter 9. Do not mix up three different sequences: the phases of change within therapy (this model), the stages of therapy (beginning, middle, end; Section 8.10) and Prochaska's stages of change (precontemplation to maintenance; Section 6.9).

Dose-effect or good-enough level?

There is a hidden assumption in the dose-effect curve. It is drawn by pooling patients, and the patients who contribute to the point at session 26 are only those who were still attending at session 26. Is the curve, then, a picture of what happens inside each patient, with every person's gains slowing down as therapy goes on? Or is it a picture of who is left?

A British group led by Michael Barkham, with William Stiles and colleagues, tested the question in 2006 with 1,868 clients seen in routine primary care services. Each client attended between 1 and 12 sessions, ended therapy by plan rather than by dropping out, and completed the Clinical Outcomes in Routine Evaluation Outcome Measure (CORE-OM) at the start and the end. If each client's improvement grew with each session, the clients who had attended more sessions should have shown higher rates of reliable and clinically significant improvement. They showed the opposite. Among clients who began above the clinical cut-off, the rate of reliable and clinically significant improvement was 88 percent for those who attended one session and fell steadily to 62 percent for those who attended twelve.

Barkham's explanation is the good-enough level (GEL) model. Clients and therapists do not stop at a fixed number of sessions; they stop, responsively, when the client has improved enough to manage, a level that differs from person to person. Clients who change quickly reach their good-enough level early and leave; clients who change slowly stay longer. Seen this way, the flattening of the aggregate curve partly reflects the progressive departure of the people who had already improved, not a slowing of change within each person. In 2009 Scott Baldwin and his colleagues confirmed the prediction with 4,676 patients in individual therapy: patients who received small total doses changed relatively fast, while those who received large doses changed more slowly.

The two models are not enemies. Both agree that some people improve in a few sessions and others need many. What the good-enough level model adds is that there is no single right dose. A rule that every client gets six sessions will be too long for some and far too short for others, which is why the modern answer to "How much therapy is enough?" is "Measure each client's progress and decide together" (routine outcome monitoring, below).

Exam trap

The good-enough level model (Barkham and colleagues, 2006; Baldwin and colleagues, 2009) does not say that therapy fails or that more sessions are harmful. It says that clients tend to stop when they have improved enough, so those who attend longest are mainly the slow responders, and the dose-effect curve partly reflects who is left in treatment. Its practical message: no uniform session limit fits everyone.

Statistical significance versus clinical significance

Imagine a trial in which 2,000 adults with depression are randomly assigned to a new therapy or to a waiting list. At the end, the treated group's average score on a depression questionnaire is 26 and the waiting-list group's is 28. With so many participants, this two-point difference is statistically significant: the probability of getting a difference so large by chance alone, if the therapy did nothing, is well under 5 percent (p < .05). The authors announce that the therapy works. But a score of 26 on most depression questionnaires still means moderate to severe depression. Has the therapy made a difference that any patient, or any patient's family, would notice?

The example shows the gap between two ideas. Statistical significance tells you only that a difference between groups is unlikely to be due to chance; with a large enough sample, even a trivial difference becomes significant, and with a small sample, an important one may not. Effect sizes (Section 8.2) improve matters by telling you how large the difference is, in standard deviation units, but they are still group averages. Clinical significance asks a different question: does the change make a real, practical difference to the life of the individual client, judged by standards that clients, families and clinicians would recognise? Alan E. Kazdin (born 1945) described it as the practical or applied importance of a treatment's effect.

Researchers have operationalised clinical significance in several ways:

  • Return to normal functioning. Does the client end treatment within the range of people who do not have the problem? This is the approach of Jacobson and Truax and of normative comparisons, both explained below.
  • Reliable change. Is the client's change larger than the error of the measuring instrument, so that we can believe it is real?
  • No longer meeting diagnostic criteria, or reaching a predefined level of functioning, quality of life or symptom freedom.
  • Social validity. Do the people who matter in the client's life judge the change to be important? Kazdin in 1977 and Montrose Wolf in 1978, both working in applied behaviour analysis, argued that a treatment's goals, methods and results should be judged acceptable and worthwhile by the client and by those around him, a test called social validity (or social validation). A boy's tantrums falling from ten a day to eight is a reliable change; whether his teacher now finds him manageable in class is the socially valid question.

Notice how these echo Strupp and Hadley's tripartite model (Section 8.1): the client's view, society's view and the professional's view of what counts as better.

Exam trap

Statistical significance concerns the probability that a group difference arose by chance, and depends heavily on sample size. Clinical significance concerns whether the change matters in the client's everyday life. An effect size is a third thing: the size of a group difference in standard deviation units. A result can be statistically significant and clinically trivial. Social validity (Kazdin, 1977; Wolf, 1978) asks whether others in the client's life judge the change important.

Jacobson and Truax: the reliable change index

The most widely used method for judging clinical significance came from Neil S. Jacobson (1949 to 1999), a psychologist at the University of Washington in Seattle who is also remembered for behavioural activation and couple therapy (Chapter 5 and Chapter 7). His own research on couple therapy showed the problem plainly: treatment reliably raised couples' average satisfaction, yet many treated couples were still distressed at the end of it. In 1984, with William Follette and Dirk Revenstorf, he proposed that clinically significant change should mean the extent to which therapy moves someone outside the range of the dysfunctional population or into the range of the functional population. In 1991, with Paula Truax, he published the paper that turned this idea into arithmetic: "Clinical significance: a statistical approach to defining meaningful change in psychotherapy research", in the Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology. Their method asks two questions about each client: is the change real, and where did the client end up? The first question is answered by the reliable change index, the second by a cut-off point.

Why do we need to ask whether a change is real? Because every test score contains error. If Meera scores 32 on a depression questionnaire today, she might score 29 or 35 next week without any real change in her depression, simply because of her mood that morning, how she read an item or the time of day. So when her score falls by 5 points after therapy, we cannot be sure the change is anything more than this ordinary wobble. The reliable change index (RCI, written RC by Jacobson and Truax) measures how large a client's change is compared with the change that measurement error alone could produce:

RC = (x2 minus x1) ÷ Sdiff

Here x1 is the client's score before treatment and x2 the score after it. The denominator, Sdiff, is the standard error of difference: the spread of the difference scores we would expect if the client had not changed at all. It is calculated in two steps from two numbers you can find in a test manual, the standard deviation of the scores of a clinical (pretreatment) group, s1, and the test-retest reliability of the measure, rxx:

SE = s1 × √(1 minus rxx)    and then    Sdiff = √(2 × SE2)

SE is the standard error of measurement, the typical size of the error in a single score (the Research Methodology book explains it fully). The second step multiplies it by the square root of 2 because a change score is built from two scores, each with its own error; this form of the denominator was an amendment suggested by Christensen and Mendoza in 1986. Finally, the rule: if the RCI is larger than 1.96 (ignoring its sign), a change that large would occur by measurement error alone less than 5 percent of the time (p < .05), so we call the change reliable.

A worked example. Meera is a 29-year-old software tester in Bengaluru who comes for help with depression. Her clinic uses a depression questionnaire on which higher scores mean more symptoms. The manual reports that people attending clinics for depression average 30 with a standard deviation of 7, that people in the community without depression average 10 with a standard deviation of 4, and that the test-retest reliability is 0.84. Meera scores 32 at her first session and 17 at her last.

  1. Standard error of measurement. SE = 7 × √(1 minus 0.84) = 7 × √0.16 = 7 × 0.4 = 2.8.
  2. Standard error of difference. Sdiff = √(2 × 2.82) = √(2 × 7.84) = √15.68 = 3.96.
  3. Reliable change index. RC = (17 minus 32) ÷ 3.96 = -15 ÷ 3.96 = -3.79.
  4. Interpretation. The sign is negative because her score fell, and on this questionnaire a fall means improvement. Ignoring the sign, 3.79 is larger than 1.96, so Meera's improvement is reliable: it is very unlikely to be measurement error.

A useful shortcut follows. Multiply 1.96 by Sdiff: 1.96 × 3.96 = 7.76. On this questionnaire, therefore, any change of 8 points or more is reliable, and a clinic can work this out once and apply it to every client. Notice also what the formula does with a poor test. If the reliability were only 0.64, SE would be 7 × 0.6 = 4.2, Sdiff would be 5.94, and a client would need to change by nearly 12 points before we could trust the change. The less reliable the measure, the more change it takes to rise above the noise.

Exam trap

The RCI answers "Is the change bigger than measurement error?", not "Is the client now normal?" It is the client's change score divided by the standard error of difference, and the critical value is 1.96 (p < .05). The formula uses the measure's test-retest reliability and the standard deviation of a clinical or normal group. Lower reliability means a larger Sdiff and a larger change needed. The method is Jacobson and Truax (1991), building on Jacobson, Follette and Revenstorf (1984); do not confuse Neil Jacobson with Edmund Jacobson of progressive muscle relaxation.

Cut-off points a, b and c, and the four outcome categories

Reliable change tells us that a client has changed, not where she has arrived. Harish, another client at the same clinic, falls from 35 to 24. That is an 11-point change, reliable by the shortcut above, yet a score of 24 is still well inside the range of clinic attenders. To capture where a client ends up, Jacobson and Truax defined three possible cut-off points between the dysfunctional and the functional populations. On our questionnaire, where lower scores are better, they work out as follows.

  • Cut-off a: the client's score after therapy should fall outside the range of the dysfunctional population, defined as two standard deviations beyond the dysfunctional mean in the direction of functioning. Here a = 30 minus (2 × 7) = 16. It needs only the clinical group's data, so it is the only cut-off available when no norms for well people exist.
  • Cut-off b: the score should fall within the range of the functional population, defined as within two standard deviations of the functional mean. Here b = 10 + (2 × 4) = 18. It needs norms for well people.
  • Cut-off c: the score should place the client closer to the mean of the functional population than to the mean of the dysfunctional population, the point beyond which a score is more likely to come from the functional group. When the two standard deviations are equal, c is simply halfway between the means. When they differ, each mean is weighted by the other group's standard deviation:
    c = (sfunctional × Mdysfunctional + sdysfunctional × Mfunctional) ÷ (sfunctional + sdysfunctional)
    Here c = (4 × 30 + 7 × 10) ÷ (4 + 7) = 190 ÷ 11 = 17.3.

Figure 8.12 shows the three cut-offs against the two populations, and it shows why the choice matters. With overlapping distributions, as here, a is the strictest and b the most lenient. Meera's final score of 17 is below c (17.3) and below b (18), but not below a (16), so whether she counts as recovered depends on which cut-off the clinic has chosen. Jacobson and Truax called c the least arbitrary of the three and gave practical advice: when norms for well people are available, b or c is usually preferable to a; when the two distributions overlap, choose c; when they do not overlap, choose b; when no norms exist, a is the only option.

Putting the two criteria together gives the four outcome categories that Jacobson and Truax used, and that most outcome studies now report alongside effect sizes:

  • Recovered: the change is reliable in the direction of improvement and the client has crossed the cut-off into the functional range.
  • Improved: the change is reliable in the direction of improvement, but the client has not crossed the cut-off.
  • Unchanged: the change is not reliable (the RCI lies between -1.96 and +1.96), whatever the final score.
  • Deteriorated: the change is reliable in the direction of worsening.

Table 8.17 applies the rules, using cut-off c, to five clients at Meera's clinic. Two of them deserve a second look. Fatima's score fell by 6 points; her family may be pleased, but 6 points is within the range of measurement error on this questionnaire, so she is classed as unchanged. Kavya began only a little above the cut-off and ended comfortably below it, yet her change is also 6 points and not reliable. She has not "recovered" in Jacobson and Truax's sense, because a client who starts near the border can cross it through measurement error alone. That is exactly why they insisted on both criteria.

Table 8.17 Five clients classified by the Jacobson and Truax method (Sdiff = 3.96; reliable change 8 points or more; cut-off c = 17.3; lower scores are better).
ClientBefore, afterRCIBelow cut-off c?Category
Meera32, 17-3.79 (reliable)YesRecovered
Harish35, 24-2.78 (reliable)NoImproved
Fatima28, 22-1.52 (not reliable)NoUnchanged
Kavya21, 15-1.52 (not reliable)YesUnchanged
Suresh24, 33+2.27 (reliable, worse)NoDeteriorated

The method is now built into everyday practice. For Lambert's Outcome Questionnaire-45, described below, a change of 14 points is reliable and the cut-off between the dysfunctional and functional ranges falls between 63 and 64: a client is classed as recovered when his score has improved by at least 14 points and ends below 64, and as deteriorated when it has worsened by at least 14 points.

Exam trap

Recovered needs both reliable improvement and crossing the cut-off; improved is reliable change without crossing; unchanged is change that is not reliable, even if the client crosses the cut-off; deteriorated is reliable worsening. The cut-offs: a = two SDs beyond the dysfunctional mean (no norms needed); b = within two SDs of the functional mean; c = closer to the functional than to the dysfunctional mean (the least arbitrary, preferred when distributions overlap).

Normative comparisons and the limits of clinical significance

The Jacobson and Truax method classifies individuals. Philip C. Kendall of Temple University, with Marrs-Garcia, Nath and Sheldrick, proposed in 1999 a complementary method for whole groups, called normative comparison. The question is whether the treated group, after therapy, is statistically indistinguishable from a normative group of people without the problem: for example, whether children treated for anxiety end up with scores like those of their non-anxious classmates. The difficulty is that an ordinary significance test cannot answer it, because failing to find a difference does not prove that two groups are the same (a small study fails to find most differences). Kendall's procedure therefore uses equivalence testing: the researcher decides in advance how close the two means must be to count as equivalent, and then tests whether the treated group's mean falls within that range of the normative mean.

Clinical significance has its own limitations, and you should be able to state them. Both methods need good norms, which many measures, and many Indian populations, lack. A return to the normal range is a fair goal for depression or panic, but an unrealistic one for chronic schizophrenia, dementia or intellectual disability, where a meaningful outcome may be greater independence rather than "normality" (Chapter 9). A single questionnaire captures only one slice of a person's life, usually the slice a client can report about himself. And the cut-offs, for all their arithmetic, rest on a convention (two standard deviations, the halfway point) rather than on what a client or family would call recovery.

Exam trap

Normative comparisons (Kendall and colleagues, 1999) work at the group level and use equivalence testing to ask whether treated clients have become indistinguishable from a normal sample. The Jacobson and Truax RCI and cut-offs work at the individual level. Both are ways of assessing clinical significance; neither is the same as statistical significance.

When therapy harms: deterioration and negative effects

Section 8.1 recorded Allen Bergin's warning of 1966. Re-reading the controlled studies of the 1950s and early 1960s, Bergin noticed that at the end of treatment the treated groups were often more spread out than the control groups: some treated clients had improved a great deal, but others had got worse. An average improvement could hide real harm to a minority. Bergin called this the deterioration effect, and his 1966 paper, "Some implications of psychotherapy research for therapeutic practice", argued that psychotherapy can make people better or worse adjusted than comparable people who receive no treatment. In 1977 Hans Strupp, Suzanne Hadley and Beverly Gomes-Schwartz devoted a book to the subject, Psychotherapy for Better or Worse: The Problem of Negative Effects, and the term negative effects has been used ever since for any harm that follows therapy.

How common is deterioration? The estimates have been remarkably stable. Reviews by Lambert and others put the proportion of adult clients who are worse at the end of therapy at about 5 to 10 percent, with figures nearer 10 or even 15 percent in substance misuse treatment. When 12 leading outcome researchers from several countries were surveyed in 2003, they agreed strongly with the statement that approximately 10 percent of clients get worse as a result of therapy. In Smith, Glass and Miller's meta-analysis (Section 8.2), about 9 percent of the effect sizes were negative.

These figures need careful reading, and Scott O. Lilienfeld (1960 to 2020) of Emory University pointed out why in 2007. A client who is worse at the end of therapy has not necessarily been made worse by it: some would have deteriorated anyway, and to blame the therapy is to commit the post hoc ergo propter hoc fallacy ("after this, therefore because of this"). On the other hand, the figures also miss harm: a client who improved a little, but would have improved more without therapy, has been harmed by having his natural recovery slowed. The only clean evidence that a therapy causes harm comes from comparing randomly assigned treated and untreated groups.

Why do some clients get worse? Research points to five overlapping sources:

  • The client and his circumstances. Severe, chronic or complex problems, marked interpersonal difficulties, low motivation, and crises outside therapy (a bereavement, a job loss) all raise the risk.
  • A poor fit. A mismatch between client and therapist, or between the client and the method, such as a fiercely confrontational approach with a fragile client, or a highly structured protocol with someone who needs above all to be heard.
  • The relationship. Therapist coldness, hostility, criticism or lack of empathy, and alliance ruptures that are never repaired (Section 8.5), together with negative countertransference that the therapist acts out rather than understands (Section 3.3). Boundary violations are the extreme case (Chapter 1).
  • Harmful or misapplied techniques. Pushing a traumatised client to relive the event before she is stable enough, encouraging catharsis that inflames rather than relieves distress, or using suggestive memory techniques.
  • Iatrogenic effects. An iatrogenic effect (from the Greek iatros, physician) is a problem created by the treatment itself, such as false memories produced by "recovered memory" techniques, or delinquent behaviour learned from fellow members of a delinquency-prevention group.

The most sobering demonstration of iatrogenic harm in the whole literature comes not from a psychotherapy clinic but from a well-meant programme for boys.

Key study: the Cambridge-Somerville Youth Study and McCord's 30-year follow-up (1978)

Question: Can friendly, long-term counselling and support prevent delinquency in boys at risk? Method: In a programme begun in 1935 by the Boston physician Richard Cabot, boys from crowded industrial areas of Cambridge and Somerville, Massachusetts, were matched in pairs and one boy of each pair was assigned to treatment by the toss of a coin. From 1939, for about five years, treated boys received visits from counsellors (on average twice a month), tutoring for over half, medical or psychiatric attention for over a hundred, summer camps for a quarter, and links with the Boy Scouts and the YMCA. In 1975 and 1976 Joan McCord (1930 to 2004) traced 480 (95 percent) of the 506 men from the 253 pairs. Result: Two-thirds of the treated men said the programme had helped them. The objective records said otherwise. Treated men who had criminal records were more likely to have committed more than one crime (78 against 67 percent); more treated men showed signs of alcoholism (17 against 7 percent), more had stress-related diseases, more of those hospitalised received severe diagnoses, those who had died had died younger, and fewer held white-collar or professional jobs. Conclusion: A caring, intensive intervention had done measurable long-term harm. Criticism: The mechanisms are uncertain. McCord suggested conflict between the counsellors' values and those of the boys' families, dependence on outside help that later disappeared, expectations raised and then disappointed, and boys coming to see themselves as people who needed help; later writers also pointed to the summer camps, where at-risk boys spent weeks in one another's company. Random assignment, however, rules out the usual explanation that the treated boys were different to begin with.

The study holds two lessons for therapy. First, clients' own satisfaction is not proof of benefit: the men who had been harmed believed they had been helped. Second, putting at-risk young people together can backfire. Thomas Dishion, Joan McCord and François Poulin brought this second lesson up to date in 1999 in a paper titled "When interventions harm", showing that adolescents in peer-group interventions for antisocial behaviour could teach one another antisocial behaviour, a process called deviancy training. A parallel warning came from group work: Irvin Yalom and Morton Lieberman's 1971 study of "casualties" of encounter groups at Stanford found that some members were harmed by intense, confrontational groups, and that the leaders were poor at recognising which members had been harmed.

In 2007 Lilienfeld drew the evidence together in a paper titled "Psychological treatments that cause harm", in Perspectives on Psychological Science. Taking the medical maxim primum non nocere ("first, do no harm") as his starting point, he argued that the field had spent far more energy listing treatments that work than listing treatments that hurt, and he offered a provisional list of potentially harmful therapies (PHTs), graded by the strength of the evidence (Table 8.18). Level I treatments are "probably harmful for some individuals"; Level II treatments are "possibly harmful for some individuals".

Table 8.18 Lilienfeld's provisional list of potentially harmful therapies (2007). Level I: probably harmful for some individuals; Level II: possibly harmful for some individuals.
LevelTreatmentPotential harmMain evidence
ICritical incident stress debriefingHigher risk of post-traumatic stress symptomsRandomised trials
IScared Straight programmesMore conduct problems and offendingRandomised trials
IFacilitated communicationFalse accusations of abuse against family membersReplicated case reports
IAttachment therapies (for example, rebirthing)Death and serious injury to childrenReplicated case reports
IRecovered-memory techniquesFalse memories of traumaReplicated case reports
ITherapy oriented to dissociative identity disorderCreation of "alter" personalitiesReplicated case reports
IGrief counselling for normal bereavementMore depressive symptomsMeta-analysis
IExpressive-experiential therapiesWorsening of painful emotionsRandomised trials
IBoot camps for conduct disorderMore conduct problemsMeta-analysis
IDARE drug-prevention programmesMore use of alcohol and other substancesRandomised trials
IIPeer-group interventions for conduct disorderMore conduct problems (deviancy training)Quasi-experiments
IIRelaxation for panic-prone patientsPanic attacks induced by relaxationSingle-case designs

Two entries on the list are examined most often. Critical incident stress debriefing (CISD), a single group session held within a few days of a trauma in which survivors are encouraged to recount the event and express their feelings, was tested in randomised trials that found it no better than nothing and, in two trials, linked with worse outcomes months or years later; Section 9.7 tells that story in full. Scared Straight programmes, which began in New Jersey in the 1970s and became famous through a widely seen television documentary, take young offenders or at-risk teenagers into prisons, where inmates describe the brutal realities of prison life in the hope of frightening them out of crime. A randomised trial of the original New Jersey programme in 1982 found that participants were arrested more often than controls, and in 2003 Anthony Petrosino and his colleagues pooled seven randomised and controlled studies and found that such programmes raised the odds of later offending by a ratio of about 1.6 to 1.7. Fear, it turned out, did not deter; if anything, it backfired. Lilienfeld drew a further lesson from the list: because some treatments are clearly harmful, the strong form of the dodo bird verdict, that all therapies are equally effective (Section 8.3), cannot be true.

Exam trap

Bergin (1966) named the deterioration effect after noticing increased variance in treated groups; about 5 to 10 percent of adult clients are worse at the end of therapy. Lilienfeld (2007) listed potentially harmful therapies; critical incident stress debriefing and Scared Straight are Level I ("probably harmful") on the strength of randomised trials. Iatrogenic means caused by the treatment itself. The Cambridge-Somerville study (follow-up by Joan McCord, 1978) found long-term harm despite clients believing they had been helped.

Routine outcome monitoring and feedback

If 5 to 10 percent of clients deteriorate, the obvious remedy is for therapists to notice it early and change course. The trouble is that therapists are poor at noticing. In a study published in 2005, Hannan, Lambert and their colleagues asked the therapists at a university counselling centre to predict which of their 550 clients would be worse off at the end of therapy. Forty clients actually deteriorated. The therapists predicted that 3 would, and only 1 of those 3 did. A simple statistical method, based on clients' weekly questionnaire scores compared with the expected course of recovery, identified every one of the 40 by the end of treatment and 85 percent of them by the third session. Therapists are also optimistic about themselves. When Walfish, Lambert and colleagues asked mental health professionals in 2012 to rate their own skill against their peers, a quarter placed themselves at the 90th percentile and not one rated himself or herself below average; on average they overestimated how many of their clients improved and underestimated how many deteriorated.

The fix is routine outcome monitoring (ROM): measuring every client's progress with a brief standard questionnaire at every session (or at regular intervals) and using the results to guide treatment. Its best-known tool is the Outcome Questionnaire-45 (OQ-45), developed by Michael Lambert and his colleagues in the 1990s. It has 45 items, takes 5 to 10 minutes and gives a total score with three subscales: symptom distress (mainly depression and anxiety), interpersonal relations, and social role functioning (problems at work, at college or at home). As noted above, a 14-point change is reliable and the clinical cut-off lies at 63/64.

Lambert's system does more than measure. Using data from thousands of clients, it charts the expected recovery curve for a client with any given starting score, and before each session it compares the client's actual score with that expectation and sends the therapist a coloured signal: white when the client is functioning like a non-patient and ending can be considered, green when progress is as expected, yellow when the client is doing less well than expected, and red when the client is at serious risk of treatment failure. Clients with yellow or red signals are called not-on-track cases. For them the therapist can also use clinical support tools: a structured checklist that prompts the therapist to examine the alliance, the client's motivation, social support and the need for referral or medication.

Key study: Lambert's feedback trials (2001 onwards)

Question: Does telling therapists, session by session, how their clients are progressing improve outcomes? Method: Five studies in routine care, mostly at a university counselling centre, in which clients completed the OQ-45 before every session and were assigned (randomly, in all but one study) either to have their progress fed back to the therapist or to treatment as usual. The same therapists saw clients in both conditions. Result: For not-on-track clients, 20 percent of those whose therapists received no feedback had deteriorated by the end, compared with 15 percent when therapists received feedback and 8 percent when they received feedback and used the clinical support tools. Reliable or clinically significant improvement rose from 22 percent to 33 percent and 45 percent. On-track clients were affected little. A combined re-analysis of six studies with 6,151 clients by Shimokawa, Lambert and Smart (2010) confirmed the benefit. Conclusion: Simple feedback roughly halves deterioration among the clients at greatest risk. Criticism: Most studies came from one research group and one type of setting; later trials elsewhere have found smaller benefits.

Other systems work on the same principle. In Britain, the CORE-OM, the measure used in Barkham's good-enough level study, is widely used in routine services. Barry Duncan and Scott Miller's Partners for Change Outcome Management System uses two four-item scales, the Outcome Rating Scale for progress and the Session Rating Scale for the client's view of each session and of the alliance, chosen for being quick enough to use in every session. The measures themselves are described in the Assessment book; what matters here is the logic. Routine outcome monitoring turns the clinician from someone who hopes therapy is working into someone who checks, and it is the practical answer to both of this section's big questions: how much therapy a particular client needs, and whether that client is getting worse.

Clinical link

You do not need special software to monitor outcome. In an Indian outpatient department, a free and brief symptom measure such as the PHQ-9 for depression, completed in the waiting area before each session and plotted on a simple graph in the file, lets you see at a glance whether a client is on track. Share the graph with the client: "Your score has not moved in four sessions; let us think together about what needs to change." Clients are rarely offended by being asked; they are often relieved that someone is checking.

Exam trap

The OQ-45 is Michael Lambert's measure: 45 items, three subscales (symptom distress, interpersonal relations, social role), a reliable change of 14 points and a cut-off of 63/64. "Not-on-track" clients are those with yellow or red signals. Therapists predict deterioration very poorly (Hannan and colleagues, 2005: 3 predicted, 40 actual, 1 correct), which is the main argument for routine outcome monitoring.

8.10 Inside the Therapy Room: Process Research, the Course of Therapy and Its EndingLower yield · not asked yet

Section 8.1 drew a line between two kinds of research: outcome research, which asks whether and how much clients change, and process research, which asks what happens in the sessions and how it produces the change. Everything in Section 8.9 was about outcome. This section goes inside the therapy room. It begins with how researchers study what happens in sessions, then follows a course of therapy from its first meeting to its last, including the endings nobody planned, and finishes with four practical decisions every clinician makes: whether to talk about oneself, whether to set homework, how often and for how long to meet, and what to write down afterwards.

Process research and the link between process and outcome

Outcome research treats therapy as a closed box: clients go in, their scores change, and the question is how much. Process research opens the box. Its raw material is what therapist and client actually say, do and experience during sessions: the therapist's questions, reflections and interpretations, the depth of the client's feeling, the quality of the relationship, the moments that the client later remembers as turning points. Some process research simply describes these events (How often do cognitive therapists interpret? What does a rupture in the alliance look like?). Process-outcome research goes further and links them to change, asking whether more of a particular event, or a better quality of relationship, predicts a better outcome. Most of what Section 8.5 reports about the alliance is process-outcome research.

The field became possible only when sessions could be recorded. Until the 1940s the only record of a session was the therapist's later memory of it. Carl Rogers and his colleagues began recording sessions, with their clients' permission, and published the first complete recorded course of psychotherapy, the case of "Herbert Bryan", in 1942 (Section 6.2). For the first time, researchers could study what therapists actually did rather than what they said they did, and they quickly found that the two were not always the same.

By the 1980s there were so many process-outcome studies that they needed a map. David Orlinsky and Kenneth Howard supplied one in 1987, which they called the generic model of psychotherapy because it applies to every school. It describes the process of any therapy under six headings:

  • The therapeutic contract, the formal side of therapy: what business these two people have with each other, and on what terms.
  • Therapeutic operations: what therapist and client do, including the techniques.
  • The therapeutic bond: the relationship, including the working alliance.
  • Self-relatedness: how open or defensive each participant is.
  • In-session impacts: the immediate effects of a session, such as relief, insight or new hope.
  • Temporal patterns: how all of these unfold across sessions and across the whole course of therapy.

When Orlinsky, Klaus Grawe and Barbara Parks used this framework to review the evidence in 1994, the most consistent process predictors of good outcome lay in the bond and in the client's own contribution, notably the client's openness and active participation, rather than in any particular technique. That finding fed directly into the common-factors argument of Section 8.4.

Process-outcome research has a trap that took years to recognise. In 1989 William B. Stiles and David Shapiro criticised what they called the "drug metaphor" in process research: the assumption that the ingredients of therapy act like doses of a drug, so that if an ingredient helps, more of it should go with better outcomes. Therapy does not work like that, because therapists are responsive. They adjust what they do, moment by moment, to the person in front of them. A therapist offers more reassurance to a client who is struggling, more interpretations to a client who seems stuck, and more time on homework with a client who has not done it. As a result, an ingredient may be used most with the clients who are doing worst, and so correlate weakly or even negatively with outcome although it helps. Stiles, Lisa Honos-Webb and Michael Surko (1998) called this the problem of responsiveness. Its lesson is that a correlation between how often something happens in therapy and how well clients do cannot, on its own, tell us whether that thing helps. You have already met one consequence: the good-enough level of Section 8.9, where the "dose" of therapy itself responds to how quickly the client changes.

Process research has also shown that change is often uneven. Stephen Ilardi and Edward Craighead argued in 1994 that in cognitive behaviour therapy for depression much of the improvement comes in the first four weeks, before the specific cognitive techniques have been delivered in any real quantity, which they took as evidence for common factors. The most influential reply came from a study of sudden jumps in improvement.

Key study: Tang and DeRubeis, sudden gains in cognitive therapy for depression (1999)

Question: Does improvement in cognitive behaviour therapy for depression happen gradually, or in jumps, and if in jumps, what precedes them? Method: Tony Z. Tang and Robert J. DeRubeis at the University of Pennsylvania examined session-by-session Beck Depression Inventory scores of depressed patients in cognitive behaviour therapy, and coded recordings of the sessions just before large improvements and of comparison sessions. Result: Many patients showed a sudden gain, a large improvement within a single interval between two sessions, averaging 11 points on the Beck Depression Inventory and accounting for about half of those patients' total improvement. Patients who had sudden gains were less depressed at the end of treatment and remained so 18 months later. The sessions before a gain contained substantial cognitive change; comparison sessions contained little. The alliance improved after the gain. Conclusion: For these patients recovery followed a three-stage sequence: preparation, a critical session with a sudden gain, and an "upward spiral". Criticism: Not all patients show sudden gains, the study was correlational, and later research has found sudden gains in therapies that do not target cognitions.

Exam trap

Process research studies what happens in sessions; outcome research studies the change that results; process-outcome research links the two. The generic model (Orlinsky and Howard, 1987) has six aspects, beginning with the therapeutic contract and ending with temporal patterns. The drug metaphor critique and the responsiveness problem belong to Stiles (with Shapiro, 1989; with Honos-Webb and Surko, 1998). Sudden gains are Tang and DeRubeis (1999): about 11 points on the Beck Depression Inventory, about half of total improvement.

What process researchers measure

Every process study must answer three questions before it can begin. Whose view? The client, the therapist and an outside observer listening to a recording each see something different, and they often disagree. In one study of brief therapy by Stiles, Shapiro and Firth-Cozens (1990), clients' own ratings of how good their sessions had been did not predict their improvement, while one therapist's ratings did and the other's did not. Which unit? A single speaking turn, an episode of a few minutes, a whole session or the entire course of therapy. Which aspect? The therapist's techniques, the client's experiencing, the relationship or the impact of the session. Table 8.19 lists the measures you are most likely to meet, several of which appear in other chapters.

Table 8.19 Some widely used process measures.
MeasureOriginWhat it ratesRated by
Recorded and transcribed sessionsCarl Rogers's group, from the early 1940sEverything said, for later codingResearchers
Response category systems (verbal response modes)Clara Hill; William StilesThe type of each statement: question, reflection, interpretation, advice, self-disclosureObservers
Session Evaluation QuestionnaireWilliam Stiles (1980)Depth and smoothness of a sessionClient and therapist
Working Alliance InventoryAdam Horvath and Leslie Greenberg (Section 8.5)Agreement on goals and tasks; the bondClient, therapist or observer
Experiencing ScaleKlein, Mathieu, Gendlin and Kiesler (Section 6.7)How deeply the client speaks from inner experience (seven stages)Observers
Process ScaleRogers and Rablen (Section 6.2)Movement from fixity to fluidityObservers
Helpful Aspects of Therapy formSusan Llewelyn, Robert Elliott and colleagues (1988)The most helpful (or hindering) event in a session, in the client's wordsClient

The Session Evaluation Questionnaire, devised by Stiles in 1980, is a good example of how a process measure works. After each session the client (and, separately, the therapist) rates the session on pairs of opposite adjectives, such as deep or shallow, valuable or worthless, and relaxed or tense. The ratings form two dimensions: depth (how powerful and valuable the session felt) and smoothness (how comfortable, safe and easy it felt). The two are not the same, and the difference teaches something about therapy: a session in which a client finally faces a painful memory may feel deep but anything but smooth.

Another family of measures classifies what therapists say. In a response category system, such as Clara E. Hill's category system for counsellor responses or Stiles's verbal response modes, trained judges listen to a recording and code each therapist statement as one of a small number of types: a question, a restatement, a reflection of feeling, an interpretation, advice, information, self-disclosure and so on. With such codes a researcher can show, for example, how differently a psychodynamic and a cognitive therapist talk, or which kinds of response tend to be followed by deeper client experiencing.

A different strategy, the events paradigm, picks out particular kinds of moments in therapy (a rupture, an insight, a client's harsh self-criticism) and studies them intensively. In task analysis, developed by Laura Rice and Leslie Greenberg in their 1984 book Patterns of Change, the researcher collects many recorded examples of one kind of event, compares those in which the client resolved the problem with those in which the client did not, and builds a step-by-step model of the route to resolution. Several of the tasks of emotion-focused therapy, including the two-chair dialogue for a split within the self, were refined in this way (Section 6.7). Finally, qualitative methods have added the client's own account of what helped. In Clara Hill's consensual qualitative research, a small team of judges reads interviews with clients or therapists and reaches agreement, by discussion, about the themes they contain.

Exam trap

Match each measure to its owner. Session Evaluation Questionnaire: Stiles (1980), depth and smoothness. Experiencing Scale: depth of the client's experiencing (Gendlin's group). Process Scale: fixity to fluidity (Rogers and Rablen). Working Alliance Inventory: Horvath and Greenberg. Task analysis: Rice and Greenberg (1984). The first published complete recorded therapy was Rogers's case of Herbert Bryan (1942).

The course of therapy: beginning, middle and end

Whatever its school, a course of therapy has a beginning, a middle and an end, and each stage has its own tasks (Figure 8.13). The idea is old and appears in many forms in this book: Harry Stack Sullivan divided even a single interview into stages (Section 2.1), Gerard Egan's skilled helper model moves through three stages (Section 6.10), interpersonal psychotherapy has initial, intermediate and termination phases (Section 3.7), and cognitive therapy has its own session structure and course (Section 5.4). What follows is the generic version that applies to all of them.

The beginning. The beginning stage usually covers the first one to three sessions, though with complex problems it may take longer. It has five tasks. The first is assessment and formulation: understanding the problem, the person and the context, including risk (Chapter 2), and deciding whether psychotherapy is the right help at all or whether a referral, for medication or to another service, is needed. The second is agreeing a contract. The third is preparing the client for therapy. The fourth is building the alliance (Section 8.5), and the fifth, which runs through all the others, is instilling hope: the remoralisation of Section 8.9.

The therapeutic contract is the explicit agreement, spoken or written, about how client and therapist will work together. It has a practical part: how often and how long sessions will be, roughly how many there will be, fees, what happens about missed or late appointments, how to make contact between sessions, and the limits of confidentiality (whose rules are set out in Chapter 1). It also has a therapeutic part: the goals, the methods, what each person is responsible for and how progress will be reviewed. A contract protects both parties, makes expectations explicit and turns therapy into a joint project rather than something done to the client. It is the first element of Orlinsky and Howard's generic model, and when the contract states the likely length of therapy, the ending has been planned from the first session.

Clients often arrive with little idea of what therapy involves. Many expect something like a medical consultation: describe the problem, receive advice or a prescription, and leave. In the early 1960s Jerome Frank's group at Johns Hopkins University tested whether preparing patients would help. Rudolf Hoehn-Saric, Frank and their colleagues (1964) gave some outpatients a role induction interview before therapy began. It explained what psychotherapy is, what patient and therapist would each do, that the patient should talk freely about feelings, and that progress would have ups and downs, including moments of frustration with the therapist. Compared with unprepared patients, the prepared patients behaved more as therapy requires and, on several measures, improved more. Role induction (also called pretherapy preparation or socialisation to therapy) is now a standard part of the beginning stage; in cognitive therapy it takes the form of teaching the cognitive model in the first session (Section 5.4).

The middle. The middle stage is where most of the remediation of Section 8.9 happens and where each school's main techniques are used: exposure, cognitive restructuring, interpretation, empathic exploration, family tasks. Psychodynamic therapists call much of this working through, the repeated re-examination of the same conflict in new contexts until insight turns into change (Section 3.2), and every school has an equivalent: practising, generalising and applying the new skill to one situation after another. The middle stage is rarely smooth. There are plateaus when nothing seems to move, setbacks after a life event, the return of ambivalence (Section 6.9) and ruptures in the alliance that must be noticed and repaired (Section 8.5). Two habits protect it: reviewing progress regularly against the goals in the contract, ideally with a measure (routine outcome monitoring, Section 8.9), and revising the formulation when the client does not change as it predicted.

The end and after. The final stage, termination, is the subject of the next subsection. After it come follow-up and booster sessions, which keep the gains in place.

Do not confuse these stages of therapy with the stages of change of James Prochaska and Carlo DiClemente's transtheoretical model (Section 6.9): precontemplation, contemplation, preparation, action and maintenance. The stages of therapy describe the treatment; the stages of change describe the client's readiness to change, which may be quite different from what the therapist assumes. A young man in precontemplation, brought to therapy by his parents, is in the beginning stage of therapy but is not ready for the action-oriented techniques of the middle stage, and mismatches like this are a common road to dropout. In a culturally diverse sample of adolescents admitted to inpatient substance-abuse treatment, for example, those in precontemplation dropped out significantly more often than those in the contemplation, preparation or action stages (Callaghan and colleagues, 2005).

Exam trap

Keep four sequences apart: the stages of therapy (beginning, middle, end), Howard's phases of change (remoralisation, remediation, rehabilitation), Prochaska and DiClemente's stages of change (precontemplation to maintenance) and Egan's three stages of the skilled helper. A question about the features of a working relationship (an observable ability to work together, a committed belief in the helping relationship, a realistic and collaborative contract) will often slip in "a psychodynamic theory base" as the odd one out: the working relationship, like Bordin's alliance, belongs to no single theory. Role induction was tested by Hoehn-Saric, Frank and colleagues (1964).

Ending well: termination

Termination is the planned process of bringing therapy to a close. It is a phase rather than an event, and some writers prefer to call it consolidation, because its purpose is to make the gains last. Endings come in three kinds. A planned or mutual termination is agreed by client and therapist because the goals have been met or the agreed number of sessions is complete. A client-initiated ending happens when the client stops before the therapist thinks the work is done; this includes premature dropout (next subsection), but not every client-initiated ending is premature, since some clients stop because they have reached their good-enough level. A therapist-initiated or forced ending happens when the therapist moves, falls ill or retires, when a trainee's placement ends, when a session limit set by a service or an insurer is reached, or when the therapist judges that therapy is not helping.

How do you know it is time to end? The usual criteria are that the goals agreed in the contract have been met; that symptoms have fallen into the normal or a manageable range (the clinical significance criteria of Section 8.9 are a formal version of this test); that the client has the skills to cope with setbacks without the therapist; that the gains have held over several sessions, including some spaced further apart; and that further sessions are bringing diminishing returns. There is also an ending for the opposite reason. If a client is not benefiting, or is being harmed, continuing is not kindness. Professional codes require a therapist to end therapy, or to refer the client elsewhere, when the client no longer needs it, is unlikely to benefit from it or is being harmed by it (Chapter 1).

How should therapy end? In 2017 John Norcross and three colleagues asked 65 experts from many theoretical traditions how often they carried out each of 80 possible tasks when ending a successful therapy. There was far more agreement across schools than difference, and the tasks fell into eight groups that make a good checklist for any ending:

  • Talking through the client's, and the therapist's, feelings about ending.
  • Discussing how the client will function and cope in the future.
  • Helping the client use the new skills beyond therapy.
  • Framing personal development as something never finished.
  • Anticipating continued growth and generalisation after therapy.
  • Preparing explicitly for the ending, well before the last session.
  • Reflecting on the client's gains and consolidating them.
  • Expressing pride in the client's progress and in the relationship.

In practice this means raising the ending several sessions before it happens; often tapering the sessions from weekly to fortnightly to monthly, so that the client tests his independence while support is still available; reviewing progress together, if possible with the scores collected along the way; attributing the change to the client's own effort, which protects his confidence; and agreeing a written relapse prevention plan that lists the early warning signs, the coping steps that worked and whom to contact if problems return (relapse prevention is taught in Section 9.9).

What do clients feel at the end? The classical view, from psychoanalysis, treats termination as a loss. Ending a close relationship revives earlier separations, so the patient may grieve, become angry or suffer a return of the original symptoms as the last session approaches, and analysing these reactions is part of the work. Freud's late doubts about whether an analysis can ever be complete appear in Section 3.2, and James Mann built his 12-session therapy around the meaning of the ending (Section 3.6). Research on shorter therapies paints a gentler picture. Reviewing it in 1993, Stephen M. Quintana found that the prediction that termination is inherently a crisis for clients had not been confirmed: most clients in planned endings experience it as a positive transition, with pride and a sense of accomplishment mixed with some sadness and some anxiety about managing alone. Quintana proposed replacing the metaphor of termination as loss with that of termination as transformation: the client leaves changed, taking the relationship with him as part of a new view of himself. Loss still matters for some clients, particularly those whose lives have been marked by abandonment, and after long, intensive therapies. Therapists have feelings too: pride, sadness, relief and sometimes doubt about whether they did enough, and trainees whose placements force an ending often feel guilty about leaving.

The work does not stop at the last session. A follow-up appointment a month or three months later lets client and therapist check that the gains have held and tells the client that the door remains open. Booster sessions, planned in advance and spaced weeks or months apart, are a standard part of behavioural and cognitive treatments (Chapter 4 and Section 5.4) and help to prevent relapse in recurrent conditions such as depression.

Indian context

In Indian practice, therapy often ends by default rather than by plan. A family that brought a son for "exam fear" may stop coming as soon as the fear lifts, which is often after remoralisation and before the work is consolidated. A grateful client may wish to keep visiting, as one might keep visiting a respected elder or teacher (the guru-chela relationship is discussed in Section 8.11), and may bring sweets or an invitation to a wedding at the last session. Discussing the likely length of therapy with the family at the start, explaining why the last few sessions matter, and thinking in advance about gifts and invitations (Section 3.3 on boundaries; Chapter 1 on ethics) prevent most of these difficulties.

Exam trap

Termination is a planned phase, prepared from the first session, not an event at the last one. Endings may be planned (mutual), client-initiated or therapist-initiated (forced). Classical theory sees termination as loss; Quintana (1993) proposed termination as transformation, since research did not confirm that ending is a crisis for most clients. Norcross and colleagues (2017) found eight groups of termination tasks shared across schools. Booster sessions come after the regular sessions end.

Premature dropout

Not every therapy reaches a planned ending. Premature termination, or dropout, is a unilateral ending by the client before the goals of therapy have been reached. It is hard to define precisely, and researchers have used several definitions: the therapist's judgement that the client left too early, failure to return after a missed session, attending fewer than a set number of sessions, or not completing a manualised treatment. The rate found in a study depends heavily on which definition it uses.

The first large review, by Michael Wierzbicki and Glenn Pekarik in 1993, pooled 125 studies and found an average dropout rate of about 47 percent: nearly half of all clients. They also found that dropout was more likely among clients from ethnic minorities, with less education and with lower incomes. Two decades later Joshua K. Swift and Roger Greenberg (2012) repeated the exercise on a far larger scale, with 669 studies and 83,834 clients, and found a lower weighted average of 19.7 percent, about one client in five. Dropout varied with the client's diagnosis and age, the therapist's experience, the setting, the definition of dropout and whether the study was an efficacy trial or an effectiveness study (Section 8.7). It was highest among younger clients, clients with personality disorders or eating disorders, and clients seen by trainees. It did not differ between theoretical orientations or between individual and group formats.

Why do clients drop out? The reasons overlap with the causes of deterioration (Section 8.9) and with the client variables of Section 8.6. Some leave because they are dissatisfied: the alliance never formed, the therapist seemed not to understand, or the method made no sense to them. Some leave because their expectations did not match the therapy, expecting advice or medicine and receiving questions, or expecting three sessions and being offered twenty. Some leave for practical reasons: fees, travel, time off work, child care, a family that disapproves. Some leave because they were never ready to change (the precontemplators above). And some, it should be remembered, leave because they feel better, which is why not every dropout is a failure.

The research suggests several ways to keep clients in therapy:

  • Prepare them. Role induction at the start (above) explains what will happen, how long it is likely to take and that progress is uneven.
  • Ask what they want, and accommodate it where possible. In a 2018 meta-analysis of 53 studies with over 16,000 clients, Swift and his colleagues found that clients who received a treatment they preferred were less likely to drop out (odds ratio 1.79) and had somewhat better outcomes (d = 0.28).
