  • Build the alliance early and ask for feedback on each session, so that dissatisfaction is voiced in the room rather than expressed by not returning (Section 8.5).
  • Monitor progress so that clients who are not on track are noticed before they disappear (Section 8.9).
  • Work with ambivalence using motivational interviewing when readiness is low (Section 6.9).
  • Remove practical barriers: short waiting times, reminders, flexible hours, sessions by telephone or video, and a phone call after a missed appointment.
Exam trap

Dropout rates: about 47 percent in Wierzbicki and Pekarik (1993), about 20 percent (19.7) in Swift and Greenberg (2012). Higher dropout goes with younger clients, personality and eating disorders and trainee therapists; theoretical orientation makes no difference. The hello-goodbye effect, in which clients exaggerate their distress at the start and their improvement at the end, is a different idea (Section 8.6).

Therapist self-disclosure

Halfway through her fourth session, a client asks her therapist, "Have you ever lost someone close to you?" What should the therapist say? The question goes to one of the oldest disagreements between the schools of therapy. Therapist self-disclosure is any statement in which the therapist reveals something personal about himself or herself: a fact, a feeling or an experience from outside the therapy.

Clara Hill, who with Sarah Knox has done most of the research on the subject, classifies disclosures by content. A disclosure may concern facts (credentials, training, whether the therapist is married or has children), feelings ("When my father was ill, I felt guilty too"), insight ("I realised that my own perfectionism came from wanting my teachers' approval") or strategies ("What helped me was making a list each morning"). Researchers also separate two kinds of statement. A self-disclosing statement reveals something about the therapist's life outside the session. A self-involving statement, also called immediacy, expresses the therapist's present reaction to the client or to the relationship: "I feel moved as you tell me this", or "I notice that I feel pushed away when you laugh about what happened". Finally, some disclosure is unavoidable. A therapist's name, accent, wedding ring, age, pregnancy, the pictures in the consulting room or a chance meeting at a temple all tell the client something, and in India a surname can reveal region, religion or caste before a word has been spoken.

Almost all therapists disclose something at some time: surveys reviewed by Jennifer Henretty and Heidi Levitt in 2010 found that over 90 percent do, although disclosures make up only a small fraction of what therapists say. The schools differ sharply in how they regard it.

  • Classical psychoanalysis discourages it. Freud advised in 1912 that "the doctor should be opaque to his patients and, like a mirror, should show them nothing but what is shown to him". The analyst's anonymity lets the transference develop and be analysed without contamination; disclosure gratifies the patient's wishes (against the rule of abstinence, Section 3.2), shifts the focus to the analyst and may burden the patient. Later relational and intersubjective analysts allow judicious disclosure, especially of the analyst's own reactions in the room (Section 3.5).
  • Humanistic therapists are far more open. For Carl Rogers, congruence or genuineness means being a real person rather than a professional facade (Section 6.2). Sidney M. Jourard (1926 to 1974) argued in The Transparent Self (1964) that honest self-disclosure is a mark of psychological health and that disclosure invites disclosure in return, which he called the dyadic effect. Existential and Gestalt therapists use the here-and-now relationship directly. Even so, Rogers did not mean that therapists should tell clients everything they feel; congruence means sharing persistent feelings that are relevant to the relationship.
  • Feminist and multicultural therapists use disclosure to reduce the power difference between therapist and client and to show shared experience, which can build trust across cultural lines.
  • Cognitive and behavioural therapists use it pragmatically, as a coping model ("I used to get anxious before presentations; here is what I did") or to normalise a reaction. Albert Ellis was famous for disclosing his own past shyness (Section 5.2).
  • Addiction counsellors who are themselves in recovery often disclose their history as part of the treatment's ethos.

What does the evidence show? In 2014 Henretty, Levitt and their colleagues pooled 53 experimental and quasi-experimental studies comparing counsellor self-disclosure with non-disclosure. Overall, disclosure had a favourable effect: clients viewed disclosing counsellors more positively and said they would be more willing to open up to them, and disclosures that revealed similarity between counsellor and client were especially helpful. In 2018 Hill, Knox and Pinto-Coelho reviewed 21 studies of real therapy and found that self-disclosure and immediacy were most often followed by a stronger relationship, improved functioning, new insight and a sense that the session had helped. The research and clinical experience agree on when disclosure helps and when it harms (Table 8.20).

Table 8.20 When therapist self-disclosure tends to help and when it tends to harm.
Tends to help when it isTends to harm when it is
Infrequent and briefFrequent or long
For the client's benefit: to normalise, validate, model coping, offer a new view or strengthen the allianceFor the therapist's benefit: to be liked, to unburden, to fill a silence
About past experiences the therapist has resolvedAbout current, unresolved problems
Relevant to the client's concern, and followed by a return of focus to the clientA shift of focus to the therapist that the client must manage
Revealing similarity, in a strong allianceIntimate (sexual or financial) or blurring the professional boundary
A short, honest answer to a culturally ordinary questionUsed with clients whose boundaries or sense of self are fragile, where it may confuse

Back to the client who asked about loss. A rigid refusal ("We are here to talk about you") can feel like a slap, and in an Indian setting, where questions such as "Are you married?" or "Do you have children?" are ordinary courtesy, it can damage the alliance before it has formed. A brief, honest answer followed by a return to the client usually works better: "Yes, I lost my mother some years ago. I wonder whether you are asking if I can understand what you are going through?"

Exam trap

Freud's "opaque ... like a mirror" (1912) is the classical case against disclosure; Jourard's The Transparent Self (1964) and the dyadic effect (disclosure begets disclosure) are the humanistic case for it. A self-involving statement (immediacy) concerns the therapist's here-and-now reaction to the client; a self-disclosing statement concerns the therapist's life outside. Hill's four content types: facts, feelings, insight, strategies. Disclosure is a boundary crossing, not in itself a boundary violation (Section 3.3).

Homework

A client spends perhaps one hour a week with a therapist and the other 167 hours living his life (Section 5.4). Homework, or between-session assignments, is any planned task the client carries out between sessions to practise, test or apply what has been learned in them. It is central in cognitive and behavioural therapies (thought records, behavioural experiments, activity scheduling, exposure practice, relaxation practice, the diary cards of dialectical behaviour therapy), common in family and couple therapy (Chapter 7), solution-focused therapy (Section 6.8) and interpersonal therapy, and used occasionally even by Gestalt therapists, who set experiments to try during the week. Classical psychodynamic therapy is the main exception.

Why should homework help? Several reasons work together. Practice strengthens new skills and makes them automatic. Carrying them out in real life helps them generalise beyond the consulting room. Self-monitoring collects data that cannot be gathered in the session. Behavioural experiments test beliefs where they matter. Doing the work oneself builds self-efficacy, because the client can attribute the change to his own effort. And homework gives continuity between sessions and rehearses the independence the client will need after termination.

Key study: Kazantzis, Deane and Ronan's meta-analysis of homework (2000)

Question: Does homework improve the outcome of cognitive and behavioural therapy, and do clients who do more of it improve more? Method: Nikolaos Kazantzis, Frank Deane and Kevin Ronan pooled 27 studies with 1,702 clients, in Clinical Psychology: Science and Practice. Result: Studies comparing therapy with and without homework gave a weighted mean effect size of r = .36 in favour of homework; the relation between how much homework clients completed (compliance) and their outcome gave r = .22. Conclusion: Assigning homework improves outcome, and clients who do more of it tend to do better. Criticism: Compliance findings are correlational: clients who are already improving may find homework easier, which is the responsiveness problem again. Later meta-analyses broadly agree: Brent Mausbach and colleagues (2010) found r = .26 for compliance across 23 studies, and Kazantzis and colleagues (2016) showed that the quality of homework (how skilfully it is done) predicts outcome as well as its quantity.

The commonest problem with homework is that clients do not do it. They may not understand the task or its purpose, find it too hard, forget, fear doing it badly (perfectionists treat it as another examination, Section 2.9), lack the time or privacy, or feel that the therapist does not really care whether it is done. Experienced therapists improve homework compliance in several ways. They design the task together with the client and explain its rationale. They make it specific (what, when, where and how often) and small enough to succeed. They start it in the session and write it down. They ask "What might get in the way?" and plan for it, and they ask how confident the client feels about doing it, simplifying the task if the answer is lukewarm. Above all, they review it at the start of the next session, every time; a therapist who forgets to ask teaches the client that homework does not matter. Partial completion is praised, and non-completion is treated as useful information rather than failure. In India the word itself can be a problem, carrying memories of school, tuition and scolding; calling the task "practice", an "experiment" or a "between-session task" often helps.

Exam trap

The homework meta-analyses are by Kazantzis and colleagues: r = .36 for assigning homework and r = .22 for compliance (2000); Mausbach and colleagues found r = .26 for compliance (2010). Homework review comes near the start of a cognitive therapy session, after the mood check, bridge and agenda (Section 5.4), and new homework is set near the end. Homework is used across many therapies, not only CBT, but rarely in classical psychoanalysis.

How often, how long, and what to write down

Frequency. Freud saw his patients six days a week, and classical psychoanalysis still meets four or five times a week (Section 3.2). Most modern therapies meet weekly, often tapering to fortnightly or monthly towards the end. Is weekly the right rhythm? A 2013 meta-regression by Pim Cuijpers and colleagues of 70 trials of psychotherapy for adult depression, with 5,403 patients, found only a small association between the total number of sessions and the effect size, and none with total contact time or the duration of therapy once other features of the studies were taken into account. The number of sessions per week, however, was strongly associated with outcome: moving from one to two sessions a week, with the total number of sessions held constant, raised the effect size by about 0.45. In 2015 David Erekson, Lambert and Dennis Eggett examined 21,488 clients of a university counselling centre, tracked with the OQ-45 over 17 years, and found that clients who attended weekly reached clinically significant change faster than those who attended fortnightly, although the total amount of change was similar. A Dutch trial that randomly assigned 200 depressed patients to cognitive behaviour therapy or interpersonal psychotherapy, once or twice a week, found twice-weekly sessions more effective on average (Sanne Bruijniks and colleagues, 2020), though a later analysis of the same trial suggested that some patients did better with weekly sessions. The practical lesson is to concentrate sessions early, when the client is most distressed and most at risk of dropping out, and to space them out later.

Length. The conventional session is the "fifty-minute hour", which leaves the therapist a few minutes for notes before the next client. But there is nothing sacred about it. Brief sessions of 20 to 30 minutes are common in primary care and with children; family and couple sessions often run longer; prolonged exposure for post-traumatic stress disorder usually uses 90-minute sessions so that anxiety has time to fall; and some treatments are deliberately massed into a single long meeting. In 1989 Lars-Göran Öst described a one-session treatment for specific phobias, combining exposure and modelling, that averaged 2.1 hours; at follow-up after an average of four years, 90 percent of his first 20 patients were much improved or completely recovered. What matters as much as the length is its predictability: the same time, the same place and a clear start and finish are part of the frame that makes therapy feel safe (Section 3.3).

Notes. Every session ends with a record. Notes preserve continuity when memory fades, communicate with colleagues, supervisors and anyone who later takes over the case, protect client and therapist if a complaint or a court case arises, allow audit and research, and are increasingly open to the client himself. A good note records the date and attendance; the client's state and any change in mental status; the risk assessment and any action taken; the main themes and interventions; the client's response; homework and outcome scores; contacts with family or other professionals; and the plan for next time. It is signed and dated.

Several formats help. The best known is the SOAP note, borrowed from Lawrence Weed's problem-oriented medical record of the late 1960s: Subjective (what the client reports), Objective (what the clinician observes, including test scores and mental status), Assessment (the clinician's interpretation and judgement of progress) and Plan (what happens next). The DAP note (data, assessment, plan) merges the first two headings. Clinicians also distinguish progress notes, the official, concise and factual record kept in the case file, from process notes, the therapist's private and more detailed reflections on what happened in a session, including hypotheses and his own reactions, which are often written for supervision (Section 8.12). In some countries the law gives such private notes extra protection.

The principles of note-keeping are simple. Write on the same day. Describe behaviour rather than attach labels: "shouted, walked out ten minutes early, returned and apologised" says more, and more fairly, than "hostile". Be concise and avoid jargon. Never alter a note after the event; add a dated addition instead. Store notes securely. And write every note as though the client, a supervisor and a judge will one day read it, because any of them may. The legal and ethical rules on records, confidentiality and clients' access to their files are set out in Chapter 1.

Clinical link

Interviewers sometimes ask a trainee, "What would you write after a session?" A clear answer uses a structure: "A SOAP note. Subjective: what he told me, in his words where it matters. Objective: what I saw and his questionnaire score. Assessment: my judgement of progress and risk. Plan: the homework, the next appointment and anything I need to discuss in supervision." Add that you would document any risk assessment carefully and keep personal reflections for supervision separate from the case record.

Exam trap

Cuijpers and colleagues (2013): the number of sessions per week predicted outcome much more strongly than the total number of sessions. Weekly sessions produced faster recovery than fortnightly ones (Erekson, Lambert and Eggett, 2015). SOAP = subjective, objective, assessment, plan. Progress notes are the official record; process notes are the therapist's private reflections. Öst's one-session treatment is for specific phobias.

8.11 Combining and Adapting Therapies: Integration, Culture and Therapy in IndiaLower yield · 1 Q

Gordon Paul's question from Section 8.1 asked what treatment, by whom, is most effective for this individual with that specific problem, and under which circumstances. The sections since have answered it piece by piece, and two of the answers have awkward consequences for the way therapy is organised. If bona fide therapies built on rival theories produce broadly similar results (Section 8.3), and if much of what heals is shared by all of them (Section 8.4), why should a therapist stay loyal to one school? And if every therapy grew up in a particular place (Vienna, London, the universities of North America), does it travel unchanged to a joint family in Mandya or a slum resettlement colony in Delhi? This section takes up both questions: first the movement to combine therapies, called psychotherapy integration, and then the adaptation of therapy to culture, ending with the answers Indian clinicians have given since the 1960s.

Why therapists began to combine therapies

For the first half of the twentieth century a psychotherapist was usually trained inside one school and stayed there. Schools had their own journals, institutes and heroes, and moving between them was treated almost as disloyalty. Then the schools multiplied. In 2001 Raymond Corsini summarised 69 new and innovative therapies, and by 2008 the count stood at more than 400. Most had little research behind them, but their number alone made the old question "Which school is right?" look less and less answerable.

What therapists actually do turned out to be different from what the textbooks implied. When James Prochaska and John Norcross combined several American surveys of more than 1,500 psychologists, counsellors, psychiatrists and social workers in 2010, the most common answer to "What is your primary theoretical orientation?" among clinical psychologists was not psychoanalytic, behavioural or cognitive but eclectic or integrative, at about 29 percent. Cognitive therapy was a close second at about 28 percent, psychodynamic therapy was chosen by about 12 percent and behaviour therapy by about 10 percent. Even therapists who name a single orientation usually borrow techniques from others. In practice, combining therapies is the rule rather than the exception.

Why did this happen? John C. Norcross, the most prolific chronicler of the movement, lists a set of reasons that are worth knowing because each connects to an earlier section of this chapter. There were too many therapies for any one to claim the whole truth. No single theory proved adequate for every client and every problem. The outcome research of Sections 8.2 and 8.3 showed that rival therapies produced similar results, which weakened the case for loyalty to any one of them. The search for common factors (Section 8.4) suggested that therapies share their most important ingredients. Treatment manuals (Section 8.7) let therapists see in detail what other schools actually did in the room. Insurers and health services pushed for brief, problem-focused treatment, which encouraged therapists to use whatever worked fastest. And by the 1980s there was a professional network to support the work: the Society for the Exploration of Psychotherapy Integration (SEPI), whose founders included the cognitive-behaviourist Marvin R. Goldfried and the psychoanalyst Paul L. Wachtel, and from 1991 its Journal of Psychotherapy Integration. The field's standard reference, the Handbook of Psychotherapy Integration edited by Norcross and Goldfried, appeared in 1992.

The idea itself is older than the movement. In 1950 two Yale psychologists, John Dollard (1900 to 1980) and Neal E. Miller (1909 to 2002), published Personality and Psychotherapy, which translated the central ideas of psychoanalysis (conflict, repression, transference) into the language of learning theory (drive, cue, response and reinforcement). A neurotic conflict, for Dollard and Miller, was an approach-avoidance conflict that had been learned, and repression was a learned avoidance of thinking certain thoughts because thinking them had been followed by anxiety. The book showed that two theories which looked incompatible could be made to speak to each other, and it remained a point of reference for every later attempt.

Two words need to be kept apart. Eclecticism (from a Greek word meaning "to select") means choosing what seems to work from several sources without necessarily joining their theories together. Psychotherapy integration is the broader effort to combine therapies into a coherent whole, at the level of techniques, theories or shared principles. Integration is the umbrella term today, and most writers treat systematic eclecticism as one form of it. What both camps reject is the unsystematic mixing of whatever a therapist happens to like, with no rationale for when or why each technique is used; critics call this syncretism, and it is the caricature of eclecticism that gave the word a bad name.

Exam trap

In the large American surveys the single most common orientation among clinical psychologists is eclectic or integrative (about 29 percent, Prochaska and Norcross, 2010), with cognitive a close second; it is not psychoanalysis. An early landmark of integration is Dollard and Miller's Personality and Psychotherapy (1950), which joined psychoanalysis and learning theory. Eclecticism selects; integration combines into a whole; unsystematic mixing is syncretism.

The four routes to integration

Integration can be attempted at different levels. In 1989 Hal Arkowitz described three strands in the movement: technical eclecticism, theoretical integration and the common factors approach. In 1992 Stanley B. Messer named a fourth, assimilative integration. These four routes are now the standard classification, and Table 8.21 sets them side by side before we take each in turn.

Table 8.21 The four routes to psychotherapy integration.
RouteWhat is combinedBest-known examplesStrengthWeakness
Technical eclecticismTechniques, chosen by evidence and by client characteristics; theories are not mergedLazarus's multimodal therapy (BASIC I.D.); Beutler's systematic treatment selectionPractical, flexible, tied to evidenceCan lack a unifying account of why change happens
Theoretical integrationTwo or more theories, fused into a new frameworkWachtel's cyclical psychodynamics; Prochaska and DiClemente's transtheoretical approach; Ryle's cognitive analytic therapyConceptual depth and coherenceHard to reconcile rival assumptions; hard to test
Common factors integrationThe ingredients all therapies shareFrank's shared features of healing; Goldfried's change principlesRests on the outcome evidence of Sections 8.3 and 8.4Can be vague about what to do next
Assimilative integrationOne home theory, plus techniques imported from othersMesser; Stricker and Gold's assimilative psychodynamic therapyKeeps a stable framework while gaining flexibilityImports may be distorted by the home theory

Technical eclecticism. The first route borrows techniques but not theories. The term technical eclecticism was introduced in 1967 by Arnold Allan Lazarus (1932 to 2013), whose multimodal therapy you met in Section 5.5. Lazarus assessed every client across seven modalities, the BASIC I.D. (Behaviour, Affect, Sensation, Imagery, Cognition, Interpersonal relationships, Drugs and biology), and then chose, problem by problem, whichever technique had the best evidence, whatever school it came from. He could use the Gestalt empty chair without accepting Gestalt theory; his own guiding framework remained social and cognitive learning theory. Lazarus was openly suspicious of the second route, warning that blending incompatible theories produces confusion rather than insight.

A second technical eclectic, Larry E. Beutler, tried to make the choice of technique more systematic. In Systematic Treatment Selection (1990), written with John Clarkin, he argued that the question is not which therapy is best but which features of treatment fit which features of the client. Systematic treatment selection matches treatment to a handful of client dimensions. Greater functional impairment calls for more intensive treatment. Coping style matters: clients with an externalising style (who act out, blame others and avoid inner experience) tend to do better with symptom-focused, skill-building methods, while clients with an internalising style (self-critical, withdrawn, inclined to blame themselves) tend to do better with insight-oriented and interpersonal work. The client's level of reactance, the tendency to resist being directed, decides how directive the therapist should be: a highly reactant client who bristles at instructions does better with a non-directive, self-paced approach, while a client low in reactance accepts and benefits from direct guidance. Subjective distress is managed so that it stays in a workable middle range: support for clients overwhelmed by it, and raised arousal for clients too comfortable to work. The reactance finding is the best supported; a 2011 meta-analysis of 12 studies by Beutler and colleagues found a large advantage (an effect size of about 0.8) when the directiveness of therapy was matched to the client's level of resistance.

Theoretical integration. The second route goes deeper and tries to join the theories themselves. Theoretical integration combines the concepts of two or more theories into a new framework that is meant to be better than either parent. Its best-known example is Paul Wachtel's cyclical psychodynamics, set out in Psychoanalysis and Behavior Therapy: Toward an Integration (1977). Wachtel was trained as a psychoanalyst, but he became dissatisfied with what he called the "woolly mammoth" view of the unconscious, the idea that childhood wishes and fears are preserved unchanged, like a mammoth frozen in ice, and simply play themselves out in adult life. In his view the past persists because the present keeps recreating it. Inner conflicts shape how a person behaves; that behaviour draws predictable reactions from other people; and those reactions confirm and strengthen the original conflict. The other people become, in Wachtel's word, accomplices in keeping the pattern alive. Because the problem runs in a circle, it can be attacked at any point on the circle: through insight into the conflict, through behavioural methods such as exposure and assertion training that change what the person does, and through the changed responses of others that follow.

A worked example shows the circle. Shalini, a 30-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru, grew up feeling that her love was welcome only when she pleased everyone. Married into a joint family, she agrees to every request from her in-laws, says nothing when she is overloaded, and seethes inside. Because she never objects, her in-laws reasonably assume she is content and ask for more; their extra demands feed her resentment and her secret conviction that her own wishes do not count, which makes her even less able to speak. A purely psychoanalytic therapist might work only on the childhood conflict; a purely behavioural one might teach assertion without asking why it feels so dangerous. Wachtel would do both, and would expect that the first small act of assertion, by changing how the in-laws respond, would itself loosen the old conviction.

The second classic example of theoretical integration is the transtheoretical approach of James O. Prochaska and Carlo C. DiClemente, set out in their 1984 book The Transtheoretical Approach: Crossing Traditional Boundaries of Therapy. Section 6.9 described its stages of change. What makes it integrative is that it assigns the contributions of the rival schools to different moments of change. The stages say when change happens; the processes of change say how, and they were drawn from many schools (consciousness raising from the insight therapies, counterconditioning and stimulus control from behaviour therapy, the helping relationship from Rogers); and the levels of change say what is to be changed, from symptoms and situations, through maladaptive cognitions, current interpersonal conflicts and family or systems conflicts, to intrapersonal conflicts. Insight-oriented processes are most useful in the early stages and action-oriented ones in the later stages, so the approach tells the therapist not which school is right but when each school is useful. A third example, Anthony Ryle's cognitive analytic therapy, which joins cognitive psychology and personal construct theory to psychoanalytic object relations theory, was described in Section 5.7.

Common factors integration. The third route builds therapy around what the schools already share. Common factors integration starts from the evidence of Sections 8.3 and 8.4: if Jerome Frank was right that every healing practice works through a confiding relationship, a healing setting, a credible rationale and a ritual, then a therapist should deliberately strengthen those ingredients whatever techniques she uses. In 1980 Goldfried proposed looking for principles of change at a level of abstraction between theory and technique, the level at which the schools agree, and named two candidates: providing the client with new corrective experiences, and giving direct feedback that helps the client see himself more accurately. Later writers built whole approaches on this footing, most visibly The Heart and Soul of Change (edited by Mark Hubble, Barry Duncan and Scott Miller, 1999), which urged therapists to organise their work around the client's resources, the alliance and hope, and to check progress with the client session by session (Section 8.9).

Assimilative integration. The fourth route is the one most practising therapists actually follow. Assimilative integration keeps a single home theory and imports techniques from other approaches into it. Messer's important point was that an imported technique does not arrive unchanged: it takes on the meaning of its new home. When a psychodynamic therapist uses the Gestalt empty chair, the exercise becomes a way of bringing an internalised parent into the room and exploring the client's relationship with that figure, not a Gestalt experiment in awareness. George Stricker and Jerry Gold described an assimilative psychodynamic psychotherapy that adds behavioural, cognitive and experiential techniques to a psychodynamic framework, and Louis Castonguay's integrative cognitive therapy for depression adds humanistic and interpersonal methods for repairing alliance ruptures (Section 8.5) to a cognitive therapy base. In surveys of integrative therapists, assimilative integration is among the most commonly endorsed forms. Figure 8.14 shows how the four routes differ in what they do with theory and with technique.

Return to Shalini to see the four routes side by side. A technical eclectic would map her problems across the BASIC I.D. and choose assertion training for her silence, relaxation for her headaches and cognitive work on "my wishes do not count", without worrying about where each technique came from. Wachtel, as you saw, would work the whole circle from conflict to behaviour to the in-laws' responses. A common factors therapist would concentrate on a strong alliance, a rationale that makes sense to Shalini and her husband, and the hope that change is possible, and would let the techniques follow. A psychodynamic therapist working assimilatively would keep the focus on the repeated relationship pattern and the feelings it stirs in the therapy, and might borrow a behavioural role-play of saying no to her mother-in-law, treating it as a way of living through the old fear in a safer relationship.

Is integration better than pure-form therapy? The honest answer is that "integration" is not one treatment and cannot be tested as one. Some integrative therapies have good trial evidence (cognitive analytic therapy, dialectical behaviour therapy, which joins behaviour therapy to Zen acceptance, Section 5.8, and the systematic matching of directiveness to reactance), while the everyday eclecticism of many practitioners has never been tested at all. Integration also asks a great deal of training: a therapist who combines four schools must understand each of them well enough to know what she is borrowing.

Exam trap

Match the name to the route. Lazarus: technical eclecticism (term coined 1967), multimodal therapy, BASIC I.D. Beutler: systematic treatment selection (with Clarkin, 1990), also a form of technical eclecticism. Wachtel: theoretical integration, cyclical psychodynamics, psychoanalysis plus behaviour therapy (1977). Prochaska and DiClemente: the transtheoretical approach, also theoretical integration (stages, processes, levels). Frank and Goldfried: common factors. Messer: assimilative integration (1992). Arkowitz (1989) named the first three strands; Messer added the fourth. Do not confuse Arnold Lazarus with Richard Lazarus, whose appraisal theory of stress belongs to the General book.

Culture and the therapy room: adapting the treatment

Every therapy in this book carries assumptions from the culture that produced it. Most of them assume that the client is an individual who makes his own decisions; that talking about feelings to a stranger is healthy; that the therapist and client meet as near equals; that the hour is fifty minutes and the client comes alone; that what is said is confidential to the client; and that insight, independence and self-expression are signs of health. None of these is a law of nature. In 1962 the American counselling psychologist C. Gilbert Wrenn warned against the culturally encapsulated counsellor, one who takes the assumptions of his own culture to be universal, ignores cultural variation among clients, and substitutes a stereotype for the real person in front of him.

Cross-cultural psychology gives a useful pair of terms for this problem. They were coined in 1954 by the linguist Kenneth L. Pike, who borrowed them from the words phonetic (sounds described by universal criteria) and phonemic (sounds as they matter inside one language). An etic approach describes behaviour with concepts assumed to apply in every culture; an emic approach describes behaviour from inside a culture, in the terms its own members use. Much of the history of cross-cultural therapy is the discovery that concepts assumed to be etic (the value of autonomy, the meaning of dependence, the right to confidentiality) are in fact the emic concepts of Western middle-class culture.

Ignoring culture leads, first of all, to wrong assessments. Picture a family therapist, trained abroad, who sees a family from rural Karnataka. The married son consults his mother before every decision, the young wife says almost nothing in front of her in-laws, and the parents expect to attend every session. A therapist who does not know how such households usually work may record "enmeshment", label the son dependent and the wife depressed or resistant, and push the son to "separate" from his parents, when what she is seeing is a normal pattern of roles in that family's culture. The result is a wrong assessment and a misinterpretation of culturally ordinary behaviour as pathology, and very likely a ruptured alliance and a family that does not return. The same mistake can run the other way, when real distress is dismissed as "just their culture". Dropout is the visible cost: in a study of community mental health services in Seattle, Stanley Sue found in 1977 that about half of ethnic minority clients did not return after the first session, compared with about 30 percent of white clients.

The remedy most researched is cultural adaptation, the systematic modification of an evidence-based treatment so that it fits the language, values and circumstances of the people receiving it while keeping its active ingredients. Section 5.11 introduced the best-known framework, the ecological validity framework of Guillermo Bernal, Janet Bonilla and Carmen Bellido (1995), with its eight dimensions that can be adapted: language, persons, metaphors, content, concepts, goals, methods and context. A second distinction, from the public health researcher Ken Resnicow and his colleagues (1999), sorts adaptations by depth. Surface structure adaptation matches what is visible: the language of the materials, the names, foods, places and examples used, the ethnicity of the therapist or the pictures on a leaflet. It makes a treatment acceptable and familiar. Deep structure adaptation builds in the cultural, social, historical and religious forces that shape how people understand the problem and its cure: beliefs about the causes of illness, family roles and obligations, ideas of fate and duty, stigma, and the place of elders and healers. It is what gives the treatment its impact.

Apply the distinction to cognitive behaviour therapy for depression in a district town in Karnataka. Surface adaptation would translate the worksheets into Kannada, use local proverbs and examples from village life, and replace written thought records with pictures for clients who cannot read. Deep adaptation would go further: it would explain depression in a way that fits local beliefs about "tension" and weakness, set goals in terms of the client's duties to the family as well as his own wishes, involve the family in planning activities, and treat beliefs about karma as part of the client's world to be worked with rather than as distortions to be disputed.

Adaptation of an imported treatment ("top-down") is not the only way. Some cultures built their own therapies from the bottom up, starting from local traditions of healing. Japan offers the best-known examples. Morita therapy, originated by the psychiatrist Shoma Morita (1874 to 1938) early in the twentieth century, treats anxious self-preoccupation through a period of isolated bed rest followed by graded, purposeful work, teaching the patient to accept feelings as they are and to act constructively regardless; Naikan therapy, developed by Ishin Yoshimoto in the 1940s from a Buddhist practice of self-examination, has clients reflect systematically on what others have given them, what they have given in return, and the trouble they have caused. Both rest on cultural ideals of acceptance and obligation to others, and both have been exported to the West in adapted form.

Does cultural adaptation make therapy work better? Several meta-analyses agree that it does, though by a modest amount. The evidence is summarised in the Key study box. Two findings stand out. Adaptations help most when a treatment is designed for one specific cultural group rather than for a mixed one, and when it is delivered in the client's own language. And the most important single adaptation seems to be the one Jerome Frank would have predicted (Section 8.4): changing the explanation of the illness and its cure so that it makes sense within the client's own worldview.

Key study: Does adapting therapy to culture help? (2006 to 2018)

Question: Are culturally adapted treatments more effective than standard ones for clients from ethnic and cultural minority groups? Method: Successive meta-analyses. Griner and Timothy Smith (2006) pooled 76 studies; Smith, Melanie Domenech Rodríguez and Bernal (2011) pooled 65; Benish, Quintana and Bruce Wampold (2011) included only direct comparisons of an adapted therapy with the same kind of bona fide therapy unadapted; Gordon Nagayama Hall and colleagues (2016) pooled 78 studies. Results: Griner and Smith found an average effect size of d = 0.45 for adapted interventions, with interventions targeted at one cultural group about four times as effective as those for mixed groups, and those delivered in the client's own language (where it was not English) about twice as effective. Benish and colleagues found adapted therapy better than unadapted bona fide therapy by d = 0.32, and the only adaptation that predicted this advantage was adapting the illness myth, the explanation of the problem and its cure. Hall and colleagues found an effect of 0.52 in favour of adapted over unadapted versions of the same intervention. Conclusion: Culturally adapted treatments do somewhat better, and the explanation offered to the client matters most. Criticism: A 2018 update of 99 studies found the average effect shrank from about 0.50 to about 0.35 once publication bias was allowed for, and many studies compared adapted treatment with no treatment rather than with the unadapted version.

There is a real tension here, sometimes called fidelity versus fit. A treatment adapted too freely may lose the very ingredients that made it work in trials; a treatment delivered with perfect fidelity to a manual written for another culture may never be accepted by the people it is meant to help. The working rule most researchers accept is that adaptation should change the packaging, the language, examples, explanations, goals and delivery, while protecting the active ingredients that the evidence identifies.

Exam trap

If a question asks what follows from poor awareness of a family's culture and customary practices, the answer is wrong assessments and misinterpretations (culturally normal behaviour read as pathology), not accurate understanding, effective communication or a strong alliance. Bernal, Bonilla and Bellido (1995) gave the ecological validity framework with eight dimensions; Resnicow (1999) gave surface versus deep structure; Wrenn (1962) gave the culturally encapsulated counsellor; Pike (1954) gave emic and etic. Adapted treatments do somewhat better (roughly d = 0.3 to 0.5), most when targeted at one group, in the client's language, and with an adapted illness explanation.

The culturally competent therapist

Adapting a treatment manual is one level of the answer. The other is the therapist. The person most associated with this second level is Derald Wing Sue (born 1942), a Chinese American psychologist whose book Counseling the Culturally Different (1981) became a standard text. In a 1982 position paper Sue and his colleagues proposed that cross-cultural counselling competence has three components, and in 1992, with Patricia Arredondo and Roderick McDavis, he expanded them into a set of multicultural counselling competencies that professional bodies adopted. The three components are the heart of what is now called multicultural competence.

  • Awareness (beliefs and attitudes): the therapist is aware of her own cultural background, values and biases, and of how they shape her view of what is normal, healthy and desirable.
  • Knowledge: the therapist understands the worldview, history, values and circumstances of the client's cultural group, including experiences of discrimination and the ways that group usually seeks help.
  • Skills: the therapist can choose and adapt intervention strategies, and ways of communicating, that suit the client's culture, and can work with family members, interpreters and traditional healers when that helps.

Sue's model told therapists what to know; Stanley Sue, a fellow Chinese American psychologist (and Derald's brother), asked what actually happens in the room that makes culture matter. With Nolan Zane he argued in 1987 that knowledge of a culture and culture-specific techniques matter only because they affect two more immediate processes. The first is credibility, the client's perception that the therapist is trustworthy and effective. Ascribed credibility is given by the client's culture because of the therapist's position: age, gender, profession, status. Achieved credibility is earned by what the therapist does: a formulation that makes sense to the client, goals the client shares, methods that seem likely to work. The second is gift-giving: the client's sense of receiving something of value early in treatment, such as relief from anxiety, a clear explanation, reassurance that the problem is understandable, a useful skill or renewed hope. A first session that sends the client home with nothing but questions is a poor start for a client who expected help.

In 1998 Stanley Sue added three characteristics of a culturally competent therapist: scientific mindedness (forming hypotheses about clients of other cultures and testing them, rather than jumping to conclusions); dynamic sizing (knowing when to generalise from what is known about a group and when to treat the person as an individual who may not fit it); and culture-specific expertise. More recently many writers have preferred the idea of cultural humility (Joshua Hook and colleagues, 2013): an interpersonal stance that is open and curious about the client's cultural identity, aware of the limits of one's own understanding, and committed to lifelong self-examination, rather than a claim to have "mastered" another culture. Derald Wing Sue and colleagues also drew attention in 2007 to racial microaggressions, the brief, everyday slights, often unintended, that communicate disrespect to members of minority groups and can quietly damage an alliance.

Does therapist competence of this kind matter for outcome? A 2018 meta-analysis of 15 studies gave a striking answer that depends on who does the rating. When clients rated their therapists' cultural competence, those ratings correlated about 0.38 with treatment outcomes; when therapists rated their own competence, the correlation was close to zero (about 0.06). Therapists, in other words, are poor judges of their own cultural competence, which is one more reason to ask clients how the therapy is going (Section 8.9).

Clinical link

A 26-year-old psychologist meets a retired headmaster of 64 who has been brought by his son for depression after his wife's death. Ascribed credibility is not on her side: she is young, female and the age of his granddaughter. She can earn it. She addresses him respectfully, asks about his career, explains his sleeplessness and loss of appetite as an understandable response to grief that also has a treatable side, and ends the first session with one concrete step (a morning walk with his old colleagues) and a clear plan. The explanation and the first step are Sue and Zane's gift; his return the next week is the achieved credibility.

Exam trap

D. W. Sue's three components of multicultural competence are awareness (of one's own values and biases), knowledge (of the client's worldview) and skills (culturally appropriate interventions). Sue and Zane (1987) gave credibility (ascribed versus achieved) and gift-giving. Stanley Sue (1998) gave scientific mindedness, dynamic sizing and culture-specific expertise. Client ratings of therapist cultural competence predict outcome; therapist self-ratings do not.

Psychotherapy in India

In 1964 (some later reviews give 1968) the Indian Journal of Psychiatry printed a short paper that had been read at a conference of Indian psychiatrists. Its authors, N. C. Surya and S. S. Jayaram, began by pointing at the programme in their hands: "The paucity of papers submitted on psychotherapy at this Conference gives some food for thought." Western psychiatrists, they observed, seemed keener on the theory and practice of psychotherapy in the Indian setting than Indian psychiatrists were. Their paper, "Some basic considerations in the practice of psychotherapy in the Indian setting", was short, but it set the agenda for the debate that followed, and the journal reprinted it in 1996.

Surya and Jayaram made five arguments. The first was about language. The Indian psychiatrist, they wrote, had learned his medicine and psychiatry in a language and in conceptual frameworks foreign to the milieu of his birth; for him the English words of psychiatry often had only a "desiccated, cognitive significance" and a prestige value, and for his patients they had little meaning at all, even when the patients understood English. Words carry motivation and feeling as well as information, and a therapy conducted in borrowed words would be weak. The second was about what the Indian patient expects, which they traced to upbringing in a joint family. Compared with his Western counterpart, they wrote, the Indian patient is "more ready to expect and accept dependency relationships; more ready to accept overt situational support, less ready to seek intrapsychic explanations; more insistent and importunate with regard to personal needs and time; more ready to discard ego-bounds and involve the therapist in direct role relationship; and finally his ideal or idealised support is the good joint-family elder." A therapist who did not consciously understand and fulfil that role, they warned, would fail.

Their third point was that Indian patients of every class orient themselves by concepts such as karma and dharma and by traditional figures, rather than by Western ideas of conscience or superego. The fourth was the most provocative: the Western ideal of mental health implies the search for intrapsychic integration, a single consistent self, whereas the implied Indian ideal allows a degree of separation between thinking, feeling and acting, so that "rigid, mutually exclusive consistencies" are a product of Western thought. The fifth used a story. In the legend of Savitri, whose husband Satyavan is fated to die, Savitri walks alongside Yama, the lord of death, wins his friendship by walking seven steps with him, and, when he offers her a boon, asks to be the mother of a hundred sons by Satyavan; Yama grants it, and only then discovers that he must return Satyavan's life to keep his word. For Surya and Jayaram the legend contained two principles of therapy in the Indian setting: first walk alongside the patient, and then work through an implied goal, an acceptable overt aim whose fulfilment requires a necessary but unwelcome intermediate step. They ended by asking psychiatrists to take religion and faith seriously as part of how people make sense of the world, not to dismiss them.

In 1973 Jaswant Singh Neki, whom you met in Section 3.10, offered a positive model in the American Journal of Orthopsychiatry: "Guru-chela relationship: the possibility of a therapeutic paradigm" (1973). The guru-chela relationship (teacher and disciple) is a voluntary relationship in which a person seeking change accepts the guidance of a master who has gone further along the same path. The guru is active, benevolent and directive; he takes responsibility for leading the disciple, helps directly with decisions where needed, and acts as a model; the chela accepts guidance and, for a time, depends on him. The goal is not lifelong obedience but the disciple's growth towards self-mastery. Neki argued that this, rather than the neutral, egalitarian, interpretive stance of Western psychotherapy, was the relationship many Indian patients were looking for.

The heart of Neki's argument was about dependence. In two papers of 1976 in the British Journal of Medical Psychology, "An examination of the cultural relativism of dependence as a dynamic of social and therapeutic relationships", he argued that Western developmental theory treats dependence as immaturity to be outgrown and, in therapy, as something to be interpreted and resolved, whereas in Indian family life dependence is expected, accepted and answered by dependability: the child and the young adult depend, and the elders make themselves dependable. What is a symptom in one culture is a norm in another. A therapist who confronts dependence too early, or treats it as pathology, will lose the client; a therapist who accepts it and works through it gradually, as the guru does, may lead the client to a mature interdependence. Neki also proposed an Indian ideal of mental health, sahaja (1975), a natural state marked by illumination, equipoise, spontaneity, freedom and harmony, and in a 1992 editorial he argued that Western notions of confidentiality and privacy have no ready equivalents in Indian languages and can seem to cut a person off from an interdependent family, so that a few sessions with the family alongside individual therapy usually help.

Not everyone accepted the paradigm. Neki returned to it in a 1974 reappraisal, and critics objected that it could foster lasting dependency, since the guru takes total responsibility for leading the disciple. Later Indian writers welcomed its cultural fit but warned that it can become an excuse for paternalism, a therapist who simply tells clients what to do. In 1982 V. K. Varma, in a review titled "Present state of psychotherapy in India", listed seven features of Indian clients that he thought made Western therapy hard to apply: dependence or interdependence; lack of psychological sophistication; social distance between doctor and patient; religious belief in rebirth and fatalism; guilt attributed to misdeeds in a past life; problems with confidentiality; and reluctance to take personal responsibility for decisions. A survey of 32 Fellows of the Indian Psychiatric Society by Varma and Ghosh (1976) had already found that most of them practised short-term supportive therapy. Other Indian clinicians disagreed with the pessimists. K. Nagaraja Rao practised existential psychotherapy and argued that the critics were judging all Western therapy by psychoanalysis, and that existential philosophy is close to Indian thought; C. Shamasundar held that psychotherapy can be conducted effectively with Indian patients. A 2010 review of the Indian Journal of Psychiatry by L. S. S. Manickam, of JSS Medical College, Mysuru, added a further caution: with globalisation, rising education, greater awareness of human rights and the spread of electronic media even in rural areas, it is a fair question whether these generalisations about "the Indian patient" still hold today.

Three practical themes run through this literature and through Indian clinics today. The first is family involvement. Families usually accompany the client, decisions about treatment, work and marriage are often family decisions, and an inpatient may have a relative staying with him on the ward. Indian therapists therefore meet families early, agree with the client what will and will not be shared with them, use family members as allies in homework and relapse prevention, and remember that the family's view of the problem may differ from the client's. The second is the expectation of direction. Many clients expect advice, a clear plan and sometimes a prescription, and experience pure non-directive listening as indifference (Section 6.2). Indian clinicians usually respond by being directive about the structure of therapy (what we will do, how often, what to practise) while keeping decisions about the client's life collaborative, which is close to what Sue and Zane called giving a gift without taking over. The third is religion and idiom: beliefs about karma, fate and God are treated as resources to work with, and local idioms of distress are respected (Section 5.11).

Indian clinicians have also drawn directly on Indian traditions. The ancient medical text the Charaka Samhita classifies treatment into three kinds: daivavyapashraya (spiritual or divine therapy), yuktivyapashraya (rational therapy with medicines and diet) and sattvavajaya (literally "conquest of the mind"), a psychological therapy that restrains the mind from harmful objects and is often described as Ayurveda's psychotherapy. In Bombay, N. S. Vahia and his colleagues built a psychophysiologic therapy on the concepts of Patanjali's yoga, reporting it in the British Journal of Psychiatry in 1966 and the American Journal of Psychotherapy in 1973 as a new approach to neurotic and psychosomatic disorders; a 1977 comparison found it more helpful than drug treatment for stress-related neurotic disorders, and Vahia's work is often credited with opening yoga to psychiatric research in India. The meditation that underlies mindfulness-based therapies (Section 5.9) has Indian Buddhist roots in vipassana, and the modern use of yoga and meditation in Indian clinical settings is described in Chapter 10.

Indian stories have served as therapy texts too. The Bhagavad Gita is, in form, a counselling dialogue: on the battlefield of Kurukshetra the warrior Arjuna, overwhelmed by grief, guilt and physical symptoms of anxiety at the prospect of fighting his own kin, lays down his bow and asks Krishna for help ("I am your disciple; guide me", in the second chapter), and at the end of the eighteenth chapter declares that his delusion is gone, his doubts have vanished and he will act as Krishna directs. The psychiatrist M. S. Reddy (2012) read the dialogue as a model of crisis counselling within a guru-shishya relationship, and Dilip Jeste and Ipsit Vahia (2008) compared its account of wisdom with modern psychological definitions. Its central teaching of action without attachment to its fruits (chapter 2, verse 47) is often compared with the acceptance and values-based action of acceptance and commitment therapy (Section 5.9). N. N. Wig of the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh, described in 2004 what he called the Hanuman complex: in the Ramayana, Hanuman lost the knowledge of his own power to fly because of a childhood curse by sages, and regained it only when Jambavan reminded him of it during the search for Sita. Wig used the story with patients who had forgotten their own strengths, and with trainees, to show that a therapist's task is often to remind clients of capacities they already have. The psychoanalyst Sudhir Kakar's argument that the Indian self is organised around family relationships, and Alan Roland's "familial self", were described in Section 3.11. Table 8.22 collects the main Indian contributions.

Table 8.22 Indian contributions to thinking about psychotherapy.
WhoWhenContribution
N. C. Surya and S. S. Jayaram1964"Some basic considerations in the practice of psychotherapy in the Indian setting": language, the dependent and support-seeking patient, karma and dharma, the Savitri legend
N. S. Vahia and colleagues1966; 1973Psychophysiologic therapy based on Patanjali's yoga
J. S. Neki1973The guru-chela relationship as a therapeutic paradigm
J. S. Neki1975; 1976Sahaja as an Indian ideal of mental health; the cultural relativism of dependence
Sudhir Kakar1978The Inner World: the Indian self and childhood (Section 3.11)
