N. N. Wig2004The Hanuman complex: reminding clients of forgotten strengths
M. S. Reddy2012The Bhagavad Gita as a model of counselling
Indian context

Neeraj, a 19-year-old engineering student from Hassan, is brought to a clinic in Mysuru by his father after failing two semesters and refusing to leave his hostel room. The father does most of the talking and asks the psychologist to "make him study". A therapist following Surya, Jayaram and Neki would not send the father out and insist on a private hour with the young man; she would meet them together first, take the father's worry seriously, agree with both what will stay confidential, and then see Neeraj alone. With Neeraj she would be warm, clear and fairly directive about the plan, offering an early gift (an explanation of why he cannot concentrate and one achievable task for the week), while making sure that the decisions about his course remain his and his family's to make together. That is cultural adaptation in a single case: the active ingredients of good therapy, delivered in a form this family can accept.

Exam trap

The guru-chela paradigm is J. S. Neki's (1973, American Journal of Orthopsychiatry); dependence is accepted and worked through, not interpreted away. The 1964 paper on psychotherapy in the Indian setting (dependency, situational support, the joint-family elder, karma and dharma, the Savitri legend) is Surya and Jayaram's, not Neki's. Sahaja (1975) and the cultural relativism of dependence (1976) are Neki's; the Hanuman complex is Wig's (2004); Patanjali-based psychophysiologic therapy is Vahia's; sattvavajaya is the psychological therapy of the Charaka Samhita; The Inner World (1978) is Kakar's.

8.12 Becoming a Therapist: Training, Supervision, the Wounded Healer and Self-CareLower yield · not asked yet

Section 8.6 showed that therapists differ: some, treating comparable clients with the same method, get consistently better results than others. That finding turns the spotlight from the therapy to the person who delivers it, and raises the questions with which this chapter ends. How is a therapist made? How does a beginner learn to do something as subtle as building an alliance or noticing her own countertransference? Who watches over her work while she learns? And what do years of listening to suffering do to the listener? This section follows a therapist from training through supervision to the hazards of the work and the ways of surviving it.

Training models in brief

Two American conferences set the pattern for training clinical psychologists, and Chapter 1 tells their story in full. At the Boulder conference of 1949 the profession adopted the scientist-practitioner model: a clinical psychologist should be trained as both a researcher and a clinician, earning a research doctorate (the PhD) and bringing scientific thinking to practice. At the Vail conference of 1973 a second route was endorsed, the practitioner-scholar model, which places practice first, treats the clinician as an informed consumer of research rather than necessarily a producer of it, and leads to the professional Doctor of Psychology (PsyD) degree. A third, the clinical science model, later pushed in the opposite direction, emphasising research training above all. In India the route to practice is a professional qualification in clinical psychology recognised by the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI), for decades the two-year M.Phil and now the newer RCI-approved programmes, followed by registration with the RCI; Chapter 1 sets out the courses and the registration rules.

Whatever the model, the core of training is the same: coursework, then clinical work with real clients under close supervision, with the trainee's independence growing as her competence does. Experience alone is not enough. In a 2016 study, Simon Goldberg and his colleagues followed 170 therapists in one university counselling service over several years and found that their clients' outcomes did not improve as the therapists gained experience; on average they declined slightly. What seems to improve therapists is not the passage of time but feedback on their outcomes and deliberate practice of the skills they lack (Sections 8.6 and 8.9). The most important vehicle of that feedback during training is supervision, so much so that supervision is often called the signature teaching method of the mental health professions.

Exam trap

Boulder (1949): scientist-practitioner model, PhD. Vail (1973): practitioner-scholar model, PsyD. Do not swap the conferences or the degrees. The clinical science model is a later, more research-centred position. Clinical experience by itself does not reliably improve a therapist's outcomes; feedback and deliberate practice do.

What clinical supervision is and what it is for

The most widely quoted definition comes from Janine M. Bernard and Rodney K. Goodyear, whose Fundamentals of Clinical Supervision (first published in 1992) is the standard text. Clinical supervision, in their definition, is an intervention provided by a more senior member of a profession to a more junior member or members of that same profession. The relationship is evaluative and hierarchical, it extends over time, and it has three simultaneous purposes: enhancing the professional functioning of the junior person; monitoring the quality of the services offered to the clients she sees; and acting as a gatekeeper for those who wish to enter the profession. That third purpose, gatekeeping, is the uncomfortable one: a supervisor must sometimes decide that a trainee is not yet safe to practise, and say so.

The definition separates supervision from three activities it resembles. It is not consultation, which takes place between professionals who may be equals, is voluntary and leaves responsibility for the client with the person seeking advice, who is free to reject it (Section 9.6 describes Caplan's types of mental health consultation); a supervisor, by contrast, shares responsibility for the supervisee's clients and evaluates her. It is not teaching in a classroom, because it is tied to particular clients and particular sessions. And it is not therapy for the supervisee: a supervisor may explore the trainee's feelings about a client, but only insofar as they affect the work. If a trainee's personal difficulties go deeper, the supervisor's job is to recommend that she seek therapy of her own, not to provide it, because combining the roles of evaluator and therapist creates a dual relationship (Chapter 1).

What does supervision actually do? The classic answer comes from social work. In Supervision in Social Work (1976), Alfred Kadushin named three functions: administrative, educational and supportive. A decade later the British counselling writer Brigid Proctor (1986) described the same three under different names, normative, formative and restorative, and both sets of terms appear in exam questions. Table 8.23 lines them up.

Table 8.23 The three functions of supervision.
Kadushin (1976)Proctor (1986)What it coversExample
AdministrativeNormativeStandards, ethics, agency rules, protecting clients, accountabilityChecking a risk assessment, case notes and consent
EducationalFormativeKnowledge, skills, understanding and professional growthTeaching formulation; reviewing a recording together
SupportiveRestorativeHelping the supervisee cope with the emotional demands of the workDebriefing after a client's suicide attempt

Supervision happens in several formats: individually, in groups, in peer groups without a designated senior, and in triads. It draws on several kinds of information about the trainee's sessions: her own account (self-report), process notes written soon after the session, audio or video recordings, and direct observation. Self-report is the most common and the least reliable, because trainees cannot report what they did not notice. Like therapy, supervision depends on its relationship. In 1983 Edward Bordin applied his model of the working alliance (Section 8.5) to supervision: the supervisory working alliance consists of agreement on the goals of supervision, agreement on its tasks, and an emotional bond of trust between supervisor and supervisee. Trainees who distrust their supervisors hide their mistakes, which is exactly what supervision exists to prevent.

Indian writers have been frank about the gaps. Manickam's 2010 review (Section 8.11) noted that young therapists in India are often given too little supervision, that the multilingual mix of patients, trainees and supervisors makes supervision harder, and that audio recording of sessions, though professionals react positively to it, is not widely used in training and is not mandatory. In a study at a general hospital psychiatry unit, Tharyan (2000) showed that group supervision of psychotherapy was feasible and acceptable to trainees, though not a substitute for individual supervision.

Exam trap

Supervision is evaluative, hierarchical and ongoing, with three purposes: developing the supervisee, protecting clients and gatekeeping the profession (Bernard and Goodyear). Consultation is between peers, voluntary and non-evaluative, and the consultee keeps responsibility. Kadushin's functions are administrative, educational, supportive; Proctor's are normative, formative, restorative. Supervision is not personal therapy for the trainee.

Models of supervision

Bernard and Goodyear sort the many models of supervision into three families. Psychotherapy-based models supervise in the language of a particular therapy. Developmental models describe how trainees change over time and what they need at each point. Process models (also called social role models) describe what the supervisor does in a session, whatever the therapy being supervised.

Psychotherapy-based models. The oldest supervision is psychoanalytic, and its classic text is The Teaching and Learning of Psychotherapy (1958) by Rudolf Ekstein and Robert S. Wallerstein. They pictured the training situation as a "clinical rhombus" of four people (patient, therapist, supervisor and the administrator of the clinic) and distinguished learning problems, difficulties in the trainee's work with patients that arise from her own blind spots, from problems about learning, difficulties that show up in the relationship with the supervisor. Psychodynamic supervisors pay close attention to transference and countertransference (Section 3.3) in both relationships. Person-centred supervisors offer the trainee the same core conditions she is meant to offer clients (Section 6.2), trusting her to grow in a climate of empathy and acceptance. Cognitive-behavioural supervision is structured like a CBT session, with an agenda, a review of homework, a focus on specific skills, role-play and rehearsal, and rating of competence on instruments such as the Cognitive Therapy Scale (Jeffrey Young and Aaron Beck, 1980). The strength of these models is consistency between what is taught and how it is taught; the weakness is that they say little about how supervision should change as the trainee develops, or what to do when supervisor and supervisee come from different schools.

Developmental models. These answer the question the first family leaves open. An early version, by Carol Loganbill, Emily Hardy and Ursula Delworth (1982), described trainees moving through stagnation, confusion and integration. The best-known and best-researched is the integrated developmental model (IDM) of Cal D. Stoltenberg, which grew from his counsellor complexity model of 1981 and was set out with Delworth in Supervising Counselors and Therapists: A Developmental Approach (1987) and later with Brian McNeill (1998). The IDM tracks three overriding structures in the trainee: self and other awareness (how far she is focused on herself or on the client, both in thought and in feeling), motivation, and autonomy (how dependent she is on the supervisor). These are assessed in eight domains of practice: intervention skills, assessment techniques, interpersonal assessment, client conceptualisation, individual differences, theoretical orientation, treatment plans and goals, and professional ethics. A trainee moves through three levels, and a fourth integrated level, as Table 8.24 and Figure 8.15 show.

Table 8.24 The levels of Stoltenberg's integrated developmental model.
LevelSelf and other awarenessMotivationAutonomyWhat the supervisor provides
Level 1 (beginner)Focused on herself and her performance; anxious about evaluationHigh, but anxious; wants the "right" techniqueDependent on the supervisorStructure, instruction, modelling, encouragement, gentle challenge
Level 2 (trial and tribulation)Focus shifts to the client; may over-identify and lose objectivityFluctuates between confidence and confusionConflict between dependence and autonomyLess structure, more autonomy, help with countertransference, challenge
Level 3 (challenge and growth)Balances empathy for the client with awareness of her own reactionsStable and consistentConditional autonomy; seeks consultation when neededCollegial, consultative; the supervisee sets the agenda
Level 3i (integrated)Level 3 functioning across all domains; a personal therapeutic styleStableAutonomous across domainsPeer consultation

Two features of the IDM are worth noticing. First, development is domain-specific: a trainee who interviews confidently may still be a beginner at formulating a case or at working with a family. Second, Level 2 is the turbulent one, often compared to adolescence. Having discovered the client, the trainee can be swept into the client's feelings, may resent supervision as interference and may swing between "I am useless" and "I know what I am doing". A supervisor who keeps treating a Level 2 trainee like a beginner provokes rebellion; one who withdraws too soon leaves her to flounder. Research supports the broad idea that trainees' needs change with experience, but the evidence for distinct, invariant stages is weaker.

Process models. The best known is the discrimination model of Janine Bernard (1979), so called because the supervisor must discriminate, moment by moment, which role to take and what to focus on. Bernard described three roles. As teacher, the supervisor instructs, demonstrates, models and evaluates. As counsellor, she helps the supervisee explore her own thoughts and feelings about the work, without becoming her therapist. As consultant, she acts as a resource for a supervisee who is trusted to lead, offering ideas and alternatives. She also described three foci. Intervention skills (in Bernard's original wording, process skills) are what the trainee observably does in the session: her questions, reflections and techniques. Conceptualisation skills are how she understands the client: seeing themes, making sense of what the client says and choosing goals. Personalisation skills are how the trainee's own person enters the work: her style, culture, feelings and countertransference, and her comfort with the client's emotions and with being challenged. Three roles by three foci gives nine possible choices, set out in Table 8.25. Later writers added professional behaviour (punctuality, record keeping, ethics) as a fourth focus.

Table 8.25 Bernard's discrimination model (1979): three roles by three foci, with an example of what the supervisor might do in each cell.
FocusTeacherCounsellorConsultant
InterventionDemonstrates how to ask about suicidal thoughts, then role-plays itExplores what made the trainee freeze when the client began to cryLists with the trainee the techniques she might try next session
ConceptualisationExplains how to link the client's history and symptoms in a formulationExplores why the trainee keeps describing the client as "manipulative"Discusses two formulations the trainee has drafted and lets her choose
PersonalisationTeaches about countertransference and cultural biasHelps her notice her anger at a client who reminds her of her fatherAsks how she would like to handle a client's personal questions

A worked example shows the model in motion. Divya, a first-year trainee at a hospital in Mysuru, brings a recording of her third session with a 40-year-old man with alcohol dependence. In the first ten minutes of supervision, her supervisor acts as teacher with an intervention focus, showing her how to respond to his "I can stop any time" with a reflection rather than an argument (Section 6.9). Then Divya admits that she cannot stand him; he talks down to her. The supervisor shifts to counsellor with a personalisation focus, helping her see that he sounds like a dismissive uncle, without turning the hour into therapy about her family. Finally, as consultant with a conceptualisation focus, she asks Divya how she now understands his contempt, and Divya suggests it may protect him from shame. The skill being modelled is the discrimination itself: choosing the role and focus the moment needs, rather than defaulting to the one the supervisor prefers.

A second process model, from Britain, is the seven-eyed model of Peter Hawkins and Robin Shohet (first set out in Supervision in the Helping Professions, 1989). It names seven things a supervisor can attend to: the client; the supervisee's interventions; the relationship between client and supervisee; the supervisee herself; the supervisory relationship (where parallel process appears, below); the supervisor's own reactions; and the wider context of organisation, profession and culture.

Exam trap

Bernard's discrimination model (1979) has three roles (teacher, counsellor, consultant) and three foci (intervention or process, conceptualisation, personalisation): nine cells. It is a process model, not a developmental one. The integrated developmental model is Stoltenberg's (with Delworth, 1987): three structures (self and other awareness, motivation, autonomy), eight domains, Levels 1, 2, 3 and 3i. Loganbill, Hardy and Delworth (1982) gave stagnation, confusion, integration. Ekstein and Wallerstein (1958) are the psychoanalytic classics. The seven-eyed model is Hawkins and Shohet's.

Parallel process and live supervision

Supervisors have long noticed something uncanny. A trainee whose client keeps saying "I don't know, you tell me what to do" arrives at supervision and, without realising it, keeps saying to the supervisor, "I don't know what to do with him; just tell me." The supervisor then feels the same pull to rescue that the trainee felt in the session. The pattern of one relationship has been re-enacted in the other. This is parallel process. It was first described in 1955 by the American psychoanalyst Harold F. Searles, who called it the reflection process, and was developed by Ekstein and Wallerstein in 1958; the name parallel process has been standard ever since. Psychodynamic writers explain it through identification: the trainee unconsciously takes on the client's way of relating and plays it out with the supervisor, often because it is the part of the case she has not yet understood. The process also runs the other way. A supervisor who is harsh and critical may find the trainee becoming harsh and critical with her clients; a supervisor who listens patiently teaches patience by example. Figure 8.16 shows both directions.

The usefulness of the idea is practical. When a supervisor notices that she feels with the trainee what the trainee seems to feel with the client, she can say so, explore what it reveals about the case, and model a different response, for example helping the trainee think rather than giving her an answer, which the trainee can then offer the client. The evidence for parallel process consists mostly of case reports and a small number of studies, so it is best treated as a useful hypothesis about a particular supervision rather than a law that always operates.

Most supervision relies on what the trainee remembers. Live supervision removes that filter: the supervisor observes the session as it happens and can intervene during it. It grew up in family therapy (Chapter 7), and the family therapist Braulio Montalvo, a colleague of Salvador Minuchin, described its methods in a 1973 paper. The supervisor or team sits behind a one-way mirror and communicates by telephoning into the room, through a small earphone worn by the therapist (the "bug in the ear"), by knocking and walking in, or by calling the therapist out for a consultation break, as the Milan team did. In the reflecting team devised by the Norwegian psychiatrist Tom Andersen (1987), the observers change places with the family and discuss their impressions openly while the family listens. Live supervision protects clients and gives the trainee help at the moment it is needed; its costs are time, staff and the risk that the trainee comes to depend on the voice from behind the mirror.

Between self-report and live observation lie recordings. A particularly well-developed method for using them is interpersonal process recall (IPR), developed by Norman Kagan at Michigan State University from the 1960s. Trainee and supervisor (in Kagan's term, the "inquirer") replay a recorded session; the trainee stops the recording whenever she remembers a thought or feeling she had at that moment but did not express, and the inquirer asks open, non-judgemental questions ("What were you feeling just then? What did you want to say?"). The method makes the trainee, not the supervisor, the expert on her own experience, and it is especially good at bringing personalisation issues to light.

Exam trap

Parallel process is the re-enactment in supervision of a pattern from the therapy (or the reverse); Searles (1955) called it the reflection process, and Ekstein and Wallerstein (1958) developed it. It is not countertransference itself (the therapist's reaction to the client, Section 3.3), though countertransference is often how it shows up. Live supervision (one-way mirror, phone-in, bug in the ear, consultation break) comes from family therapy; Montalvo (1973) described it; the reflecting team is Tom Andersen's. Interpersonal process recall is Kagan's.

The wounded healer

Why do people become therapists? One old answer is that many of them have suffered first. Carl Gustav Jung (1875 to 1961), as Section 3.8 described, called the analyst a wounded healer, and drew the image from Greek myth. The centaur Chiron, the wise teacher of medicine who raised the healer-god Asclepius, was wounded by one of Heracles' poisoned arrows; being immortal he could not die, and his wound never healed, yet he remained the great teacher of the healing arts. For Jung the doctor is effective only when he himself is affected by the patient; "only the wounded physician heals", and an analyst who hides behind his professional role like a suit of armour has no effect. It was this conviction that led Jung's Zurich school to insist that every analyst must first be analysed (the training analysis of Section 3.2). The phrase reached a wider audience through the Dutch priest Henri Nouwen's book The Wounded Healer (1972), written for clergy, which argued that a helper's own acknowledged suffering can become a source of compassion for others.

How wounded are therapists in fact? In 1994 Kenneth S. Pope and Barbara Tabachnick mailed a survey to 800 American psychologists, and nearly 60 percent replied. About 84 percent had been in therapy themselves. Sixty-one percent reported at least one episode of clinical depression, 29 percent had felt suicidal, and almost 4 percent had made at least one suicide attempt. Norcross's review of 25 years of research on the psychotherapist's own psychotherapy (2005) drew the same broad picture: most mental health professionals have had personal therapy, most found it helpful, and many say it taught them lasting lessons about what it is like to be a client, such as the importance of warmth, of being heard and of the therapist's patience.

The wound cuts both ways. A review by Zerubavel and Wright (2012) listed the benefits a therapist's own struggles can bring: deeper empathy, the ability to offer realistic hope, humility, and a feel for the client's side of the room. It also listed the dangers: a recovery that is not yet stable, difficulty managing countertransference (for example over-identifying with clients whose stories resemble one's own), greater vulnerability to compassion fatigue (below), and in the worst case impairment. Because the profession rarely talks about its own wounds, the authors argued, therapists who struggle tend to hide it and seek help too late. The point of the wounded healer is therefore not that a wound is an asset in itself, but that a wound which has been acknowledged and is being healed can become one.

Should therapists be required to have therapy themselves? Psychoanalytic training has required it for about a century, and some counselling bodies require a set number of hours of personal therapy during training. The case for it is strong on grounds of self-knowledge, of experiencing the client's role and of the therapist's own well-being. The evidence that a therapist's personal therapy directly improves her clients' outcomes, however, is surprisingly thin and inconsistent, so most training programmes in clinical psychology recommend it rather than require it. Whether and how a therapist should mention her own experiences to a client is a separate question, the question of self-disclosure (Section 8.10).

Exam trap

The wounded healer is Jung's archetype (from the myths of Chiron and Asclepius), not Freud's, Rogers' or Adler's; Henri Nouwen's book of that title (1972) popularised it for clergy. Pope and Tabachnick (1994): about 84 percent of psychologists had been in therapy and 61 percent reported clinical depression. The idea is not that unhealed wounds help, but that acknowledged, healing ones can.

When helping hurts the helper: burnout, compassion fatigue and vicarious traumatisation

In the early 1970s Herbert J. Freudenberger (1926 to 1999), a psychoanalyst in private practice in New York, spent his evenings volunteering at a free clinic for young drug users in the East Village. After months of full days at his practice and long nights at the clinic, he noticed that he and his fellow volunteers, idealistic people who had come to help, were becoming exhausted, irritable, cynical and detached from the very clients they had come to serve. He borrowed a word already used on the street for the burnt-out state of chronic drug users and, in a 1974 paper in the Journal of Social Issues titled "Staff burn-out", described the condition in helpers. Burnout is a state of physical and emotional exhaustion, with negative attitudes towards one's work and the people one serves, that develops from prolonged, unrelieved stress at work.

Freudenberger named burnout; Christina Maslach (born 1946) measured it. A social psychologist at the University of California, Berkeley, she had been interviewing nurses, social workers, lawyers and other human service workers about how they coped with emotionally demanding work, and found them describing the same pattern. With Susan E. Jackson she published the Maslach Burnout Inventory (MBI) in 1981. The original Human Services Survey has 22 items, each rated for how often the feeling occurs, from 0 (never) to 6 (every day), and it measures three dimensions.

  • Emotional exhaustion: feeling emotionally drained and used up by one's work, with nothing left to give (nine items). It is the central, most widely reported dimension.
  • Depersonalisation: a detached, callous and impersonal response to the people one serves, treating clients as objects or "cases" rather than as people (five items). It is the helper's way of protecting herself from further emotional demands.
  • Reduced personal accomplishment: a decline in one's feelings of competence and achievement at work, a sense that one is no longer making a difference (eight items, scored so that low accomplishment indicates burnout).

The three subscales are scored separately, and burnout is indicated by high emotional exhaustion, high depersonalisation and low personal accomplishment. Later versions extended the inventory to teachers (the Educators Survey) and to all occupations (the General Survey, whose dimensions are named exhaustion, cynicism and professional efficacy). With Michael Leiter, in The Truth About Burnout (1997), Maslach argued that burnout is above all a problem of the job rather than of the person: it arises from a mismatch between the worker and six areas of working life, namely workload, control, reward, community, fairness and values. Its positive opposite is engagement: energy, involvement and a sense of efficacy. Jerry Edelwich and Archie Brodsky (1980) described the slide into burnout in four stages: enthusiasm, stagnation, frustration and apathy.

Is burnout a mental disorder? The World Health Organization's ICD-11 answers no. It lists burn-out (code QD85) as an occupational phenomenon in the chapter on factors influencing health status or contact with health services, defines it by three dimensions that echo Maslach's (energy depletion or exhaustion; increased mental distance from one's job, or negativism and cynicism about it; and reduced professional efficacy), and restricts it to the occupational context. That last point distinguishes it from depression, which spreads to every area of life; the disorder itself belongs to the Psychiatric Disorders book.

Therapists face the general hazards of any helping job, high caseloads, little control, paperwork and isolation, and some special ones: clients who are suicidal, hostile or not improving, the one-way intimacy of the work, and the need to hold strong feelings without discharging them. A second set of hazards comes specifically from working with trauma. In 1992 the nurse Carla Joinson used the term compassion fatigue for a loss of the ability to nurture that she observed in nurses, and in 1995 the traumatologist Charles R. Figley developed it in Compassion Fatigue: Coping with Secondary Traumatic Stress Disorder in Those Who Treat the Traumatized. Figley equated compassion fatigue with secondary traumatic stress: the stress that results from helping, or wanting to help, a traumatised person, with symptoms that mirror post-traumatic stress disorder (intrusive images of the client's trauma, avoidance of reminders, numbing and hyperarousal) and that can come on suddenly. It is often described as the cost of caring. Its counterpart is compassion satisfaction, the pleasure and meaning that come from helping well, and the Professional Quality of Life Scale (ProQOL) developed by Beth Hudnall Stamm measures compassion satisfaction, burnout and secondary traumatic stress together.

A third concept came from therapists who worked with survivors of trauma. In 1990 I. Lisa McCann and Laurie Anne Pearlman published "Vicarious traumatization: a framework for understanding the psychological effects of working with victims" in the Journal of Traumatic Stress. Vicarious traumatisation is a cumulative transformation of the therapist's inner experience that results from empathic engagement with clients' traumatic material. Drawing on their constructivist self-development theory, McCann and Pearlman argued that hearing story after story of cruelty gradually alters the therapist's own beliefs, or schemas, about the world and other people, especially in the areas of safety, trust, esteem, intimacy and control. A therapist may begin to see danger everywhere, distrust ordinary kindness, or feel her own family is never safe. Unlike compassion fatigue, which can strike after a single harrowing case, vicarious traumatisation builds up over many clients and is, to some degree, an expected consequence of the work.

Pearlman and Karen Saakvitne (1995) also drew a distinction that examiners like. Countertransference (Section 3.3) is a reaction to a particular client, shaped in part by the therapist's own history, and it arises within one relationship; vicarious traumatisation accumulates across all the therapist's trauma work, whoever the client. The two interact: a therapist whose own history resembles her clients' is more vulnerable to both. Writers on therapist stress sometimes speak loosely of "countertransference fatigue" for the wearing effect of strong reactions to particular clients that are never noticed or worked through; the phrase is descriptive rather than a formal construct with its own measure, and the cure is the same as for countertransference generally: noticing, understanding and supervision. Table 8.26 brings the four concepts together.

Table 8.26 Four ways the work can affect the therapist.
ConceptNamed byWhat it isWho is at riskOnset
BurnoutFreudenberger (1974); measured by Maslach and Jackson (1981)Exhaustion, depersonalisation and reduced accomplishment from chronic work stressAny helper, indeed any workerGradual
Compassion fatigue (secondary traumatic stress)Joinson (1992); Figley (1995)PTSD-like symptoms from helping traumatised peopleTrauma therapists, nurses, emergency workers, carersCan be sudden
Vicarious traumatisationMcCann and Pearlman (1990)Lasting change in the helper's beliefs about safety, trust, esteem, intimacy and controlTherapists of trauma survivorsCumulative
CountertransferenceFreud (1910); Section 3.3Reaction to a particular client, partly from the therapist's own historyEvery therapistWithin one relationship
Exam trap

Freudenberger coined burnout (1974); Maslach (with Jackson, 1981) built the MBI, whose three dimensions are emotional exhaustion, depersonalisation and reduced personal accomplishment. Depersonalisation in the MBI means a cynical, impersonal attitude to clients, not the dissociative symptom of feeling detached from oneself. ICD-11 calls burn-out an occupational phenomenon (QD85), not a medical condition. Compassion fatigue is Figley's (term first used by Joinson, 1992); vicarious traumatisation is McCann and Pearlman's (1990). Burnout can affect anyone in any job; vicarious traumatisation is specific to trauma work.

Self-care and prevention

If the work can harm the worker, looking after oneself is not a luxury but part of professional competence. The APA Ethics Code makes the point directly: under its standard on personal problems and conflicts (2.06), psychologists refrain from starting work when they know or should know that their personal problems are likely to prevent them from performing competently, and when they become aware of such problems they take appropriate measures, such as obtaining consultation or assistance and deciding whether to limit, suspend or end their work (Chapter 1 covers the codes, including the RCI's). Writers on the topic distinguish distress, which every therapist feels at times, from impairment, in which distress has begun to reduce the quality of care. Self-care is the set of deliberate practices by which a therapist keeps distress from becoming impairment.

The best-known practical guide is Leaving It at the Office: A Guide to Psychotherapist Self-Care by Norcross and James D. Guy (2007; a second edition with Gary VandenBos appeared in 2018). Its recommendations sound simple and are hard to keep: value the person of the therapist as much as the technique; refocus on the rewards of the work; recognise its hazards; attend to the body through sleep, exercise and food; nurture relationships outside work; set boundaries on hours, availability and caseload; restructure unhelpful beliefs such as "I must save every client"; keep healthy escapes; seek personal therapy when needed; and cultivate a sense of meaning in the work. Many Indian therapists add the practices of their own tradition (yoga, meditation and prayer) and the support of family, which can protect against the isolation that private practice especially brings.

Because burnout is largely a problem of the job, prevention also has to happen at the level of the organisation. Reasonable caseloads, a mix of trauma and non-trauma work for those who see survivors, regular supervision whose restorative function is taken seriously, peer consultation groups, and a culture in which admitting strain is safe all reduce risk. Group discussion of difficult cases has a long history in medicine too: from the 1950s the Hungarian-born psychoanalyst Michael Balint ran groups in London in which general practitioners discussed their relationships with difficult patients, and Balint groups still meet today. Trauma-informed organisations (Section 9.7) build staff care into their design. Measures such as the MBI and the ProQOL can be used to monitor staff, though they are screening tools, not diagnoses. In Indian public hospitals, where a clinical psychologist may see dozens of patients a day and trainees work long hours, the organisational side of prevention is often the weakest, and the stigma of a mental health professional seeking help for herself remains a barrier.

Clinical link

Ananya, a 28-year-old psychologist at a one stop centre for women affected by violence in Bengaluru, notices over six months that she dreads Mondays, sleeps badly, has stopped seeing friends and has begun to think of her clients as "the cases". She checks the locks at night several times, though nothing has happened to her. In supervision she and her supervisor sort out the strands: exhaustion and depersonalisation (burnout), intrusive images from one client's account of an assault (compassion fatigue), and a new conviction that no man can be trusted (vicarious traumatisation). The plan works at both levels: a capped caseload with fewer acute trauma referrals, fortnightly restorative supervision, a return to her evening yoga class and her friends, and a few sessions of personal therapy. Six months later she still finds the work hard, and she is still doing it well.

Exam trap

Self-care is an ethical obligation tied to competence (APA standard 2.06 on personal problems and conflicts), not an optional extra. Distress is normal; impairment is distress that reduces the quality of care. Leaving It at the Office (2007) is Norcross and Guy's. Burnout is prevented at two levels, the individual and the organisation, and Maslach's research says the organisation matters most.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  3. Keeping one home theory and importing techniques that take on its meaning is Stanley Messer's integration.
  4. Resnicow's distinction is between structure adaptations (language, names, examples) and deep structure adaptations (beliefs, family roles, religion).
  5. In Benish, Quintana and Wampold's 2011 meta-analysis, the only adaptation that predicted the advantage of culturally adapted therapy was adapting the illness .
  6. The guru-chela relationship was proposed as a therapeutic paradigm by in 1973.
  7. Kadushin's three functions of supervision are administrative, educational and .
  8. Bernard's discrimination model has three roles (teacher, counsellor and ) and three foci (intervention, conceptualisation and personalisation).
  9. When a pattern from therapy is re-enacted between trainee and supervisor, it is called process; Searles called it the reflection process.
  10. The three dimensions of the Maslach Burnout Inventory are emotional exhaustion, and reduced personal accomplishment.

Answers: (1) technical eclecticism, (2) cyclical psychodynamics, (3) assimilative, (4) surface, (5) myth (the explanation of the illness and its cure), (6) J. S. Neki, (7) supportive, (8) consultant, (9) parallel, (10) depersonalisation.

The case of Eysenck's two-thirds, revisited

Eysenck asked a fair question in 1952, and you can now see why his answer did not survive. His two-thirds came from comparing groups that were not alike: insurance claimants with jobs to return to against patients who had sought psychoanalysis, "untreated" patients who were in fact seeing their family doctors, and dropouts from analysis counted as failures (Section 8.1). Better baselines put spontaneous remission nearer 30 percent (Bergin) or at a median of 43 percent (Lambert), and once Gene Glass had invented meta-analysis the whole literature could be weighed at once: the typical treated client was better off than 75 percent of untreated people in Smith and Glass's analysis (1977) and than about 80 percent in Smith, Glass and Miller's (1980). Even Eysenck's own figures, run through the dose-effect method by McNeilly and Howard (1991), showed psychotherapy producing in about 15 sessions the improvement that spontaneous remission took two years to bring (Section 8.9). Therapy, on that reading, changes less where people end up than how quickly they get there, and for someone who is suffering, speed matters.

Now go back to Ravi, the engineering student in Mysuru whose panic attacks eased after ten sessions. Eysenck would have asked whether he would have got better anyway, and part of the answer is yes: the end of his examinations, his family's support and the fact that he came for help at his worst all belong to the 40 percent slice that Lambert credited to client and extratherapeutic factors (Section 8.4). But the chapter also tells you what else was at work, and how to check it. For panic disorder, cognitive therapy with interoceptive exposure beats credible alternatives (Section 8.3); it works better through a strong alliance of agreed goals, agreed tasks and a bond (Section 8.5), and in the hands of a psychologist who handles difficult moments well (Section 8.6); and whether Ravi has really recovered, rather than simply saying a grateful goodbye, is a question for the reliable change index and a cut-off score (Section 8.9). The answer to Eysenck, in other words, is not that time does nothing. It is that a good therapist, using a well-chosen treatment and checking progress session by session, gives a person like Ravi better odds, sooner, than waiting would.

Chapter summary

8.1 Eysenck's Challenge: Does Psychotherapy Work at All? Outcome research asks whether clients improve; process research asks what happens in sessions. Improvement after therapy is not proof, because spontaneous remission, regression to the mean, life events, support and expectancy produce change, and Strupp and Hadley's tripartite model (1977) adds that society, the client and the professional judge outcome differently. Hans Eysenck's 1952 review set about 44 percent improvement with psychoanalysis and 64 percent with eclectic therapy against Denker's and Landis's untreated baselines, and concluded that about two-thirds of neurotic patients improve within two years with or without psychotherapy. Rosenzweig and Luborsky (1954) replied first; critics showed the groups were not comparable, the criteria differed, the "untreated" were treated and dropouts counted as failures. Bergin (1971) put remission near 30 percent and Lambert (1976) at a median of 43. Kiesler's uniformity myths and Paul's 1967 question set the new agenda.

8.2 Meta-Analysis: Measuring How Much Therapy Helps. Box-score reviews counted wins and ties, ignoring effect size and precision. Gene Glass coined meta-analysis in 1976 for the statistical combination of many studies, using the effect size: the difference between treated and control means divided by the standard deviation, judged against Jacob Cohen's benchmarks of 0.2, 0.5 and 0.8. Smith and Glass (1977), with 375 studies, found an average effect size of 0.68, the typical client better off than 75 percent of untreated people; Smith, Glass and Miller (1980), with 475 studies, found 0.85 (80 percent) and little difference between kinds of therapy. Eysenck called it "an exercise in mega-silliness". The criticisms are garbage in, garbage out, apples and oranges, Rosenthal's file-drawer problem and non-independence, and re-analyses confirmed the effect. Lambert's summaries put the average effect near 0.8, with most gains maintained and a minority getting worse.

8.3 The Dodo Bird Verdict and the Specific-Effects Debate. Saul Rosenzweig (1936) took the Dodo's "Everybody has won, and all must have prizes" from Lewis Carroll to argue that therapies work through implicit common factors rather than specific techniques. Luborsky, Singer and Luborsky (1975) found a tie score and popularised the label dodo bird verdict. Wampold and colleagues (1997), using 277 direct comparisons of bona fide therapies, put the upper bound of their difference at about 0.2, against 0.82 for therapy versus no treatment. Specific-effects researchers (Rachman and Wilson) point to ERP for obsessive-compulsive disorder (Lindsay and colleagues; Foa and colleagues), Clark's cognitive therapy for panic, behavioural methods for children and Tolin's CBT meta-analysis. Critics (Chambless, Beutler) cite averaging across disorders, similar treatments, diluted outcomes, low power and allegiance, while dismantling studies find little. The verdict is roughly right for many common problems and wrong for some.

8.4 Common Factors: Frank, Lambert's Pie and Wampold's Contextual Model. Jerome Frank's Persuasion and Healing (1961) compared psychotherapy with religious healing, thought reform and placebo: clients come demoralised, and every therapy remoralises them through four shared features, an emotionally charged confiding relationship, a healing setting, a rationale or myth, and a ritual or procedure. Michael Lambert (1992) estimated that client and extratherapeutic factors account for about 40 percent of improvement, relationship factors 30, expectancy 15 and techniques 15, rough judgements rather than measured results. Beecher's "The powerful placebo" (1955), Kirsch's response expectancy theory and Kaptchuk's sham acupuncture trial show how much expectation and the relationship carry; Howard's phase model runs remoralisation, remediation, rehabilitation. Bruce Wampold set a medical model of therapy against a contextual model in which an initial relationship works through the real relationship, expectations and healthy actions; the evidence favours the contextual model on most counts.

8.5 The Therapeutic Alliance and the Core Conditions. The alliance grew from Freud's friendly transference, Zetzel's therapeutic alliance (1956), Greenson's working alliance (1965) and Luborsky's helping alliance (1976). Edward Bordin (1979) made it pantheoretical: agreement on goals, agreement on tasks and a bond. Horvath and Greenberg's Working Alliance Inventory (1989) has 36 items in three subscales, with 12-item short forms. Meta-analyses from Horvath and Symonds (1991) to Flückiger and colleagues (2018, 295 studies) put the alliance-outcome correlation at about .28, similar across therapies but correlational. Safran and Muran described withdrawal and confrontation ruptures, repaired through metacommunication. Truax and Carkhuff's rating scales and the Barrett-Lennard Relationship Inventory tested Rogers' core conditions, which the 2018 meta-analyses found facilitative (empathy r = .28) but not shown to be necessary or sufficient. Gelso's real relationship differs from the alliance, and Norcross's Division 29 task force and its successors listed evidence-based relationship elements.

8.6 Therapist Effects, Allegiance Effects and Client Variables. Ricks's "supershrink" (1974) showed that therapists differ, and multilevel studies put therapist effects at about 5 percent of outcome variance (Baldwin and Imel, 2013), more than the differences between treatments. Experience, training and orientation predict little; facilitative interpersonal skills and the ability to form alliances predict more, and deliberate practice may help. The researcher allegiance effect (Luborsky and colleagues, 1999) explained 69 percent of the variance in outcome differences. Prognostic variables predict outcome in any treatment and prescriptive variables, such as reactance, predict which treatment suits whom. Schofield's YAVIS (1964) describes the clients therapists prefer, not those who benefit most; readiness, expectations, self-efficacy, severity, attachment and preferences matter more. Hathaway's hello-goodbye effect (1948), regression to the mean, Orne's demand characteristics and placebo responding inflate pre-post change. In India the family, the route to help and stigma shape client factors.

8.7 Testing a Treatment: Efficacy, Effectiveness and the Randomised Controlled Trial. Efficacy asks whether a treatment can work under ideal conditions and prizes internal validity; effectiveness asks whether it works in ordinary practice and prizes external validity, as in Seligman's Consumer Reports survey (1995). Analogue research uses non-clinical participants, as in Gordon Paul's 1966 experiment; case studies and single-case designs come earlier. The randomised controlled trial, which dates from Bradford Hill's streptomycin trial of 1948, needs registration, inclusion and exclusion criteria, randomisation, allocation concealment, blind assessment, intention-to-treat analysis and enough power, and reports effect sizes, risk ratios and numbers needed to treat. The control condition (no treatment, waiting list, attention-placebo, treatment as usual, an active treatment, dismantling or additive designs) decides what is ruled out. Manuals, adherence and competence define fidelity, CONSORT governs reporting, and practical trials, benchmarking and practice-based evidence carry trials into clinics.

8.8 Landmark Trials, Empirically Supported Treatments and Evidence-Based Practice. The NIMH Treatment of Depression Collaborative Research Program (Elkin and colleagues, 1989) assigned 250 outpatients to CBT, IPT, imipramine or placebo, each pill with clinical management, for 16 weeks: the psychotherapies roughly equalled each other and imipramine, differences appeared only among the more severely depressed, and few stayed well over 18 months. Project MATCH (1997) tested the matching hypothesis in 1,726 clients with alcohol problems and found few matches; four sessions of motivational enhancement therapy did about as well as twelve of the other treatments. The Division 12 Task Force chaired by Chambless (1995, 1998) defined well-established and probably efficacious treatments, criticised by Seligman, Wampold, Westen and Garfield. Evidence-based practice in psychology (APA, 2006), built on Sackett's evidence-based medicine, integrates the best available research with clinical expertise in the context of patient characteristics, culture and preferences.

8.9 How Much Therapy, How Much Change: Dose, Clinical Significance and Deterioration. Howard, Kopta, Krause and Orlinsky (1986) applied probit analysis to 2,431 patients and found a negatively accelerated dose-effect curve: about half improved by 8 sessions and three-quarters by 26. Howard's phase model runs remoralisation, remediation, rehabilitation, and Barkham's good-enough level model explains part of the curve by clients leaving once improved enough. Clinical significance asks whether change matters in a client's life, not whether it is statistically significant. Jacobson and Truax (1991) divide the change score by the standard error of difference: a reliable change index beyond 1.96 is reliable, and cut-offs a, b and c classify clients as recovered, improved, unchanged or deteriorated; Kendall's normative comparisons assess groups. Bergin's deterioration effect touches about 5 to 10 percent, Lilienfeld (2007) listed potentially harmful therapies, McCord's Cambridge-Somerville follow-up showed harm, and Lambert's OQ-45 feedback reduces deterioration.

8.10 Inside the Therapy Room: Process Research, the Course of Therapy and Its Ending. Process research studies what happens in sessions and process-outcome research links it to change. Rogers's recordings made the field possible; Orlinsky and Howard's generic model (1987) runs from the therapeutic contract to temporal patterns; Stiles warned that responsiveness defeats the drug metaphor; Tang and DeRubeis (1999) described sudden gains. Measures include Stiles's Session Evaluation Questionnaire, response category systems and task analysis. Therapy has a beginning (assessment, contract, role induction, alliance, hope), a middle and an end. Termination is a planned phase, which Quintana (1993) recast as transformation rather than loss. Dropout averaged about 47 percent in Wierzbicki and Pekarik (1993) and about 20 percent in Swift and Greenberg (2012). Self-disclosure divides the schools, from Freud's mirror to Jourard's transparent self; homework improves outcome (Kazantzis and colleagues, 2000); frequency, session length and SOAP notes complete it.

8.11 Combining and Adapting Therapies: Integration, Culture and Therapy in India. Eclectic or integrative is the commonest orientation among clinical psychologists, and Dollard and Miller (1950) were an early landmark. Integration has four routes: technical eclecticism (Lazarus, 1967; multimodal therapy; Beutler's systematic treatment selection), theoretical integration (Wachtel's cyclical psychodynamics; Prochaska and DiClemente's transtheoretical approach), common factors integration (Frank, Goldfried) and assimilative integration (Messer, 1992). Wrenn warned against the culturally encapsulated counsellor, and Pike's emic and etic name the problem. Cultural adaptation (Bernal's eight dimensions; Resnicow's surface and deep structure) helps modestly, most when the illness explanation is adapted. Derald Wing Sue's awareness, knowledge and skills, Sue and Zane's credibility and gift-giving, and cultural humility describe the competent therapist. In India, Surya and Jayaram (1964) described the dependent, support-seeking patient, Neki proposed the guru-chela paradigm (1973) and sahaja, Varma listed obstacles, Vahia built yoga-based therapy and Wig described the Hanuman complex.

8.12 Becoming a Therapist: Training, Supervision, the Wounded Healer and Self-Care. Boulder (1949) set the scientist-practitioner model and Vail (1973) the practitioner-scholar model; experience alone does not improve outcomes. Bernard and Goodyear define clinical supervision as evaluative, hierarchical and ongoing, with gatekeeping among its purposes; Kadushin's administrative, educational and supportive functions match Proctor's normative, formative and restorative ones. Models are psychotherapy-based (Ekstein and Wallerstein), developmental (Stoltenberg's integrated developmental model, Levels 1 to 3i) or process models (Bernard's discrimination model of three roles by three foci). Parallel process (Searles), live supervision (Montalvo) and Kagan's interpersonal process recall add tools. Jung's wounded healer draws on Chiron, and Pope and Tabachnick found that most psychologists had had therapy. Freudenberger named burnout and Maslach measured it (emotional exhaustion, depersonalisation, reduced accomplishment); Figley's compassion fatigue and McCann and Pearlman's vicarious traumatisation complete the hazards, and self-care is an ethical duty.

From the past papers

  1. According to the "Dodo Bird Verdict," what is the primary factor responsible for equivalent outcomes across different therapeutic modalities? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) The specific techniques used by the therapist (b) Common factors such as the therapeutic alliance and empathy (c) The theoretical orientation of the clinician (d) The specific diagnosis of the patient

    Answer: (b) Common factors such as the therapeutic alliance and empathy. Saul Rosenzweig (1936) borrowed the Dodo's "Everybody has won, and all must have prizes" to argue that different therapies succeed through what they share, and Luborsky, Singer and Luborsky (1975) credited their tie score to the shared components, "especially the helping relationship with a therapist". The specific technique, the clinician's theoretical orientation and the patient's diagnosis are exactly what the verdict says matter least.

  2. ERP is a form of therapy used for: (RML 2024)

    (a) Personality disorders (b) Depression (c) Schizophrenia (d) Obsessive-compulsive disorder

    Answer: (d) Obsessive-compulsive disorder. In exposure and response prevention the client faces the feared situation while giving up the ritual that normally follows, and it is the clearest specific effect in the literature: Lindsay, Crino and Andrews (1997) found OCD symptoms fell with ERP but not with anxiety management, and Foa and colleagues (2005) found 62 percent responders against 8 percent on placebo. It is not the treatment of choice for depression, schizophrenia or personality disorders.

  3. 'Healing and Persuasion' is the work of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Alfred Pritz (b) Jerome Frank (c) Charles Winnicot (d) Jeff Klerman

    Answer: (b) Jerome Frank. The book is Persuasion and Healing: A Comparative Study of Psychotherapy (1961), with the title reversed in the question; Frank argued that demoralised clients are remoralised by four shared features, a confiding relationship, a healing setting, a rationale or myth and a ritual. Alfred Pritz is an Austrian psychoanalyst linked to the Sigmund Freud Private University and the World Council for Psychotherapy, "Charles Winnicot" garbles Donald Woods Winnicott, and "Jeff Klerman" garbles Gerald Klerman, co-developer of interpersonal psychotherapy.

  4. In Expectancy Theory, motivation is highest when an individual believes: (1) effort leads to performance, (2) performance leads to outcomes, and (3) outcomes are: (RML 2025)

    (a) Uncertain (b) Valuable (c) Difficult (d) Negative

    Answer: (b) Valuable. In Victor Vroom's expectancy theory of work motivation (1964) the third belief is valence: the outcome must be valued, and uncertain, difficult or negative outcomes are not what motivates. Keep it apart from Irving Kirsch's response expectancy theory and from the client's expectancy of help, which are common factors in therapy (Section 8.4).

  5. A working relationship has all of the following characteristics EXCEPT: (RML 2020)

    (a) Observable ability of the worker and the client to work together (b) A committed belief in the helping relationship (c) A psychodynamic theory base (d) A contract to work together realistically and collaboratively

    Answer: (c) A psychodynamic theory base. The working relationship is the social-work and counselling name for Bordin's working alliance, which is pantheoretical: its origins are psychoanalytic, but it is defined by agreement on goals, agreement on tasks and a bond, so it belongs to no single theory. The other three options are its listed characteristics.

  6. The concept of the "working alliance" in therapeutic assessment refers to the: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Client's score on a measure of therapeutic outcome. (b) Collaborative relationship and bond between the clinician and client, focused on the tasks and goals of assessment/therapy. (c) Agreement between two different clinicians on the client's diagnosis. (d) Client's ability to work independently on therapeutic homework.

    Answer: (b) Collaborative relationship and bond between the clinician and client, focused on the tasks and goals of assessment/therapy. Edward Bordin (1979) defined the working alliance as agreement on goals, agreement on tasks and a personal bond, the three subscales of Horvath and Greenberg's Working Alliance Inventory (1989). An outcome score is what the alliance predicts (r about .28), agreement between two clinicians concerns the diagnosis rather than the relationship with the client, and independent homework is a separate topic (Section 8.10).

  7. Which of the following is NOT a key concept in Carl Rogers' Client-Centered Therapy? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Conditions of Worth (b) Unconditional Positive Regard (c) Actualizing Tendency (d) The Therapeutic Alliance (as defined by Bordin)

    Answer: (d) The Therapeutic Alliance (as defined by Bordin). Bordin's alliance of goals, tasks and bond grew out of the psychoanalytic line of Zetzel and Greenson and was generalised by a counselling psychologist in 1979; it is not Rogers's vocabulary. Conditions of worth, unconditional positive regard and the actualising tendency are central to Rogers's theory (Section 6.2).

  8. Which therapeutic approach would be LEAST likely to use the term "therapeutic alliance," preferring instead to focus on the "real relationship" and the therapist's unconditional positive regard? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Psychoanalysis (b) Cognitive-Behavioral Therapy (c) Person-Centered Therapy (d) Interpersonal Therapy

    Answer: (c) Person-Centered Therapy. Person-centred therapists prefer the language of the core conditions and the genuine, person-to-person real relationship. Psychoanalysis gave the alliance its names (Zetzel, 1956; Greenson, 1965), and alliance research spans CBT and interpersonal therapy, where the alliance predicts outcome about as strongly as anywhere else.

  9. Schofield's (1964) 'YAVIS Syndrome' describes: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Therapy technique (b) Psychiatric Illness (c) Therapist's Qualities (d) Patient's Characteristics

    Answer: (d) Patient's Characteristics. In Psychotherapy: The Purchase of Friendship (1964) William Schofield argued that therapists prefer clients who are young, attractive, verbal, intelligent and successful. Despite the word "syndrome" it is not an illness, it describes clients rather than therapists or techniques, and it is a bias in who receives therapy, not proof that such clients benefit more.

  10. Which of the following best defines "prognosis"? (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) History of the illness (b) Likely outcome or course of the disorder (c) Immediate symptoms (d) Diagnostic criteria

    Answer: (b) Likely outcome or course of the disorder. Prognosis is the forecast of whether, how far and how fast a person is likely to recover, relapse or become chronic. The history, the immediate symptoms and the diagnostic criteria are what assessment and diagnosis work from; a prognostic variable, such as severity, predicts outcome whatever treatment is given.

  11. All of the following are the characteristics of efficacy studies EXCEPT (RML 2020)

    (a) Therapist actions are controlled by a protocol or treatment manual (b) Therapist flexibility is encouraged (c) The treatment is applied to a homogenous client population (d) The research design relies on random selection and random assignment to a treatment or control condition

    Answer: (b) Therapist flexibility is encouraged. Efficacy studies test a treatment under ideal, controlled conditions, so a manual, a homogeneous single-diagnosis sample and random assignment protect internal validity; flexibility belongs to ordinary practice and to effectiveness research. Option (d) is loosely worded, because efficacy trials randomly assign volunteers but almost never achieve random selection, yet the intended exception is (b).

  12. Analogue research involves: (RML 2022)

    (a) Undertaking research to determine the efficacy of treatments and interventions (b) Undertaking research to compare the effectiveness of two different types of treatment (c) Undertaking research on clinical populations (d) Undertaking research on healthy, non-clinical populations

    Answer: (d) Undertaking research on healthy, non-clinical populations. Analogue research studies treatment under conditions that only resemble clinical practice, most often with non-clinical volunteers such as Lang and Lazovik's snake-fearful students (1963) or Paul's speech-anxious students (1966). Efficacy research uses real patients under controlled conditions, comparing two treatments is a comparative outcome study, and research on clinical populations is the opposite of analogue research.

  13. A recent study showed that viral medications might be useful in fighting certain bacterial infections. Participants with gastrointestinal issues were assigned to either a placebo or viral medication for the first four weeks of the study, followed by an additional four weeks on the other treatment. Their symptoms showed significantly more improvement from the viral medication than the placebo. What type of study is this, and what is the explanatory variable? (RML 2021)

    (a) An observational study where symptom improvement is the explanatory variable (b) An observational study where the type of treatment (viral or placebo) is the explanatory variable (c) An experiment where symptoms improvement is the explanatory variable (d) An experiment where the type of treatment (viral or placebo) is the explanatory variable

    Answer: (d) An experiment where the type of treatment (viral or placebo) is the explanatory variable. The researchers, not the participants, decided who received what, in a crossover design in which each participant had both conditions in turn, so the study is an experiment and the assigned treatment is the explanatory (independent) variable. Symptom improvement is the response (dependent) variable, which rules out (a) and (c), and an observational study assigns nothing.

  14. In a cohort study investigating the association between a new psychological intervention and relapse rates in schizophrenia, the risk ratio is calculated as 1.0. This finding indicates that: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) The incidence of relapse in the exposed group is double that of the unexposed group. (b) The intervention has a significant protective effect against relapse. (c) There is no difference in the risk of relapse between the intervention and control groups. (d) The odds of exposure are equal in both the relapse and non-relapse groups.

    Answer: (c) There is no difference in the risk of relapse between the intervention and control groups. A risk ratio divides the proportion of one group who relapse by the proportion of the other, so 1.0 means the risk is the same in both. Double the incidence would give 2.0 and a protective effect a ratio below 1.0 (0.5 halves the risk), while (d) describes the odds ratio of a case-control study, not the risk ratio of a cohort study.

  15. To investigate a rare psychological outcome (for example, a specific dissociative disorder) and its potential past exposures (for example, childhood trauma), which of the following epidemiological study designs would be the MOST feasible and appropriate? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Prospective Cohort Study (b) Cross-Sectional Study (c) Case-Control Study (d) Randomized Controlled Trial

    Answer: (c) Case-Control Study. Starting from people who already have the rare outcome and comparable controls, and looking back at their past exposures, avoids waiting for a rare disorder to develop; Doll and Bradford Hill used the design to link smoking with lung cancer (1950). A prospective cohort would have to follow thousands of people for years, a cross-sectional snapshot cannot show which came first, and nobody can be randomly assigned to childhood trauma.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. The working alliance as defined by Edward Bordin (1979) is the collaborative relationship and bond between clinician and client: agreement on goals, agreement on tasks and a bond. It is pantheoretical, so a working relationship needs a realistic collaborative contract, an observable ability to work together and a committed belief in helping, but not a psychodynamic theory base. asked 4 times
  2. Bordin's therapeutic alliance is not a key concept of Rogers's client-centred therapy (conditions of worth, unconditional positive regard and the actualising tendency are), and person-centred therapy is the approach least likely to use the term, preferring the real relationship and the core conditions. asked twice
  3. The dodo bird verdict holds that different bona fide psychotherapies produce roughly equivalent outcomes, explained by common factors such as the alliance, empathy and hope. Saul Rosenzweig first applied the Dodo's "Everybody has won, and all must have prizes" in 1936; Luborsky, Singer and Luborsky popularised the label in 1975; Wampold and colleagues (1997) put the upper bound of the differences at about 0.2. asked twice
  4. A risk ratio or odds ratio of 1.0 means no difference in risk (no association) between the groups; above 1.0 the event is more common in the treated or exposed group, and 0.5 means the risk is halved. asked twice
  5. Persuasion and Healing (1961) is by Jerome Frank (a past paper reversed it as "Healing and Persuasion"): demoralised clients are remoralised by four shared features, an emotionally charged confiding relationship, a healing setting, a rationale or myth and a ritual or procedure. asked once
  6. Schofield's YAVIS (1964, Psychotherapy: The Purchase of Friendship) describes patient characteristics, the young, attractive, verbal, intelligent and successful clients therapists prefer: a bias in who receives therapy, not a technique, an illness or a set of therapist qualities. asked once
  7. Efficacy studies ask whether a treatment can work under ideal conditions (internal validity): random assignment, a treatment manual, fixed sessions, a homogeneous single-diagnosis sample and blind raters. Therapist flexibility belongs to effectiveness research in ordinary practice, whose classic example is Seligman's Consumer Reports survey (1995). asked once
  8. Analogue research studies treatment under conditions that only resemble clinical practice, most often with healthy, non-clinical participants such as volunteer students with mild fears; it buys control at the price of external validity. asked once
  9. Exposure and response prevention is the treatment of choice for obsessive-compulsive disorder and the clearest specific effect: it beat anxiety management (Lindsay, Crino and Andrews, 1997) and placebo (Foa and colleagues, 2005: 62 against 8 percent responders). asked once
  10. Prognosis is the forecast of a disorder's likely course and outcome (recovery, relapse or chronicity), not its history, its immediate symptoms or its diagnostic criteria. asked once
  11. For a rare outcome and past exposures (a rare dissociative disorder and childhood trauma) the most feasible design is the case-control study, which starts from cases and controls and looks back; a cohort follows people forward, a cross-sectional study cannot show which came first, and nobody can be randomised to a past exposure. asked once
  12. When the researcher assigns the conditions, as in a crossover design where each participant receives both in random order, the study is an experiment, the assigned treatment is the explanatory (independent) variable and the outcome is the response (dependent) variable. asked once
  13. In Victor Vroom's expectancy theory of work motivation (1964), motivation is highest when effort is believed to lead to performance, performance to outcomes, and the outcomes are valued (valence); it is not Kirsch's response expectancy theory. asked once
  14. Hans Eysenck (1952) claimed that about two-thirds of neurotic patients improve within two years with or without psychotherapy (spontaneous remission). Smith and Glass (1977, 375 studies) answered with an average effect size of 0.68 (the typical client better off than 75 percent of untreated people), and Smith, Glass and Miller (1980) with 0.85 (80 percent), so "psychotherapy is generally ineffective" is false. asked once
  15. Self-efficacy (Albert Bandura, 1977) is a person's belief in his capability to organise and carry out the actions needed to reach a specific goal; it is not global self-worth, not belief in fate or chance, and not Adler's striving against inferiority. asked once
  16. Allocation concealment hides the upcoming allocation from the person enrolling participants until each one is enrolled, preventing selection bias; blinding hides the allocation from participants, therapists or assessors after assignment. asked once
  17. Poor awareness of a family's culture and customary practices leads to wrong assessments and misinterpretations, culturally normal behaviour read as pathology such as "enmeshment", not to accurate understanding, effective communication or a strong alliance. asked once
  18. Lambert's (1992) estimates of the sources of improvement: client and extratherapeutic factors about 40 percent, relationship (common) factors 30, expectancy or placebo 15 and techniques 15; rough judgements, not measured results, and not Frank's four features. not asked yet
  19. The Division 12 Task Force (appointed by David Barlow in 1993, chaired by Dianne Chambless; reports 1995 and 1998): a well-established treatment needs at least two good group experiments beating a placebo or another treatment (or equalling an established one), or a large series of single-case experiments, with a manual, a specified sample and two independent teams; beating a waiting list twice earns only probably efficacious. not asked yet
  20. The reliable change index (Jacobson and Truax, 1991) divides a client's change score by the standard error of difference; beyond 1.96 the change is reliable (p < .05), and with a cut-off it classes clients as recovered, improved, unchanged or deteriorated. not asked yet
  21. The APA (policy 2005, report 2006) defines evidence-based practice in psychology as the integration of the best available research with clinical expertise in the context of patient characteristics, culture and preferences, the three overlapping circles. not asked yet
